Markers are placed at normalized-negativity-bin midpoints, and the shaded regions are pointwise 95% paired-bootstrap intervals. Gaps in G32 occur where N2 = 0.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, scalable method to certify entanglement in high-dimensional quantum states by extending local randomized measurements to third order. This third-order information allows for the detection of entanglement that is invisible using standard second-order tests. The framework provides dimension-independent error bounds, offering a practical path for experimental quantum characterization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, resource-efficient protocol for quantifying entanglement in complex quantum systems, which is highly relevant for experimental quantum information processing platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of developing a scalable method to certify entanglement in high-dimensional quantum systems, circumventing the exponential cost of full state tomography.

**Main result.** They derive a finite-size entanglement certificate that extends local randomized measurements to third order, revealing entanglement missed by second-order tests.

**Method.** The method utilizes a third-order extension of local randomized measurements to test the positivity of a specific operator (the Projective Reduction Criterion), which is then analyzed using moment matrix reconstruction techniques.

**Model / system.** The focus is on certifying entanglement for a general bipartite quantum state $ho$ in high-dimensional Hilbert spaces ($\mathcal{C}_A \otimes \mathcal{C}_B$). The analysis is generalized to qubits-qudit ensembles.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the local unitary invariants ($x_1$ through $x_{10}$) and the minimum eigenvalue ($\lambda_{\min}$) of the constructed matrix $ar{M}(ho)$, which serves as the entanglement certificate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The performance is analyzed in terms of detection efficiency ($\eta_3$), relative gain ($G_{32}$), and the dimension $d$ of the subsystems.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes the availability of trusted local random unitaries drawn from the Haar measure, and the finite-sample error bounds rely on concentration inequalities like Markov's or McDiarmid's.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the 10 index-equality classes used for three-outcome measurements. Figure 3 compares the detection performance ($\eta_3, G_{32}, \Delta_{32}$) for the qubit-qudit ensemble.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the theoretical requirement (the reduction criterion) to the practical measurement scheme (local randomized measurements up to third order), followed by rigorous analysis of finite-sample error bounds using moment matrix reconstruction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Certifying entanglement in high-dimensional systems usually requires full state tomography, whose cost grows rapidly with the system dimension. Local randomized measurements offer a scalable alternative, but existing tests based on second-order correlations access only limited information about the state. Here, we derive a finite-size entanglement certificate that extends local randomized measurements to third order. The additional third-order information reveals entanglement that remains undetected at second order, while a dimension-independent concentration bound provides rigorous control of finite-sample errors. Our result opens a practical route to extracting stronger entanglement information from experimental platforms without the dimension-dependent overhead of state tomography.

</details>

### [First fault-tolerant quantum memory demonstration for a generalized superfast encoding](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08957v1)

**Authors:** James Brown, Kenny Heitritter  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.08957v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **3/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.08957_figures/2609.08957_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Logical error rate per round per logical qubit versus physical depolarizing error rate p, for the [[48, 8, 6]] and [[64, 8, 8]] even-weight GSE codes, decoded with Relay-BP using AlphaSyndrome- scheduled syndrome extraction. Solid curves are log-log power-law fits to each code’s data. The dashed line is the unencoded (physical) error rate for reference. The higher-distance code suppresses logical error more strongly at low p with the two curves approaching each other near p ≈4 × 10−3.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel, constant-weight Generalized Superfast Encoding (GSE) designed for fault-tolerant quantum memory of fermionic systems. By simulating performance under circuit noise, the authors report the first observation of threshold-like scaling for such a fermion-mapping, achieving a threshold around $4 	imes 10^{-3}$. This advances the practical feasibility of using quantum error correction for simulating fermionic quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, simulated demonstration of fault tolerance for a fermion-mapping, which is crucial for quantum simulation efforts involving fermionic Hamiltonians.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of demonstrating fault-tolerant quantum memory specifically for a fermion-to-qubit mapping.

**Main result.** They achieved a simulation showing a threshold-like scaling for fault tolerance at a physical error rate of approximately $4 	imes 10^{-3}$ for two specific code instances.

**Method.** The authors constructed an even-distance, constant stabilizer-weight Generalized Superfast Encoding (GSE) and simulated quantum memory operations under circuit-level depolarizing noise.

**Model / system.** The system involves encoding fermionic modes into qubits using the GSE, which utilizes stabilizer generators with weights of 4 or 6. The simulation tests two specific stabilizer codes: $[[48, 8, 6]]$ and $[[64, 8, 8]]$.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate ($ar{\epsilon}_L(p)$) as a function of the physical depolarizing error rate ($p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The observed threshold is $\approx 4 	imes 10^{-3}$. The tested codes have distances $d=6$ and $d=8$, and logical qubits $k=8$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on simulating circuit-level depolarizing noise and assumes the applicability of the developed GSE structure for fault tolerance.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the logical error rate ($ar{\epsilon}_L(p)$) for both tested codes versus $p$, illustrating the approach to a pseudo-threshold near $p \approx 4 	imes 10^{-3}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the construction of the constant-weight GSE stabilizers, analyzes the operator weights using 'a' and 'b' families of local Majorana operators, and simulates the memory performance using AlphaSyndrome extraction and Relay-BP decoding.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Generalized Superfast Encoding (GSE) is a fermion-to-qubit mapping that has error-correcting/detecting properties. To this point, all demonstrations have been relegated to error-detection only, as no fault-tolerance under circuit-level noise has been observed. Here, we introduce an even-distance $d$ constant stabilizer-weight GSE where each of $N$ modes is assigned a $d$-qubit block arranged on a ring. The resulting stabilizer generators have constant weight 4 or 6 for any even distance $d$. Furthermore, the full stabilizer set of this construction can always be partitioned into four qubit-wise commuting groups, which enables compact syndrome-extraction scheduling. We simulate quantum memory experiments under circuit-level depolarizing noise for two instances of this code, $[[48,8,6]]$ and $[[64,8,8]]$ and the threshold is observed to be $\approx 4\times10^{-3}$. This is, to our knowledge, the first fault-tolerant quantum-memory characterization of a fermion-mapping with threshold-like scaling.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a methodology to overcome severe spectral distortions in magneto-ARPES by employing detailed electron trajectory simulations. By accurately modeling how magnetic fields distort photoemitted electron paths, the authors successfully reconstruct the true electronic structure of $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Se}_3$. This establishes critical technical guidelines for performing high-resolution ARPES measurements in strong magnetic fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous development of post-processing techniques to correct for complex, field-dependent electron optics effects is highly valuable for any experiment probing quantum states in external fields, bridging experimental measurement limitations with theoretical models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is mitigating spectral distortions and broadening in magneto-ARPES measurements caused by significant electron trajectory aberrations when strong magnetic fields are applied.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated that the electronic structure can be accurately reconstructed in post-processing, maintaining high momentum resolution even in the presence of strong trajectory aberrations.

**Method.** The technique involves using detailed electron trajectory simulations (based on the equations of motion) to correct the measured photoemission angles and energies.

**Model / system.** The study uses $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Se}_3$, a topological insulator, measured using a high-resolution, laser-based ARPES system equipped with a tunable dipole magnetic field.

**Key observables.** Angle contraction ($	heta_f$), angle rotation ($\phi_f$), momentum distribution curves (MDCs), and the field-induced gap opening at the Dirac point.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical technical requirements identified are 2D momentum mapping, a micron beam spot size, and precise numerical field simulations. Scaling laws relating field dependence are also derived.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the electronic structure can be recovered via post-processing corrections, and residual field effects inside the analyzer lens column are neglected.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magneto-ARPES setup, electron trajectory simulations showing contraction and rotation, and comparative plots showing the successful reconstruction of the electronic structure by applying trajectory corrections.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the technical limitations of magneto-ARPES due to trajectory aberrations, details the simulation methodology using electron equations of motion, applies this correction procedure to $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Se}_3$ data, and finally discusses the physical implications for probing spin and orbital coupling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The control and perturbation of quantum phenomena with magnetic fields is an indispensable tool in materials research. Recently, in-situ field tuning was implemented into angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (magneto-ARPES), which provides direct momentum and energy-resolved information of the field-dependent electronic structure. However, aberrations of the electron trajectories were shown to be substantial, leading to significant spectral distortions and broadening even in small fields. Here we show that the electronic structure can be recovered from magneto-ARPES spectra with high accuracy while maintaining high momentum resolution even when strong trajectory aberrations are present. We studied Bi$_2$Se$_3$ in a dipole field of a coil using a laser-based ARPES system. The electronic structure was reconstructed in post-processing using detailed electron trajectory simulations. We identify two-dimensional (2D) momentum mapping, a micron beam spot size, and precise numerical field simulations as critical technical requirements for high-resolution magneto-ARPES. We show how circular dichroism can provide additional information about the coupling of the magnetic field to the spin. The experimental achievements and the scaling laws from our simulations provide a road map towards magneto-ARPES in larger fields.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a fundamental impossibility result for quantum computation, proving that no one-way one-round quantum algorithm can reliably 4-color directed cycles. The authors achieve this by developing a deep connection between quantum graph coloring and noncommutative extremal combinatorics, using matrix energy bounds to derive the necessary lower bound.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a strong, non-trivial lower bound for quantum computation models, showing limitations beyond classical or simpler quantum models, which is fundamental for understanding quantum algorithm complexity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proves a quantum lower bound demonstrating that a one-way one-round quantum LOCAL algorithm cannot 4-color directed cycles with high probability.

**Main result.** The impossibility of such a quantum coloring is established by connecting the problem to a lower bound on the $\Lambda$-energy of matrix-space decompositions.

**Method.** The proof employs noncommutative extremal combinatorics, mapping the quantum coloring problem to bounding the $\Lambda$-energy of a set of POVM elements.

**Model / system.** The model is the quantum LOCAL algorithm applied to graph coloring, specifically directed cycles. The analysis uses quantum information tools like POVMs and matrix energy bounds.

**Key observables.** Collision Probability ($	ext{Pr}[c_i = c_{i+1}]$) and $\Lambda$-energy ($E_\Lambda(W)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of colors ($q=4$), the locality (one-way, one-round), and the size of the local random-seed space ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the quantum coloring problem can be mapped to the energy bound, and the lower bound is shown to be stronger than those from bounded-dependence models.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the argument by first establishing classical lower bounds, then generalizing this to the quantum setting using matrix energy bounds, culminating in the proof that the energy lower bound implies the coloring impossibility.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that one-way one-round quantum LOCAL algorithms cannot $4$-color directed cycles with high probability, even with unbounded local computation and quantum message length. This is the first lower bound in the high-probability quantum LOCAL setting that goes beyond the non-signaling and bounded-dependence models, exploiting the structure of distributed quantum algorithms.   Our proof connects distributed quantum computing with noncommutative extremal combinatorics by identifying local collision probabilities with the weighted multiplicative energy of matrix-space decompositions. We obtain our lower bound by proving a dimension-independent weighted stability theorem for a directed noncommutative analogue of Mantel's theorem.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates the emergence of topologically protected, gapless Weyl-line phonons in frustrated Mott insulators modeled on the Maxwell lattice. The mechanism involves spin-lattice coupling distorting the lattice into a topological state. The key finding is the unique $T^2$ specific heat signature associated with these topological phonons, suggesting new avenues for phonon-mediated quantum phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding of topologically protected, non-Goldstone zero-frequency modes provides a novel platform for studying emergent quantum phenomena mediated by lattice vibrations, which can couple strongly to electronic degrees of freedom.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the existence and topological nature of zero-energy gapless Weyl-line phonons in frustrated Mott insulators modeled on the Maxwell lattice.

**Main result.** The system hosts topologically protected, bulk zero-frequency Weyl-line phonons, which yield a characteristic low-temperature specific heat of $C_{	ext{ph}} \sim T^2$, distinguishing them from conventional Goldstone modes.

**Method.** The analysis employs magnetopological mechanics and lattice dynamics, calculating the phonon spectrum using the dynamical matrix and characterizing topology via winding numbers.

**Model / system.** The system is a pyrochlore antiferromagnet modeled as a frustrated Mott insulator, whose lattice is distorted by spin-lattice coupling into a topological Maxwell lattice.

**Key observables.** Zero-frequency Weyl-line phonons, winding numbers ($n_W$), and the low-temperature specific heat ($C_{	ext{ph}} \sim T^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spin-lattice coupling strength, magnetic field strength, and the lattice structure (Maxwell lattice).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the quadratic approximation for lattice dynamics and assumes the spin interaction is primarily Heisenberg-like.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spin configuration inducing the topological lattice, winding number diagrams showing Weyl line projections, and real-space cuts demonstrating boundary zero-mode localization.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical model (spin-lattice coupling in pyrochlores), deriving the effective topological lattice, calculating the phonon spectrum using topological invariants (winding numbers), and finally predicting the unique low-temperature thermal signature.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that zero-energy gapless Weyl-line phonons of the Kane-Lubensky's type can arise in the three-dimensional Maxwell lattice frustrated Mott insulators through the magnetopological mechanics. In a pyrochlore antiferromagnet with the spin-lattice coupling, a magnetic field selects the spin state whose lattice distortion generates a $P4_3 32$ topological lattice. More crucially, the spin-lattice coupling and the spin configuration cause the bending of the neighbouring bonds, and converts the system into the topological Maxwell lattice. Remarkably, the resulting system is found to host the bulk zero-frequency Weyl-line phonons protected topologically, and these gapless phonons are not Goldstone modes. Unlike the conventional ${C_{\rm ph}\sim T^3}$ for the Goldstone phonons, these one-dimensional zero-mode manifolds yield a characteristic low-temperature phonon specific heat ${C_{\rm ph}\sim T^2}$. Our results could find applications in the Cr-based spinel systems, and moreover, we establish a low-energy platform where an extensive number of topological zero-frequency phonons can strongly couple to other degrees of freedom, opening a route to exotic phonon-mediated phenomena.

</details>

**Authors:** Jan Seyfried, Sayantan Sen, Marco Tomamichel  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.08733v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This work analyzes the sample complexity for testing quantum properties like state equality and independence using limited measurements. It compares the efficiency of adaptive versus non-adaptive protocols for both trace distance and fidelity metrics. The findings show that fidelity testing reveals structural differences, particularly for unknown states, suggesting that adaptivity can provide significant advantages over non-adaptive methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** The results provide rigorous, quantitative bounds on the resources needed to verify quantum properties, which is fundamental for quantum characterization protocols in experimental quantum information science.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the sample complexity required to certify, equivalently test, or test for independence of unknown quantum states using limited measurements.

**Main result.** For fidelity testing, adaptive algorithms provide better sample complexity bounds for equivalence and independence testing compared to non-adaptive methods, demonstrating a separation from state certification.

**Method.** The authors develop a framework using partial learning and reducing the testing problems to an $\ell_2$-distance testing subroutine, comparing results across trace distance and fidelity metrics.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum states $ho$ and $\sigma$ in $d$-dimensional Hilbert spaces, including bipartite ($ho_{AC}$) and tripartite ($ho_{ABC}$) systems, analyzed in the single-copy measurement setting.

**Key observables.** Quantum states ($ho, \sigma$), fidelity ($F$), trace distance ($\|\cdot - \cdot\|_{	ext{tr}}$), and quantum mutual information $I(A:C)ho$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Decision gap $\varepsilon$, state rank $r$, and system dimensions ($d, d_A, d_C$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed in the single-copy measurement setting, and results are compared between adaptive and non-adaptive measurement protocols.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention tables comparing sample complexities for identity and equivalence testing across different distance measures (Trace distance vs. Fidelity) for both classical and quantum settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares sample complexities for quantum state certification, equivalence testing, and independence testing under both trace distance and fidelity metrics, culminating in specific bounds for Markov chain testing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the problems of quantum state certification, equivalence testing and independence testing. In certification, given samples of an unknown quantum state $ρ$ and the description of a state $σ$, the goal is to test whether $ρ=σ$, or whether $ρ$ and $σ$ are far in a given distance measure. In equivalence testing, $σ$ is also unknown and only accessible via samples. Independence testing decides whether $ρ_{AC}=ρ_A\otimesρ_C$, or is far from being a product. The sample complexities of these problems are now well-understood for a decision gap $\varepsilon$ in trace distance: in the single-copy measurement setting with $d$-dimensional states, all three tasks can be solved using the same non-adaptive approach, which uses $Θ(d^{3/2}/\varepsilon^2)$ samples and is optimal in general, even without adaptivity.   In this work, we consider decision gaps expressed in fidelity and study possible separations between these problems and how adaptivity can help. We prove that certification with respect to fidelity for a state $σ$ of rank $r$ does not benefit from adaptivity and requires $\widetildeΘ(r^{3/2}/\varepsilon)$ samples. For equivalence testing and independence testing, we provide adaptive algorithms using $\widetilde{O}(\min\{d^{3/2}/\varepsilon^2,d^{9/4}/\varepsilon\})$ and $\widetilde{O}(\min\{(d_Ad_C)^{3/2}/\varepsilon^2,d_A^{9/4}d_C^{3/4}/\varepsilon\})$ samples, for $d_A\geq d_C$, respectively. Our main technique is a framework that uses partial learning and a reduction to testing in $\ell_2$-distance, adapted from the distribution testing literature. We show that adaptivity matters for equivalence testing in fidelity by proving that $\widetildeΩ(1/\varepsilon^2)$ samples are necessary in the non-adaptive case even for qubits, showing a separation from certification.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work adapts Sklar's theorem from classical statistics to quantum mechanics to determine if a quantum state can be uniquely reconstructed from its dependence structure measured across all possible local measurement contexts. The authors prove that for two qubits, this all-context information is sufficient to identify the state up to a simple ambiguity, and they show how global quantum discord emerges from this framework.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, information-theoretic tool to characterize quantum correlations by demanding consistency across all possible local measurements, linking fundamental concepts like quantum discord to classical dependence structures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how much quantum state information ($ho$) can be recovered by analyzing the dependence structure derived from *all* possible local quantum measurements, analogous to Sklar's theorem in classical statistics.

**Main result.** For two-qubit states, the full set of context-dependent dependence structures uniquely determines the state up to a discrete double-spin-flip ambiguity. Furthermore, global quantum discord can be reproduced by optimizing over these local contexts.

**Method.** It adapts classical copula theory to quantum measurements, defining an invariant quantity (like the odds ratio $Q$) that characterizes the dependence structure across different measurement bases.

**Model / system.** The focus is on two-qubit quantum states ($ho$), analyzed using local projective measurements defined by vectors on the Bloch sphere ($x, y \in S^2$). The theory is conceptually extended to multiqubit and continuous-variable systems.

**Key observables.** Dependence nucleus, invariant $Q$, Connected Correlation ($\Gamma_ho$), Quantum Discord ($D_{GQ}$), and the Copula structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Two-qubit states, local measurement bases ($x, y$), and the double-spin-flip ambiguity ($eho$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reconstruction theorem is rigorously established for two-qubit systems; generalizations to higher dimensions or continuous variables face open mathematical challenges.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the classical analogy of Sklar's theorem (copula decomposition) to the quantum setting. It first establishes the invariant measures for two qubits, derives the reconstruction theorem based on all-context compatibility, and finally discusses conceptual extensions to higher-dimensional and continuous systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sklar's theorem separates the marginals of a joint distribution from its dependence structure. We apply this viewpoint to Born statistics generated by local quantum measurements. For non-product two-qubit states, the dependence nuclei over all local binary projective measurement contexts determine the state up to at most a discrete double-spin-flip ambiguity. The corresponding scalar optimization, combined with the quantum total correlation, reproduces global quantum discord.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper extends nonequilibrium statistical thermodynamics by incorporating memory effects via boundary functionals into Zubarev's statistical operator method. It achieves this by mapping non-Markovian history dependence onto the geometry of an extended phase space using information potentials. This provides a rigorous tool for analyzing rare events and memory in complex stochastic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, non-calculus-based method to handle memory effects in open systems, which is crucial for modeling complex dynamics in nanoscale or biological environments where history matters.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a generalized framework for nonequilibrium stochastic thermodynamics that rigorously incorporates non-Markovian memory effects arising from boundary functionals (like first-passage times or absolute extrema) into the statistical description.

**Main result.** The authors show that incorporating history-dependent functionals allows the non-Markovian memory effects to be encoded into the geometry of an extended phase space via an information potential, thus generalizing Zubarev's formalism.

**Method.** The method generalizes Zubarev's nonequilibrium statistical operator approach by employing the maximum information entropy method, which is shown to be equivalent to the Donsker-Varadhan large deviation formalism and the Escher shift.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to open stochastic systems, modeled by Langevin equations, which are analyzed by extending the state space to incorporate history-dependent functionals (e.g., residence time, FPT, absolute extremum).

**Key observables.** Three trajectory functionals (residence time, FPT, absolute extremum), the generalized Zubarev partition function Q(t), and Onsager susceptibilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spectral gap of the modified Feynman-Katz operator ($	ext{gap}$), the internal dimensionless scale ($	ext{micro}_	au$), and the time scale $	au$ relative to these gaps.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard Markovian Fokker-Planck description is insufficient; the formalism assumes the equivalence between entropy maximization under mean constraints and the Escher shift.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the limitations of Markovian approaches, then generalizing Zubarev's method to include three types of functionals, establishing mathematical equivalences (entropy/large deviation), and finally applying the framework to solve optimization problems like stochastic particle separation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper develops a generalization of Zubarev's nonequilibrium statistical operator method for the case of simultaneous inclusion of additive and nonlocal boundary functionals of trajectories. Using a unified thermodynamic approach, a three-parameter model of nonequilibrium systems is constructed, including the first-passage time, the dwell time above a given level, and the absolute extremum of the process. A correspondence is demonstrated between the maximum information entropy method for trajectories and the Donsker-Varadan large deviation formalism. Using the Doob`s h-transform, it is demonstrated that fixing extremal functionals of history induces efficient non-Markovian transport in the system with dynamic adaptation to historical records. Criteria for the applicability of the developed apparatus in the "large time" domain and the possibilities of its use for optimizing stochastic particle separation in periodic potentials are discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive theoretical framework to calculate the noise (Fano factor) in RNA copy number resulting from stochastic gene expression. It models the system as doubly stochastic, linking the noise directly to the statistics of the fluctuating transcription rate. The work provides general formulas and verifies them across various complex rate dynamics models, including those with different boundary conditions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework for calculating noise in open quantum systems (like gene expression) by coupling a fluctuating rate process to a decay process is highly relevant to modeling quantum noise sources in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to understand the noise (fluctuations) in RNA copy number resulting from stochastic transcription and degradation processes in gene expression.

**Main result.** It derives the Fano factor, which quantifies noise, showing it is composed of an intrinsic Poisson contribution and a second term dependent on the variance and autocorrelation of the fluctuating transcription rate.

**Method.** The authors develop a doubly stochastic framework, deriving analytical results for the Fano factor using general stochastic calculus and applying it to various rate dynamics models.

**Model / system.** The system models gene expression where RNA production is driven by a time-varying, random transcription rate $\lambda(t)$ and subject to degradation. The analysis uses continuous-time Markov chains and diffusion approximations.

**Key observables.** Fano factor ($F[n] = V[n]/E[n]$), RNA copy number $n(t)$, Mean copy number $E[n]$, and the autocorrelation function $ho(h)$ of $\lambda(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Degradation rate ($\mu$), Variance of $\lambda$ ($V[\lambda]$), Mean of $\lambda$ ($E[\lambda]$), and the timescale ratio between rate fluctuation and degradation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The general derivation assumes independent, finite-mean mRNA lifetimes. Simplified results often rely on the assumption of stationarity or the exponential lifetime approximation.

**Figures summary.** The notes describe results derived for different boundary conditions (Reflecting, Periodic, First-Passage Resetting) for the rate dynamics, showing how the noise factor $F[n]$ is calculated for each case.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the noise factor $F[n]$ by first establishing general relations, then applying them to specific rate dynamics models (M/M/1, Drift-Diffusion with various boundaries), and finally presenting data-driven estimation techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Stochastic models of gene expression typically begin with a microscopic model of transcription and propagate its statistics to the RNA distribution. Here we develop a doubly stochastic framework in which RNA production is Poisson conditional on a time-varying transcription rate $λ(t)$. For RNA lifetimes drawn independently from an arbitrary finite-mean distribution, we derive the RNA Fano factor in terms of the lifetime survival function and the autocovariance of $λ(t)$. For a Poisson degradation process with rate $μ$, the survival kernel provides an exponential temporal filter, and the result depends only on the mean, variance, and normalized autocorrelation of $λ(t)$. These quantities may be calculated from an explicit rate model or, under ergodicity and adequate sampling, estimated from a sufficiently long rate trajectory. We validate the data-driven estimator using simulated transcription-rate trajectories, without supplying the known autocorrelation to the estimator. We also obtain exact analytical results for transcription-rate dynamics modeled by a discrete M/M/1 process and by drift-diffusion with reflecting, periodic, or first-passage-reset boundaries, and verify each result by direct simulation of the coupled transcription-rate and RNA copy-number processes.

</details>

### [Causal-Class Hierarchies in Coherence-Constrained Channel Transformation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08639v1)

**Authors:** Lin Zhu, Benchi Zhao, Xuanqiang Zhao, Ranyiliu Chen, Xin Wang, Shenggen Zheng  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.08639v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **2/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes how the causal structure of multi-step quantum operations affects the efficiency of channel transformation under coherence constraints. Using SDPs, the authors prove that for some channels, the causal order matters significantly, while for others, the structure collapses, indicating that parallelization can negate any potential causal advantage.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings provide deep structural insights into the limitations of quantum operations, showing that the advantage of complex causal structures can be entirely nullified by the underlying resource properties of the channels involved.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether increasing causal freedom (from fixed-order sequential to general higher-order processes) improves quantum channel transformation when the process is constrained by a resource theory.

**Main result.** A strict causal hierarchy is proven for amplitude-damping channels, but this hierarchy collapses for mixed-Pauli channels, suggesting free program-state parallelization is a structural obstruction to causal enhancement.

**Method.** The analysis employs a unified semidefinite-programming (SDP) framework to minimize the transformation error under various causal constraints (Parallel, Sequential, General) and coherence resource constraints (MISC, DISC).

**Model / system.** The study models quantum channel transformations involving two resource channels and a target channel, focusing on two-qubit amplitude-damping channels and mixed-Pauli channels.

**Key observables.** The optimal error in channel transformation, $D_{	ext{Class}}^F(\dots)$, and the coherence coefficients ($x_c, \mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The damping strength ($\epsilon$) of the amplitude-damping channels, and the specific coherence constraints (MISC or DISC).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The collapse condition for causal enhancement is shown to be a sufficient, not necessary, characterization of structural obstructions.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but mention the use of various equations (e.g., (201) through (239)) containing core mathematical derivations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the SDP framework, establishing the causal classes, deriving the necessary constraints for different orders, and finally applying these tools to prove the hierarchy for amplitude-damping channels and the collapse condition for mixed-Pauli channels.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Higher-order quantum transformations allow multiple channel uses to be combined through different causal architectures, from parallel and fixed-order sequential networks to general higher-order processes. Whether this causal freedom improves channel transformation when the higher-order operation is also constrained by a resource theory remains largely unexplored. We study this question in the dynamical resource theory of coherence using a unified semidefinite-programming framework. For two qubit amplitude-damping channels and the identity target, we prove a strict causal hierarchy at every nontrivial damping strength under both maximally incoherent superchannels (MISC) and dephasing-covariant incoherent superchannels (DISC). In contrast, mixed-Pauli channels admit a common teleportation simulation that transfers the channel dependence to Bell-diagonal program states prepared in parallel. The remaining processing can then be absorbed into a single quantum operational, so parallel, fixed-order sequential, and general higher-order strategies achieve the same optimal error for any target. These results identify free program-state parallelisation as a structural obstruction to causal enhancement.

</details>

### [Stochastic Processes as Non-Metric Geodesics in Information Geometry](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08870v1)

**Authors:** T. Koide, A. van de Venn  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.08870v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `quantum measurements` **2/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.08870_figures/2609.08870_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. The Gaussian manifold is shown in the (θ1, θ2) plane. The physically permissible states of this manifold lie strictly in the region below θ2 = 0. An e-geodesic corresponds to an arbitrary straight line in this θ-plane. For example, a straight line passing through the origin represents a specific process where the mean µ remains constant. A straight line parallel to the θ1-axis represents a process where the variance σ2 remains constant. A general straight line not passing through the origin describes a process where both µ and σ2 change.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.08870_figures/2609.08870_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. The Gaussian manifold is shown in the (η1, η2) plane. The physically permissible states of this manifold lie strictly inside the region bounded by the curve η2 = (η1)2. An m-geodesic is shown as a part of a straight line connecting two boundary points on the curve. In contrast, standard Brownian motion, which corresponds to the limit of an m-geodesic with an infinite slope, can only evolve in the direction of increasing η2. The bridge-process interpretation applies specifically to the subclass of maximal non-vertical straight geodesics extended to the deterministic boundary (σ2 = 0), pinned at fixed boundary times tA and tB.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper connects the geometry of probability distributions (the Gaussian statistical manifold) to the dynamics of continuous stochastic processes. By using $\alpha$-connections, the authors show that physical observables, like the restoring force in relaxation, dictate the underlying geometric structure, emphasizing the crucial role of non-metricity in describing these systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit link between non-metricity, optimal thermodynamic protocols, and the geometry of stochastic processes provides a powerful, general mathematical framework applicable to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a geometric correspondence between geodesics on the Gaussian statistical manifold and continuous stochastic processes. It aims to characterize these processes using advanced tools from information geometry, particularly focusing on non-metric connections.

**Main result.** Geodesics are classified into three types, and the framework shows that the physical restoring force governing relaxation dynamics directly determines the underlying connection parameter alpha. This highlights the active role of non-metricity in describing stochastic processes.

**Method.** The authors employ Information Geometry, utilizing $\alpha$-connections and dual coordinates (expectation parameters) on the Gaussian statistical manifold. They analyze geodesic equations and relate them to stochastic differential equations.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Gaussian statistical manifold, which serves as the space of probability distributions. The dynamics are modeled by continuous stochastic processes, including generalized bridge processes, Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) relaxation, and free Brownian diffusion.

**Key observables.** Physical restoring force, non-metricity, work-minimizing optimal protocol, and the connection parameter $\alpha$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Connection parameter $\alpha$, noise intensity, relaxation rate $\gamma$, and the slow-driving limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the Gaussian statistical manifold, and the interpretation relies on extending geometric concepts (like geodesics) to non-Riemannian structures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate e-geodesics (straight lines) in the natural parameter space and m-geodesics (straight lines) in the expectation parameter space for the Gaussian manifold.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the geometric structure using $\alpha$-connections, deriving the correspondence between geodesics and stochastic processes, analyzing the reduction to fundamental dynamics (OU/free diffusion), and finally applying the framework to stochastic thermodynamics to constrain the geometry via physical observables.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish a one-to-one correspondence between geodesics associated with the one-parameter family of $α$-connections on the Gaussian statistical manifold and a class of continuous stochastic processes characterized by a time-independent noise intensity. We demonstrate that geodesics in expectation parameters naturally classify into three distinct geometric categories, among which the Boundary-connecting class allows us to construct an explicit linear stochastic realization with constant diffusion, representing a generalized bridge process. This result demonstrates how continuous stochastic processes within this Gaussian class can be extended along geometric curves. Under appropriate operational limits, this generalized bridge process reduces to fundamental stochastic dynamics, either Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) relaxation or free Brownian diffusion. Crucially, the physical restoring force governing the resulting OU relaxation directly determines the underlying connection parameter $α$, providing a concrete physical observable to constrain the manifold geometry. Depending on the chosen affine connection representation, this restoring force can be attributed either to scalar curvature or purely to non-metricity, establishing a direct conceptual analogy with the Geometrical Trinity of Gravity. Furthermore, applying this framework to driven stochastic thermodynamics, we show that the work-minimizing optimal protocol in the slow-driving limit coincides precisely with an expectation geodesic of the statistical manifold with non-metricity equipped with $(g, {}^{(1/2)}Γ, {}^{(-1/2)}Γ)$, highlighting the active physical role of non-metricity in information geometry.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reveals that in non-Hermitian many-body systems, the topology of the Hilbert space connectivity graph is a primary determinant of physical behavior. Specifically, particle occupancy constraints act as crucial bottlenecks, inducing robust, directional localization and controlling the rate of spectral amplification. This establishes connectivity as a versatile control knob for non-Hermitian critical transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct link drawn between the abstract mathematical structure of the Hilbert space graph (connectivity) and observable physical phenomena like spectral amplification and localization makes it highly relevant for understanding non-Hermitian physics in condensed matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how Hilbert space connectivity fundamentally controls spectral amplification and state localization in non-Hermitian many-body systems.

**Main result.** The connectivity graph structure, particularly when constrained by particle occupancy, dictates the emergence of robust, size-dependent spectral amplification and specific localization patterns (unipolar/bipolar).

**Method.** The analysis involves constructing and analyzing the Hilbert space connectivity graph, calculating eigenenergy spectra, and comparing results under different boundary conditions and occupancy constraints.

**Model / system.** The study uses a minimal 1D bosonic model Hamiltonian involving correlated two-body hoppings, analyzed for $n=2, 3, 4$ particles, exhibiting non-Hermitian dynamics.

**Key observables.** Spectral amplification ($\max 	ext{Im}(E)$), Hilbert space connectivity graph structure, and localization patterns (unipolar, asymmetric bipolar) quantified by average polarization weight ($ar{P}_{	ext{pol}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Boson number ($n$), system size ($N$), and the occupancy constraint ($n_{	ext{max\_occ}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing systems with and without the occupancy constraint, and uses the average center-of-mass position as a diagnostic for localization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the connectivity graphs for different particle numbers and boundary conditions, and show localization patterns (e.g., unipolar/bipolar) induced by the occupancy constraint, contrasting them with unconstrained cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the role of connectivity in generating spectral transitions (2-boson case), then detailing how bosonic symmetry creates global loops, and finally demonstrating that occupancy constraints are necessary to induce robust, directional localization patterns.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Various exotic many-body phenomena such as quantum scars and fractons have been linked to Hilbert space fragmentation. In this work, we find that in non-Hermitian settings, Hilbert space connectivity has an even more universal and fundamental influence, tightly controlling the nature of spectral amplification and state localization. Far more complicated than real-space lattices, non-Hermitian many-body Hilbert space graphs not only possess intricate competing amplification channels, but also global feedback loops connecting remote Fock states related by particle symmetry. These features lead to amplification behavior with unconventional scaling and localization properties. Particle occupation constraints can furthermore remove selected Hilbert space pathways, leading to robust unipolar and asymmetric bipolar skin localization in otherwise reciprocal processes. These results extend beyond simple interacting bosonic models and establish Hilbert space connectivity as a versatile control knob for many-body non-Hermitian critical transitions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced Time-Dependent Quantum Monte Carlo method for fermions, extending it to a spinor formalism to accurately capture active spin-dependent exchange interactions. By deriving the effective interaction using Bayesian conditioning in physical space, the method overcomes limitations of previous approaches and provides a systematic framework connecting to established limits like Hartree-Fock theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous inclusion of spin dynamics via the spinor formalism and the systematic derivation of the conditional interaction make this a powerful, advanced tool for studying correlation effects in condensed matter systems beyond standard mean-field approximations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational challenge of solving the many-electron Schrödinger equation by developing a sophisticated Quantum Monte Carlo approach that correctly treats the active exchange interaction.

**Main result.** The authors generalize the Time-Dependent Quantum Monte Carlo method to a spinor formulation, allowing the treatment of active exchange effects and providing a systematic framework that reduces correctly to known limits like Hartree-Fock and two-particle spin equations.

**Method.** The core method is a Time-Dependent Quantum Monte Carlo (TDQMC) approach that samples the many-electron state in physical space using an ensemble of walkers. The interaction is replaced by a derived conditional interaction based on Bayes' theorem, avoiding the sign problem for fermions.

**Model / system.** The system is a general fermionic many-body system (electrons in atoms, molecules, or solids). The formalism is extended to spinors to properly account for spin degrees of freedom and the active exchange interaction.

**Key observables.** Energy estimators (particle and mixed walker-wave), correlation energy, and the decay rate of orbital orthogonality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nonlocal length ($\sigma$), which controls the spatial resolution of the conditional interaction; the limits $\sigma 	o \infty$ (mean-field) and $\sigma 	o 0$ (pair limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on approximating the true density using a conditional interaction derived via Bayes' theorem, and it assumes the guide wave propagation approximates the exact dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general TDQMC framework, first establishing the conditional interaction for the direct (charge) surface. It then addresses the complex exchange (coherence) surface, leading to the spinor generalization. Finally, it analyzes the theoretical reductions, showing how the spinor theory encompasses the scalar theory via collinear and mean-field limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The time-dependent quantum Monte Carlo method represents a many-electron state by an ensemble of replicas, in which each electron is described by a cloud of walkers which samples its density in physical space, one walker and one guide wave per replica, and it replaces the Hartree potential by a conditional interaction built from the walker positions. The sampling is in physical space rather than in configuration space, which is what keeps the cost polynomial. Here that conditional interaction is derived rather than postulated. The method has so far been applied mostly to opposite-spin electrons, where the exchange is dormant rather than absent, and the present formulation addresses the regime in which it is active. Treating the walker as a localization of its electron to a finite resolution, Bayes theorem and a single empirical substitution yield the Nadaraya-Watson form which the method has used heuristically. The nonlocality length thereby acquires a meaning as the width of the conditioning rather than as a fitted coupling, and the pair and mean-field limits follow from one construction. For fermions, exchange cannot be carried by the walkers and stays in the wave sector, which is what leaves the positive walker sampling free of the sign problem. Because the conditioning removes the gauge freedom which eliminates the orthonormality multipliers in Hartree-Fock, orthonormality is enforced here by a term derived from the constraint. The formulation is generalized to spinors, where the Pauli suppression at coincidence becomes graded by the local spin alignment, and it reduces through the collinear and mean-field limits to the known two-particle spin equations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a quantum algorithm utilizing multistep quantum resonant transitions (mQRT) to calculate the PageRank vector of a web graph. By sequentially evolving the system through Hamiltonians derived from nested subgraphs, it efficiently prepares a quantum state encoding the desired eigenvector. This offers a resource-efficient, digital alternative to existing quantum linear algebra solvers.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of resonant transitions and Hamiltonian evolution paths to solve a classically defined linear algebra problem (eigenvector calculation) is a direct application of quantum simulation techniques relevant to open quantum systems and quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a quantum algorithm to efficiently obtain the quantum state encoding the PageRank vector of a large-scale network, modeled by the Google matrix.

**Main result.** The proposed multistep quantum resonant transition (mQRT) algorithm achieves this state preparation with a runtime proportional to the number of graph partitioning steps, $O(\log N)$.

**Method.** The method involves constructing a Hamiltonian evolution path by sequentially passing through the ground states of intermediate Hamiltonians derived from nested subgraphs of the web graph.

**Model / system.** The system is the web graph, whose PageRank vector is the principal eigenvector of the Google matrix. The computation is framed using a sequence of Hamiltonians $H_k$ and the mQRT technique.

**Key observables.** The final quantum state $|\pi^{(m)}_0angle$, whose measurement probabilities yield the components of the PageRank vector $\pi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The runtime scales as $O(\log N)$ steps, and efficiency relies on the energy gap and ground state overlaps being polynomially large.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The algorithm assumes the ability to partition the web graph such that the overlap between ground states of adjacent subgraphs is polynomially large, and that the energy gap remains polynomially large.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the directed graph structure, the process of partitioning the graph into nested subgraphs (e.g., C -> B -> A), and the transition frequency spectrum related to the resonance steps.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem (PageRank via Google matrix), details the mQRT method based on graph partitioning, derives the necessary Hamiltonians and evolution path, analyzes the complexity using product formula methods, and validates the approach via simulations on small and large graphs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a quantum algorithm for obtaining a quantum state that encodes the PageRank vector of the Google matrix through multistep quantum resonant transition (mQRT). In the algorithm, the PageRank vector is encoded in the ground state of a problem Hamiltonian associated with the Google matrix. By dividing the web graph corresponding to the Google matrix into a series of subgraphs with nested structure, we construct a sequence of Hamiltonians based on the subgraphs to form a Hamiltonian evolution path from a simple initial Hamiltonian to the problem Hamiltonian. The ground state of the problem Hamiltonian is obtained by going through ground states of the intermediate Hamiltonians via QRT step by step. This algorithm requires only one ancillary qubit, and the runtime of the algorithm is proportional to the number of steps. It provides a new way for efficiently obtaining the quantum state of the PageRank vector of large-scale networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously defines the spectral sign implication for quantum logic using spectral projections of the difference between two quantum propositions. It proves that while this implication satisfies several desirable logical axioms, it fundamentally deviates from standard polynomial quantum implications. The work connects this abstract logic to concrete physical concepts like optimal projective tests for state discrimination.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO physics, the rigorous mathematical framework connecting spectral theory to logical implication provides advanced tools for analyzing quantum information structures that might underpin quantum measurement or state discrimination protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the mathematical definition and properties of the 'spectral sign implication' ($	ext{P} \Rightarrow_{	ext{sp}} 	ext{Q}$) within the framework of quantum logic.

**Main result.** The spectral sign implication satisfies key logical axioms (like Hardegree's conditions) but is fundamentally non-polynomial, differing from standard quantum implications because its value need not belong to the ortholattice generated by the input projections.

**Method.** The analysis relies heavily on spectral theory, defining the implication as the non-negative spectral projection of the difference operator ($	ext{Q}-	ext{P}$), and connecting it to variational principles.

**Model / system.** The model is abstract quantum logic, where propositions are represented by projections ($	ext{P}, 	ext{Q}$) on a Hilbert space, analyzed using operator theory within a von Neumann algebra.

**Key observables.** The implication projection $	ext{P} \Rightarrow_{	ext{sp}} 	ext{Q}$, and the associated threshold truth value $T_{sp}(X,Y)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the projections $	ext{P}$ and $	ext{Q}$, and the difference operator $	ext{Q}-	ext{P}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a spectral structure and, in some sections, restricts the system to finite dimensions for variational characterizations.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the definition of the spectral sign implication, proves it satisfies several logical axioms, demonstrates its non-polynomial nature compared to classical implications, and finally characterizes it variationally using optimal projective tests related to Helstrom discrimination.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the spectral sign implication for quantum logic, defined by the nonnegative spectral projection of the operator obtained by subtracting the antecedent projection from the consequent projection. The construction agrees with classical material implication on commuting projections and compares arbitrary pairs of projections through the spectral structure of their difference. It satisfies Hardegree's four minimal implicative conditions, his law of contraposition, and a falsity condition. It differs from the standard polynomial implications in that its value need not belong to the ortholattice generated by its arguments. Within a uniform class of Borel constructions for pairs of projections, the operations satisfying entailment, contraposition, and the falsity condition correspond exactly to measurable choices of spectral branch. In the continuous subclass, these three conditions determine the spectral sign implication uniquely. In finite dimensions, the operation is also the largest among the acceptance projections of optimal projective tests for Helstrom discrimination between subspace states with priors proportional to rank. These results connect quantum implication with the operator theory of two projections and binary quantum discrimination.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a quantum steganography scheme that embeds classical data by subtly perturbing the phase of quantum states using a novel Quantum Hilbert Transform (QHT). The QHT is crucial because it provides a unitary quantum analogue to the classical transform needed for this phase manipulation. The scheme's security and reliability are analyzed by quantifying the trade-off between embedding strength, channel noise, and the resulting error rate.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, unitary quantum mechanism for information hiding, which is highly relevant for secure quantum communication protocols and understanding quantum state manipulation in noisy channels.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of developing a quantum steganography scheme by hiding classical bits within quantum communication messages using a novel phase-domain technique.

**Main result.** The Quantum Hilbert Transform (QHT) provides a unitary quantum analogue of the classical Hilbert transform, enabling the embedding of hidden bits via controlled phase perturbations.

**Method.** The protocol involves embedding the message by applying a unitary phase perturbation derived from the QHT to a cover state, followed by recovery using binary state discrimination, block aggregation, and classical error correction.

**Model / system.** The system operates on quantum states in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, utilizing the QHT, which is constructed as a unitary phase operator based on the Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT).

**Key observables.** Bit Error Rate (BER) and Trace Distance (TD) are used to quantify the reliability and covertness of the embedded message.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Embedding strength ($\epsilon$), depolarizing probability ($p$), and the block size for error correction are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes the receiver (Bob) has access to the expected cover-state model, and the analysis relies on the QHT maintaining unitarity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 5 illustrates the trade-off between embedding strength, channel noise (depolarizing noise), and performance metrics, showing how BER increases while TD decreases with noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QHT construction, details the steganography protocol (embedding and recovery), and concludes with numerical verification analyzing the performance metrics like BER and TD.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The main goal of steganography is to transmit hidden messages in legitimate-looking communication messages. Phase-domain information hiding, however, has not been fully explored for quantum systems. This work introduces a finite-dimensional Quantum Hilbert Transform (QHT) as a unitary phase operator based on the Quantum Fourier Transform. Using this construction, we develop a QHT-based quantum steganography scheme that embeds classical bits as weak signed phase perturbations of quantum cover states. Bob recovers the hidden message through binary state discrimination, block aggregation, and classical error-correcting decoding.

</details>

**Summary.** This work extends the concept of Entanglement Wedge Reconstruction in AdS/CFT by employing the framework of Quantum Error Correction. By introducing the 'twirled Petz map,' the authors derive a systematic method to calculate subleading 1/N corrections, thereby providing a controlled theoretical path to understanding bulk physics in fully quantum gravitational regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between QEC theory (a tool from quantum information) and the fundamental structure of spacetime locality (holography) provides a powerful, mathematically controlled framework for understanding quantum gravity effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of extending the Entanglement Wedge Reconstruction (EWR) principle beyond the semiclassical, large N limit in AdS/CFT correspondence.

**Main result.** The authors provide a systematic and explicit method using the twirled Petz map to incorporate subleading 1/N corrections into bulk reconstruction formulas.

**Method.** The approach utilizes Quantum Error Correction (QEC) theory, specifically generalizing the Petz map to the 'twirled Petz map' to systematically calculate higher-order corrections in the 1/N expansion.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the AdS/CFT correspondence, focusing on reconstructing bulk local observables (the entanglement wedge) from boundary quantum field theory data.

**Key observables.** The explicit bi-local correction controlled by a subleading Petz kernel, and the reconstruction of bulk fields in terms of boundary modular operators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The large N expansion parameter (1/N), and the subleading quantum gravitational corrections.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial analysis is restricted to semiclassical AdS geometries without horizons, though the framework aims to provide a controlled path toward fully quantum gravitational settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of EWR beyond leading order, then grounds the solution in the formalism of Quantum Error Correction (QEC) using the Petz map, and finally applies this generalized map to derive explicit, controlled corrections to the holographic reconstruction formulas.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement wedge reconstruction provides the sharpest formulation of bulk locality in AdS/CFT, identifying the entanglement wedge as the largest bulk region reconstructible from a boundary subregion. While this statement is well understood at leading order in the large N expansion through both modular flow and Petz map reconstruction, its extension beyond the semiclassical regime has remained unclear. In this work, we show that quantum error correction provides the natural framework for addressing this problem. At leading order, we reproduce the known leading-order reconstruction formulas, and by employing the twirled Petz map, we obtain a systematic and explicit method for incorporating subleading 1/N corrections. Beyond the leading approximation, we have obtained an explicit bi-local correction controlled by a subleading Petz kernel. Our results establish a controlled approach to bulk reconstruction beyond large N and sharpening the role of quantum error correction in holography. This framework provides a controlled path toward understanding bulk reconstruction in fully quantum gravitational settings.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the convergence of Cluster Perturbation Theory and Coupled Cluster methods when applied to the $J_1$-$J_2$ Heisenberg Spin Lattice. By extending calculations to high orders, the authors confirm that both theoretical series converge, yielding robust predictions for the system's energy and magnetic phase boundaries. This validates the use of these advanced quantum many-body techniques for strongly correlated magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous testing of convergence for high-order many-body methods like CC and PT is crucial for developing reliable theoretical tools to predict phase transitions and ground states in complex magnetic materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central goal is to rigorously confirm the convergence of high-order Perturbation Theory (PT) and Coupled Cluster (CC) series when applied to strongly correlated quantum spin systems.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that both the PT and CC series converge at high orders (up to cPT7 and cCCSDTQ5), providing reliable estimates for the energy and critical points of the model.

**Method.** The study employs advanced many-body quantum chemistry techniques, specifically Cluster Perturbation Theory and Cluster Coupled Cluster methods, adapted for lattice Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the $J_1$-$J_2$ Heisenberg Spin Lattice, described by a Hamiltonian involving nearest-neighbor ($J_1$) and next-nearest-neighbor ($J_2$) exchange interactions between spins.

**Key observables.** Energy per site, magnetization ($\langle S_i^z angle$), and the critical point position as a function of the ratio $J_2/J_1$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of coupling constants $J_2/J_1$, and the calculation is performed in the Thermodynamic Limit (TDL).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The methods rely on the cluster mean-field (cMF) wavefunction as a zeroth-order approximation, and the calculations utilize approximations inherent to the CC formalism for the TDL.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy per site and magnetization across different ground state regimes (Néel, paramagnetic, collinear) for both PT and CC results, and show the convergence of the critical point estimate.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by extending calculations to higher orders (cPT7, cCCSDTQ5). It then presents the convergence analysis for energy and magnetization across various $J_2/J_1$ ratios, comparing the results from PT and CC methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In previous work, it was shown that using the cluster mean field(cMF) wavefunction as the zeroth order wavefunction for perturbation theory and coupled cluster produces a reasonable approximation for the J 1-J 2 Heisenberg model in the thermodynamic limit(TDL). However, this work was limited to cPT4 and cCCSD. The central claim of cMF is that it takes strongly correlated systems and makes them weakly correlated so that PT weakly correlated methods can be used. To confirm that this claim is true, it is important to show that the PT and CC series converge. In this paper, we extend the previous calculations to cPT7 and cCCSDTQ5 where we show that the energy is converging. We also discuss how to perform cluster based calculations efficiently in the thermodynamic limit, which is crucial for these more expensive calculations.

</details>

Renormalization inflates the weight of the rest, so the total probability is always unit. Four questions cut 256 →64 →16 →4 →1, and the last line standing is the truth. The truth is the bitwise negation ψ(q) = ¬q (j = 27, “0123” base 4), and the four questions are asked in the order 00, 10, 01, 11 (“0, 2, 1, 3” base 4). Each answer terminates the 3/4 of surviving maps whose truth-table digit at the asked question disagrees. Every survivor’s thickness, proportional to its weight, is rescaled by the renormalization factor of that update, preserving their ratios. After four questions a single map remains. Interestingly, the first cull’s survivors are evenly spaced because it’s the “smallest” question. After the three smallest q, even...</sub>

At ℓ= 2 the stack still grazes the line from above (0.013 bits); only from ℓ= 3 on do the stacks fall short of it, as genuine deduction overtakes the early loss.</sub>

**Summary.** This theoretical work develops a measure called 'leverage' to quantify how much knowledge is gained about an unknown function from each piece of information received. It models this using information entropy and derives a macroscopic profile describing this gain rate. The findings provide a rigorous mathematical tool for understanding the limits of inference based on prior knowledge.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the mathematical machinery developed for quantifying information gain and complexity bounds (e.g., using polynomial degrees or block laws) has parallels in characterizing the complexity of quantum states or the information capacity of quantum channels.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to quantify the rate of information gain, or 'leverage,' when sequentially querying an unknown function (modeled as a boolean map) using principles analogous to Shannon entropy.

**Main result.** The average leverage $L(t)$ in the thermodynamic limit is determined by a profile $\gamma(t)$, which can be explicitly calculated for specific prior structures, such as the simplicity prior.

**Method.** The analysis uses information theory concepts like surprisal and entropy reduction, generalizing the framework from simple binary questions to complex boolean maps defined over finite fields.

**Model / system.** The system is a boolean map $\psi: Q 	o A$, where $Q$ and $A$ are sets of questions and answers. The learning process is modeled by updating a prior belief distribution over all possible maps based on observed answers.

**Key observables.** Leverage ($L_\ell$), which is the ratio of total reduced uncertainty to total surprisal received; the entropy profile $\gamma(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fraction of questions asked, $t = \ell/Q$; the initial entropy $\eta_0$; and the structure of the prior belief over the maps.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on calculating the average over the truth prior and the question order, and in the macroscopic limit, it assumes the profile $\gamma(t)$ is governed by specific mathematical forms (e.g., Bernstein polynomials for certain priors).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup (Werner's learner), plot the entropy reduction and leverage as a function of prior parameters, and show the approach to the macroscopic limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the core concept of leverage using entropy reduction in a belief matrix, deriving the average leverage formula, and then generalizing this to the thermodynamic limit using profiles $\gamma(t)$ derived from specific prior constructions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A learner holds a prior belief over boolean maps that answer a finite set of $Q$ questions, and receives answers one by one. Each answer costs surprisal and destroys uncertainty, not only about the question asked but every question still unasked. We call the ratio the leverage: table entropy destroyed per bit of surprisal received. It is unit for a uniform prior, but with an intelligent prior can be higher (not lower).   Averaged over the truth prior and over the question order, the leverage is found exactly, and is generated by one sequence: the mean entropy $G_\ell$ of the answers to $\ell$ questions. Sending the number of input bits to infinity at fixed asked fraction $t = \ell/Q$, the increments of that sequence become a profile $γ(t)$, and initial question entropy $η_0$. The leverage closes to a thermodynamic limit. $L(t) = [η_0 - (1-t)γ(t)]/\int_0^t γ$.   Exchangeable priors, by de Finetti, all give a flat $γ(t)$ and hence a hyperbolic $L(t)$, their deduction confined to a boundary layer at $t = 0$. We construct a simplicity prior that escapes this, grading Boolean maps by the degree of their polynomial over $\mathbb{F}_2$ and budgeting weight across degree shells by a CDF $F$. Reed-Muller capacity then gives $γ(t) = 1 - F(t)$ exactly, so any nonincreasing profile, and any leverage curve it generates, is realizable at macroscopic times.

</details>
