**Summary.** This work derives the Meissner effect in magnetized 2SC quark matter, showing that the response is governed by the quantum geometry of the lowest Landau level. The resulting transverse Meissner mass scales with the pairing gap, a topological feature, which suggests a potential signature for quasi-periodic oscillations observed in magnetars.

**Why it may be interesting.** The topological origin of the Meissner mass scaling provides a novel, field-theory approach to understanding superconducting responses in extreme astrophysical environments, linking condensed matter theory to high-energy astrophysics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the Meissner response in magnetized two-flavor color-superconducting (2SC) quark matter, focusing on the role of quantum geometry in the superconducting response.

**Main result.** The transverse Meissner mass scales with the pairing gap ($\propto |\Delta|$) rather than the chemical potential ($\propto \mu$), a result attributed to the topological constraints imposed by the Lowest Landau Level (LLL) quantum metric.

**Method.** The Meissner mass is calculated from the static quadratic response of the thermodynamic potential using a spectral representation that explicitly incorporates Landau-level eigenstates and quantum geometric tensors.

**Model / system.** The system is magnetized 2SC quark matter, analyzed using the Nambu–Gor’kov (NG) spinor and propagator. The analysis is performed in the strong-field and Isolated Landau-Level (ILL) limits.

**Key observables.** Transverse Meissner mass ($M^2$), quantum metric ($g_N$), and Chern number ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pairing gap ($\Delta$), chemical potential ($\mu$), magnetic field strength ($	ilde{B}$), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the ILL limit ($|d_{NN'}| \gg |\Delta|$), and the strong-field regime ($\omega 	ilde{B} \gg \mu$) is crucial for the topological scaling.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the crossover of the renormalized Meissner mass as a function of the magnetic field strength relative to the chemical potential. Figure 2 schematically shows magnetic wave propagation across interfaces in a hybrid magnetar.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the Meissner response by decomposing the NG propagator, calculating the polarization tensor, and isolating the quantum-geometric contribution in the ILL limit, leading to the final scaling laws.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We find a quantum-geometric Meissner response in magnetized two-flavor color-superconducting (2SC) quark matter. Landau quantization quenches the transverse quasiparticle dispersion, suppressing the conventional Fermi-surface contribution and giving rise to a Meissner response governed by the quantum geometry of the Landau levels. In the strong-field regime, the response becomes dominated by the quantum metric of the lowest Landau level (LLL), and the leading scaling of the transverse Meissner mass is consequently set by the pairing gap, in contrast to the chemical potential scaling of conventional color superconductors. This unconventional scaling has a topological origin, as the LLL quantum metric is constrained by its Chern number. The reduced transverse Meissner mass provides potential implications for kHz quasi-periodic oscillations in magnetars.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a comprehensive resource theory for quantum-state texture, quantifying structural irregularities in quantum states. It develops new texture measures and solves the deterministic transformation problem for qubits under texture-preserving operations. The work's main contribution is proving a converse theorem that systematically characterizes all possible texture monotones.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, resource-theoretic framework to quantify structural imperfections (texture) in quantum states, which is crucial for characterizing the limitations of quantum gates and circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a comprehensive resource-theoretic formulation for quantum-state texture (QST) and systematically investigate state transformation problems under texture-free operations.

**Main result.** The authors establish that every texture monotone corresponds to a non-negative function satisfying specific mathematical properties, providing a converse to the convex-roof extension construction for QST.

**Method.** The work involves defining and comparing multiple texture measures (e.g., trace distance, fidelity, relative entropy) and applying resource theory concepts to characterize state transformations.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on quantum states $ho$ on a Hilbert space $H$ (including qubits), using the uniform superposition state $	au_f$ as a reference state for texture quantification.

**Key observables.** Quantum-state texture ($T(ho)$), transformation success probability $P(ho 	o \sigma)$, and the derived function $h(x)$ for pure states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the Hilbert space ($d$), and the parameters governing specific measures (e.g., $\mu$ for Tsallis entropy).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on defining 'texture-free operations' (CPTP maps preserving the reference state) and assuming the existence of 'resource monotones' satisfying specific properties (T1)-(T5).

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews existing texture measures, proposes new classes (convex-function-based), solves the deterministic transformation problem for qubits, and culminates in proving the converse relationship between texture monotones and derived functions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum-state texture quantifies structural irregularities of a state in a selected basis and has emerged as a valuable resource for gate characterization in universal circuits. Consequently, a class of contractive distance based texture measures and a framework for constructing convex-roof extended texture measure have been proposed. Here, we extend this theory in several directions. We examine the convertibility of a pure state to any state under texture-free operations, and fully solve the deterministic transformation problem between any two states in the qubit case. We then propose two classes of texture measures: convex-function-based measures and texture cost defined via minimal cost of texture contained in pure states. Moreover, we show that every texture monotone gives rise to a non-negative function which admits the same properties as the function used in the convex-roof extension---thus providing a converse to that construction. Our work thereby offers a relatively comprehensive resource-theoretic formulation of quantum-state texture.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a renormalization scheme for one-dimensional bosons with semirelativistic dispersion and contact interactions. By systematically handling logarithmic divergences using advanced resolvent techniques, the authors prove that the full interacting Hamiltonian converges to the established Lieb-Liniger model in the nonrelativistic limit. This provides a powerful, rigorous framework for studying strongly interacting quantum gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, field-theoretic approach to handling strong, short-range interactions in low-dimensional quantum systems, connecting a relativistic-like dispersion to a standard nonrelativistic model (Lieb-Liniger).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the renormalization of a quantum many-body system consisting of one-dimensional spinless bosons interacting via attractive contact potentials, which leads to ultraviolet divergences.

**Main result.** The theory successfully constructs a cutoff-independent, self-adjoint Hamiltonian whose nonrelativistic limit precisely reproduces the well-known attractive Lieb-Liniger Hamiltonian.

**Method.** The methodology involves advanced renormalization group techniques using an enlarged Fock space and the Schur-complement representation of the resolvent to systematically absorb divergences.

**Model / system.** The system models spinless bosons in one dimension with a semirelativistic dispersion relation ($\hat{p}^2 + m^2$) and attractive contact interactions ($\delta(x)$). The Hamiltonian is studied in the context of quantum field theory renormalization.

**Key observables.** The zero-total-momentum two-body bound-state energy ($E_B$), the renormalized resolvent $R(E)$, and the mean-field binding scale $|E_{MF}|$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bare coupling constant ($\lambda$), the particle number ($N$), and the momentum cutoff ($\Lambda$ or $c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The procedure assumes the existence of a unique self-adjoint Hamiltonian defined by the resolvent, and convergence is established by comparing the semirelativistic result to the known Lieb-Liniger model.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference mathematical equations (e.g., Eq. 1.1, Eq. 5.15, Eq. C.102) detailing the formal structure of the Hamiltonians, resolvents, and convergence proofs, rather than describing visual figures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the divergent Hamiltonian and the renormalization procedure (using the resolvent) to explicitly calculating the two-body sector, and finally demonstrating the convergence of the full $N$-body theory to the nonrelativistic Lieb-Liniger model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study one-dimensional spinless bosons with semirelativistic spinless-Salpeter dispersion and attractive pairwise contact interactions. Because the dispersion becomes linear at large momentum, the contact interaction is marginal by power counting and produces a logarithmic ultraviolet divergence. We construct the renormalized many-body theory using an enlarged Fock space and a Schur-complement representation of the resolvent, eliminating the bare coupling in favor of the physical zero-total-momentum two-body bound-state energy. The resulting cutoff-independent resolvent defines a self-adjoint Hamiltonian in each fixed particle-number sector. We treat the two-body problem explicitly and show, in the norm-resolvent sense, that the nonrelativistic limit reproduces the attractive Lieb--Liniger Hamiltonian. We also formulate a mean-field approximation directly within the renormalized theory. In the massless and deeply bound large-particle-number regimes, it predicts an exponentially increasing binding scale whose exponent is determined by a one-dimensional variational problem.

</details>

### [Snakes on a Hyper-Plane: Dynamical and Ontological Locality in Everettian Quantum Mechanics](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07860v1)

**Authors:** Travis Norsen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.07860v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **2/5**

Should this be considered compatible with dynamical locality?</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2609.07860_figures/2609.07860_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 4: Diagram for the several formulations of dy- namical locality. σ and Σ are, respectively, earlier and later space-like hyperplanes. 3 is the intersection of the past light cone of 1 with σ, while 4 is the intersection of the future light cone of 2 with Σ. 3 and 2 are non- overlapping, so 2 is at space-like separation from 1. Ac- cording to Bell’s formulation of dynamical locality: “Full specification of what happens in 3 makes events in 2 ir- relevant for predictions about 1 in a locally causal the- ory.” [9]</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2609.07860_figures/2609.07860_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>

</details>

For example: Is a snake- shaped non-local beable such as bΣ (that is partly inside of, but also partly outside of, the future light cone of re- gion 2) allowed to be among the effects of causes in 2? Is bσ (partly inside of, but also partly outside of, the past light cone of region 1) allowed to be among the causes of effects in 1?</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper critically examines whether Everettian Quantum Mechanics, which posits non-local ontological states, can remain compatible with relativistic causality. By analyzing how local interventions affect global, non-local properties, the authors argue that the theory likely violates the principle of dynamical locality. This forces a re-evaluation of how non-local beables must be treated in relativistic quantum theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** It challenges fundamental assumptions about how quantum theories incorporating non-local ontologies can respect relativistic causality, which is a major topic in quantum foundations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper questions the consensus that Spacetime State Realism (SSR) Everettian Quantum Mechanics (EQM) is compatible with relativistic causality, despite positing ontological non-locality.

**Main result.** The involvement of non-local beables introduces complications for dynamical locality, suggesting that SSR EQM might violate relativistic causality.

**Method.** The argument modifies thought experiments (like the snake analogy) and analyzes the behavior of local versus non-local beables (like reduced density matrices) under measurement interventions.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on Everettian Quantum Mechanics (EQM) under the Spacetime State Realism (SSR) interpretation, using conceptual models of localized and non-localized quantum states.

**Key observables.** Local beables (Reduced Density Matrices for localized regions) and non-local beables (joint/holistic states for non-localized regions).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The causal structure, specifically the relationship between causes and effects relative to the future light cone.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption being challenged is that dynamical locality can be maintained when the underlying ontology includes non-separable, non-local beables.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the original snake thought experiment (Figure 1) and a modified version (Figure 2) showing a localized cause affecting a global, irreducible holistic property partially outside the future light cone.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the conflict between ontological non-locality and dynamical locality, then formalizing the conflict using modified thought experiments, and finally applying this framework to the EPR setup to show the effect on the joint state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

An apparently emerging consensus holds that Everettian Quantum Mechanics (EQM), in the Spacetime State Realism (SSR) version of Wallace and Timpson, involves ontological non-locality (i.e., non-separability) but is fully compatible with the dynamical notion of locality usually taken to be required by relativity. The claim of dynamical locality is based on the fact that the local beables postulated by the theory (namely, Reduced Density Matrices, RDMs, associated with localized regions) are not affected by interventions outside their past light cones. However, the theory's non-separability means that it posits non-local beables (namely RDMs for non-localized regions) in addition to the local ones. We argue here that the involvement of non-local beables introduces complications for extant formulations of dynamical locality and at least suggests, contrary to the emerging consensus, that SSR EQM should perhaps not be considered compatible with relativistic causality after all.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a Fisher-information speed limit that quantifies the maximum rate at which a neural network can learn about underlying latent variables during stochastic training. By modeling SGD as a stochastic process, the authors decompose this rate into deterministic (drift) and fluctuating (noise) components. This provides a powerful, information-theoretic diagnostic tool for understanding the efficiency and limits of machine learning algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of information-theoretic speed limits to characterize the dynamics of complex, non-equilibrium learning processes provides a quantitative physical analogy to relaxation dynamics seen in many-body systems or open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To derive a Fisher-information flow speed limit that bounds the rate at which trainable parameters in neural networks acquire information about latent variables during stochastic learning.

**Main result.** The rate of information acquisition is bounded by the sum of drift and noise contributions, and this bound is shown to be tight at the peak of the flow in analytically tractable models.

**Method.** The authors formulate Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) as a Markovian stochastic process and utilize the Fisher information framework to derive an upper bound on the information flow.

**Model / system.** The model is stochastic learning dynamics, specifically analyzing SGD applied to supervised learning where data is generated from a latent variable $Z$. The analysis is specialized to basis-function linear regression, whose dynamics near convergence reduce to an Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process.

**Key observables.** Fisher-information flow ($J_{	heta, \chi}^{Z,t}$), Fisher information ($F_{	heta}^{Z,t}$), Drift information budget ($I_{	ext{drift}, \chi}^{Z,t}$), and Noise information budget ($I_{	ext{noise}, \chi}^{Z,t}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relaxation rates ($\lambda_i$), characteristic time scales ($	au_{\chi, i}$), and the ratio of initial to final covariance values.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mini-batch fluctuation is approximated as Gaussian, and the analysis relies on deriving an upper bound, which is not necessarily an exact decomposition of the actual flow.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of Fisher information and the instantaneous flow, comparing the measured flow against the theoretical speed limit derived from the drift and noise components, showing the bound is tight at the peak.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general Fisher-information flow speed limit for SGD, then specializes this framework to linear regression. It analyzes the dynamics using modal decomposition, showing that the peak time of the information flow matches the relaxation time of the drift budget for each mode.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neural networks acquire internal representations through learning. In this work, we formulate stochastic gradient descent (SGD) as a Markovian stochastic process and derive a Fisher-information flow speed limit that bounds the rate at which trainable parameters can acquire information about latent variables in the data-generating process. The resulting inequality decomposes the information flow into drift and noise contributions, thereby quantifying the roles of deterministic learning forces and SGD-induced fluctuations from an information-theoretic perspective. We verify the bound in analytically tractable basis-function linear regression, where the information budget predicted by the bound reproduces the ordering and characteristic time scales with which different latent variables are encoded in the learned parameters. These results establish Fisher-information speed limits as a quantitative framework for diagnosing when and how different aspects of the data-generating mechanism are acquired during stochastic learning.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the challenge of tracking complex quantum many-body dynamics by introducing the 'Scrooge ensemble.' By constraining this maximum-entropy ensemble only with the expectation values of slow, conserved modes, the authors demonstrate a principle called 'global restricted typicality.' This allows for the accurate reconstruction of nonlinear quantum properties, like entanglement entropy, even when standard hydrodynamic methods are insufficient.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, non-standard statistical tool (the Scrooge ensemble) to extract global, non-linear information about non-equilibrium quantum states, bridging the gap between local observables (hydrodynamics) and full quantum state characterization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Characterizing the time evolution of generic quantum many-body systems is computationally intractable due to the exponential scaling of state representation.

**Main result.** The authors introduce 'global restricted typicality,' showing that complex, nonlinear quantum properties can be accurately reconstructed by constraining the maximum-entropy Scrooge ensemble using only the system's slow, conserved modes.

**Method.** The core method involves constructing the Scrooge ensemble, which maximizes information entropy subject to fixing the first statistical moments (expectation values) of the slow modes, thereby bypassing standard coarse-graining assumptions.

**Model / system.** The work applies to generic quantum many-body systems, particularly those exhibiting a timescale separation where dynamics are governed by the transport of conserved charges, such as those studied in quantum hydrodynamics.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy (half-chain) and participation entropy in the computational basis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Timescale separation, slow modes (conserved charges), and the constraints derived from the system's slow dynamics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes a timescale separation and that the dynamics are governed by the transport of conserved charges, allowing the slow modes to constrain the global state.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 conceptually illustrates global restricted typicality, showing the time-evolved state lying on a dynamically restricted manifold, and outlines the operational pipeline from microstate to the Scrooge ensemble.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by defining the Scrooge ensemble, applying it to systems with slow dynamics, and demonstrating its ability to recover nonlinear quantum properties by constraining the ensemble solely with slow mode expectation values.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Characterizing the time evolution of generic quantum many-body systems is a fundamental challenge, as representing the exact state requires exponentially scaling computational resources. While hydrodynamics and statistical mechanics successfully simplify this task by predicting the expectation values of local observables, these macroscopic frameworks provide no information about nonlinear characteristics of the quantum state. In this work, we demonstrate that for systems exhibiting a timescale separation, with dynamics governed by the transport of conserved charges, this lost information can be systematically recovered. By constraining the maximum-entropy Scrooge ensemble solely by the system's slow modes, we accurately reconstruct complex, nonlinear quantum properties of the global time-evolved state, including the half-chain entanglement entropy and the participation entropy in the computational basis. Our results generalize the paradigm of canonical typicality, revealing that a hydrodynamically bottlenecked system behaves as a typical pure state within a dynamically restricted submanifold of the Hilbert space - a phenomenon we term global restricted typicality.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a theoretical link between Quantum Speed Limits and Quantum-State Texture, using measures like trace distance and Jensen-Shannon divergence. By analyzing specific open quantum systems, the authors derive quantitative lower bounds on the minimum evolution time. This framework offers new diagnostic tools for understanding fundamental constraints in quantum control and metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a generalized framework for deriving fundamental time constraints in open quantum systems, connecting resource theory concepts (QST) directly to fundamental physical limits (QSLs).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental lower bounds on the time scale of quantum evolution (Quantum Speed Limits, QSLs) by linking them to the quantitative measure of quantum-state texture (QST).

**Main result.** QSLs derived from various QST quantifiers, such as trace distance, state rugosity, and Jensen-Shannon divergence, successfully capture the fundamental constraints on quantum evolutionary speed.

**Method.** The authors derive lower bounds ($	au \ge 	au_{QSL}$) by analyzing the time evolution of the chosen QST measure using various mathematical tools, including analyzing the time derivative of the measure.

**Model / system.** The analysis considers general quantum systems undergoing evolution, specifically modeling dephasing and dissipative dynamics using master equations. Examples include a two-level atom coupled to a bosonic reservoir or analyzed via the amplitude damping channel.

**Key observables.** Quantum Speed Limits ($	au_{QSL}$), Trace Distance ($\Delta_{	ext{tr}}$), State Rugosity ($\Delta R$), Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD), and the normalized relative error ($	ilde{\delta}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dephasing rate ($\gamma_t$), cavity decay rate ($\gamma_t$), and initial state parameters ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis involves approximations for non-unitary dynamics (CPTP maps) and relies on defining the QSL based on the rate of change of the QST measure.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention numerical observations showing the behavior of $	au_{QSL}^{JS}$ and the relative error $	ilde{\delta}_{JS}(	au)$ for different parameter regimes, indicating when the bound is tight or loose.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces QSLs and QST, derives bounds using different quantifiers (trace distance, rugosity, JSD), and applies these derivations to specific physical models like dephasing and amplitude damping channels.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum speed limits impose intrinsic lower bounds on the shortest time scale for quantum system evolution. As an emerging paradigm in quantum resource theory, quantum-state texture has attracted research interest amid the rapid advancement of quantum theory. Herein, we investigate the interplay between quantum speed limits and quantum-state texture via several canonical quantifiers, including trace distance, state rugosity and Jensen-Shannon divergence. To demonstrate our findings, we analyze the minimum evolution time of physical systems subject to dephasing and dissipative dynamics. For the Jensen-Shannon divergence, we further explore nonunitary dynamics described by completely positive and trace-preserving maps, taking the amplitude damping channel as a typical example. In addition, we explore the tightness of these bounds in the considered dynamical models. Our results reveal that quantum speed limits derived from quantum-state texture capture the fundamental constraints on quantum evolutionary speed, with promising applications in quantum computing, quantum control and quantum metrology.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously investigates how topological invariants, such as the modular commutator and Hall conductance, maintain their robustness when the idealized local quantum Markov condition is only approximately met. By combining continuity proofs with finite-time dynamical analysis, the authors establish that these invariants remain asymptotically invariant in gapped phases. This work extends theoretical no-go theorems by providing quantitative bounds on the necessary entanglement structure required to support non-zero topological responses.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools to test the stability of topological invariants in realistic, non-ideal physical systems where exact symmetries are broken by finite-size effects or dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the robustness and invariance of the modular commutator and Hall conductance estimator as topological invariants when the exact local quantum Markov condition is violated.

**Main result.** The modular commutator and Hall conductance estimator remain asymptotically invariant under topology-preserving deformations if the local quantum Markov property holds approximately. Furthermore, a quantitative lower bound is established for the conditional mutual information required to sustain a non-zero invariant.

**Method.** The analysis combines proofs of asymptotic invariance using trace-norm continuity, the study of finite-time dynamics generated by modular Hamiltonians, and deriving quantitative bounds on entanglement measures.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on two-dimensional gapped topological phases, particularly chiral topological phases characterized by the chiral central charge ($c_-$) and systems exhibiting Hall conductance ($\sigma_{xy}$).

**Key observables.** Modular commutator ($J$), Hall conductance estimator ($\Sigma$), and Conditional Mutual Information ($I(A:C|B)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of violation of the local quantum Markov condition (A1), and the exponential decay rates of entanglement measures.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of an 'approximately local quantum Markov property' and relies on asymptotic limits and continuity arguments.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the geometry for the three-sector disk partition used for calculating $J$ and $\Sigma$, alongside the local geometry related to the A1 condition.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing asymptotic invariance under approximate Markov conditions, then proving trace-norm continuity, and finally deriving quantitative lower bounds on the required conditional mutual information by analyzing finite-time dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The modular commutator provides a bulk, local, single-wave-function probe of the chiral central charge $c_-$ for gapped ground states. Its invariance under deformations was previously established under a local quantum Markov condition---namely, the conditional mutual information $I(A:C|B)$ is zero for all tripartitions of a disk into three consecutive strips A, B, and C \cite{Modular-commutator-Gapped}. However, the local quantum Markov condition also forces the probe to vanish, leaving open whether modular commutator remains robust in physically relevant states where the Markov property holds only approximately. In this paper, we resolve this tension for finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces: the approximate local quantum Markov property implies the change of the modular commutator under topology-preserving deformations vanishes asymptotically. We next prove trace-norm continuity of the modular commutator. Combining deformation invariance with trace-norm continuity, we establish that the modular commutator remains asymptotically invariant within gapped quantum phases connected by quasi-local unitary paths that preserve the approximate local quantum Markov condition. Conversely, by analyzing finite-time dynamics generated by modular Hamiltonians, we derive a quantitative lower bound on the conditional mutual information required for a non-zero modular commutator: $I(A:C|B)$ across such strip tripartitions cannot decay faster than exponentially with the width of B given that the state satisfies entanglement area law, extending the exact no-go theorem of \cite{strict-J-2024} to a quantitative finite bound . Finally, we demonstrate that those conclusions apply equally to the Hall conductance estimator \cite{FanSahayVishwanath2023}.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a low-cost experiment using a thermal simulator to teach physics concepts related to energy balance and heat transfer using solar radiation as the energy source. The study successfully measured a significant temperature difference between the simulated and control environments. Crucially, it emphasizes the educational value of such models while cautioning against overinterpreting local experimental results as global climate evidence.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is classical thermodynamics and heat transfer, the rigorous discussion of energy balance, non-equilibrium processes, and the limitations of simplified models resonates with the boundary conditions and energy conservation principles central to many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the educational challenge of connecting physical concepts, empirical evidence, and socially relevant environmental issues like climate change.

**Main result.** The low-cost simulator successfully demonstrated a measurable thermal difference between a confined and an open system, proving its educational potential for teaching energy balance.

**Method.** The researchers conducted a low-cost experimental investigation by building a simplified thermal simulator and comparing its temperature evolution to an open control system exposed to solar radiation over 11 days.

**Model / system.** The system is a simplified thermal simulator designed to model energy balance concepts related to the greenhouse effect, comparing a confined system against an open control system exposed to solar radiation.

**Key observables.** Average temperature inside the simulator (45.91°C), average temperature in the external control system (38.58°C), and the mean temperature difference (7.33°C).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis involves concepts like electromagnetic radiation, energy balance, heat transfer, and temperature gradients ($\Delta T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed temperature rise should not be interpreted as a direct measure of the atmospheric greenhouse effect because the apparatus involves both radiative and non-radiative heat transfer mechanisms.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the location of the investigation and the temporal evolution of temperature differences ($\Delta T$), with tables summarizing daily statistical comparisons of temperatures.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem (climate change), details the low-cost experimental setup and measurements over 11 days, presents quantitative results showing measurable thermal differences, and concludes by emphasizing the pedagogical value while strictly limiting the interpretation of the findings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Climate change poses a major scientific, environmental, and educational challenge, requiring approaches that connect physical concepts, empirical evidence, and socially relevant environmental issues. This study presents and analyzes a low-cost experimental investigation developed within the "Physics and the Environment" course of a Physics teacher training program at the State University of the Tocantina Region of Maranhão (UEMASUL), Brazil. The experiment involved constructing a simplified thermal simulator using accessible materials and comparing a confined system with an open control system, both exposed simultaneously to solar radiation. The results showed that the average temperature inside the simulator was $45.91^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$, compared to $38.58^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ in the external control system, corresponding to a mean temperature difference of $7.33^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ (among other analyses). These results demonstrate that the experimental setup was capable of producing and measuring a significant thermal difference between the two systems. However, the observed temperature rise should not be interpreted as a direct measure of the atmospheric greenhouse effect, given that the experimental apparatus involves both radiative and non-radiative heat transfer mechanisms. The study emphasizes the educational potential of low-cost experimentation as a strategy to integrate electromagnetic radiation, energy balance, temperature, heat transfer, and climate science into the initial training of physics teachers. It also highlights the importance of explicitly discussing the limitations of experimental models and distinguishing local experimental evidence from conclusions regarding global climate processes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to quantify gravitational decoherence arising from the emission of soft gravitons. By utilizing the soft-graviton S-matrix and analyzing the overlap of coherent radiation states, the authors derive a gauge-invariant decoherence exponent. The results are significant for understanding quantum interference in the presence of a gravitational background, particularly in cosmological settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between soft-graviton scattering, infrared quantum information, and decoherence provides a novel theoretical framework for understanding how gravitational interactions can destroy quantum coherence in macroscopic systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the decoherence of matter superpositions caused by the emission of soft gravitons, a process governed by the leading soft sector of the graviton S-matrix.

**Main result.** The decoherence exponent is derived to be gauge-invariant and non-negative, showing that soft gravitons carry away which-path information, leading to an enhancement proportional to the gravitational-wave energy density and the fourth power of the observation time in certain regimes.

**Method.** The analysis employs Weinberg's soft theorem, calculating the decoherence functional via the overlap of coherent radiation states and extending the formalism to include squeezed graviton states.

**Model / system.** The system involves matter superpositions interacting with a gravitational field, modeled using the soft-graviton S-matrix formalism and analyzed in regimes ranging from the nonrelativistic limit to cosmological squeezed backgrounds.

**Key observables.** The decoherence functional ($\Gamma_{\alphaeta}$), the decoherence exponent ($\Delta B_{\alphaeta}$), and the reduced density matrix elements ($ho_{\alphaeta}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Soft graviton frequency cutoff ($\lambda$), UV cutoff ($\Lambda$), gravitational-wave energy density ($\Omega_{	ext{gw}}$), and squeezing parameters ($r_q, \phi_q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the leading soft-graviton approximation and assumes specific conditions for the infrared cutoff identification (e.g., flat relic spectrum).

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure moving from the general soft theorem derivation to the calculation of the decoherence functional, followed by specialized analyses for squeezed states and the nonrelativistic limit, concluding with physical implications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit gravitational decoherence of matter superpositions using the leading soft sector of the graviton $S$-matrix. Starting from Weinberg's soft theorem and the overlap of coherent radiation states, we derive, for branch configurations with the same total four-momentum, a gauge-invariant, nonnegative leading-soft decoherence exponent and evaluate the polarization sum and angular integrals exactly at this order. In the vacuum, coherence between branches with distinguishable soft radiation vanishes as the infrared cutoff is removed. The leading nonrelativistic term is purely quadrupolar as a consequence of energy--momentum conservation. We extend the analysis to single- and two-mode squeezed graviton states, including the two-mode case motivated by inflation. Squeezing can enhance or suppress decoherence depending on the relative phase, while phase averaging gives an enhancement governed by the graviton occupation. For a phase-averaged relic background with an approximately flat energy-density spectrum over the relevant soft frequency band, the sharp-cutoff identification of the infrared scale with the inverse observation time gives a contribution that grows with the gravitational-wave energy density and with the fourth power of the observation time. These results connect soft-graviton scattering, infrared quantum information, and decoherence in nonvacuum gravitational backgrounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses ARPES to probe the electronic structure of 1T-TaS2 across its first-order CDW phase transition. By monitoring a distinct in-gap electronic state during controlled heating and cooling cycles, the authors provide direct spectroscopic evidence of the transition's first-order nature. The study strongly links the electronic reconstruction, evidenced by the state's hysteretic collapse, to the material's macroscopic transport properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The clear, hysteretic collapse and recovery of a specific electronic state provides a direct spectroscopic fingerprint of a first-order phase transition, which is a hallmark of strongly correlated electron physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the spectroscopic evolution of the low-energy electronic structure across the first-order Charge-Density Wave (CDW) transition in 1T-TaS2.

**Main result.** The in-gap electronic state exhibits pronounced thermal hysteresis, collapsing upon heating into the NC-CDW phase and re-emerging upon cooling, directly mirroring the first-order resistivity transition.

**Method.** Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) was employed in temperature-dependent and photon-energy-dependent measurements across controlled heating and cooling cycles.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the layered transition-metal dichalcogenide 1T-TaS2, which undergoes a first-order CDW transition between the C-CDW and NC-CDW phases.

**Key observables.** The key observables are the spectral weight and intensity of the low-energy in-gap state and the flat band, tracked as a function of temperature and photon energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical parameters include the transition temperatures (e.g., 180 K to 220 K) and the temperature cycling range used for ARPES measurements.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the observed electronic features are directly linked to the structural and electronic reconstruction associated with the first-order CDW transition.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize the resistivity hysteresis, ARPES intensity maps showing the in-gap state's collapse/recovery upon thermal cycling, and detailed EDCs/MDCs confirming the hysteretic spectral features near the Fermi level.

**Paper structure.** The paper uses ARPES to investigate the electronic structure across the CDW transition. It first characterizes the low-temperature C-CDW phase, then uses temperature-dependent measurements to track the in-gap state's evolution through heating and cooling cycles, correlating these spectroscopic changes with known transport anomalies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The first order phase transition between the nearly commensurate (NC-CDW) and commensurate (C-CDW) charge density wave phases in 1T-TaS2 underpins its exotic electronic behavior, yet the spectroscopic evolution of the low energy electronic structure across this transition remains crucial to understand. Using angle resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES), we investigate the low temperature C-CDW phase, characterized by a flat band commonly associated with the lower Hubbard band and a distinct in-gap state located closer to the Fermi level. Photon energy dependent measurements distinguish these two low energy features through their different spectral weight evolution. Temperature dependent ARPES across heating and cooling cycles reveals that the in-gap state undergoes an abrupt collapse upon heating into the NC-CDW phase and re-emerges sharply upon cooling back into the C-CDW phase. This pronounced thermal hysteresis provides direct spectroscopic evidence of the first order nature of the transition. Furthermore, the disappearance and recovery of the in-gap state closely track the corresponding changes in resistivity, highlighting its intimate connection to the electronic reconstruction across the C-CDW/NC-CDW phase transition.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the scarcity of layered magnetic materials by employing polymorph engineering to stabilize $	ext{GdAlGe}$ in ultrathin films. Using a combination of DFT calculations and advanced characterization techniques, the authors confirm the material's layered structure, ferromagnetic properties, and suitability for spintronics. The findings provide a blueprint for designing novel 2D magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the study of phase stability, dimensionality effects, and electronic transport in crystalline solids touches upon concepts relevant to correlated electron systems and interface physics, which are key areas in condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge of developing a sufficient number of layered rare-earth magnets suitable for spintronic applications.

**Main result.** The authors successfully engineered and characterized an epitaxial film of the overlooked layered polymorph $	ext{GdAlGe}$, demonstrating its stability in ultrathin films and its ferromagnetic nature.

**Method.** The study combines computational modeling (DFT) to predict phase stability with extensive experimental characterization using MBE growth, RHEED, XRD, STEM, SQUID magnetometry, and transport measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the layered rare-earth magnet $	ext{GdAlGe}$, which is grown epitaxially on a $	ext{Ge}(111)$ substrate. The research focuses on controlling the crystal structure via polymorph engineering to stabilize the desired layered phase.

**Key observables.** Anisotropic ferromagnetic moment, Anomalous Hall Effect (AHE), negative magnetoresistance (MR), and structural confirmation via RHEED/STEM.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical thickness limit of 5 monolayers (MLs) where the desired polymorph remains stable, and the growth temperature of $190^\circ	ext{C}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that surface energy dictates the polymorph stability, favoring the layered structure in the 2D limit over bulk phases.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the structural confirmation (RHEED, STEM) of the 5 ML film, the magnetic response (temperature dependence of FM moment in various fields), and the electronic transport properties (MR and AHE hysteresis).

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure moving from theoretical prediction (polymorph stability via energy comparison) to experimental realization (MBE growth and structural confirmation) and finally to functional characterization (magnetism and transport).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The recent advances in research on layered magnets have established them as invaluable materials for spintronic applications and precursors to 2D magnets. The problem is that the number of such materials available for experimentation is still low. To tackle the problem, one may employ phase engineering; in particular, the interplay between the phase stability and dimensionality provides great opportunities for design of layered magnets. Here, this interrelation is harnessed to produce epitaxial films of an overlooked layered polymorph of GdAlGe. The compound is formed by alternating triangular Gd and honeycomb AlGe lattices. It is stable in ultrathin films, up to 5 monolayers. In thicker films, an additional unwanted phase emerges. The structural quality of the ultrathin films is witnessed by electron and X-ray diffraction as well as electron microscopy. Magnetization measurements detect an anisotropic ferromagnetic state. The GdAlGe magnetism is corroborated by a study of electron transport revealing the anomalous Hall effect and negative magnetoresistance. The material is naturally integrated with Ge, a technological semiconductor, which should facilitate applications. The present study can serve as a blueprint for design of layered magnets via polymorph engineering.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents Grinch, a novel, bottom-up generative framework that uses Generative Flow Networks to design quantum optical circuits. Instead of optimizing existing dense graphs, Grinch learns to sample optimal circuit topologies directly based on the fidelity reward. This allows for the efficient discovery of multiple, high-fidelity circuit realizations for complex entangled states and quantum gates.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the computational challenge of quantum circuit synthesis, providing a powerful, data-free generative AI tool for quantum optics that can explore alternative, hardware-constrained circuit topologies.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is the inverse design of quantum optical experiments, which requires searching over vast spaces of discrete circuit topologies and continuous parameters to generate a target quantum state.

**Main result.** The proposed framework successfully generates multiple high-fidelity optical graphs for complex entangled states and quantum gates, while also identifying asymptotic solutions for states that cannot be perfectly realized by simple graph structures.

**Method.** The authors introduce Grinch, a bottom-up, reward-driven generative framework based on Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) that samples circuit graphs directly from a reward proportional to the state fidelity.

**Model / system.** The physical system is quantum optical experiments modeled by weighted colored graphs $G(V, E)$. The state generated is a superposition of perfect matchings of this graph, and the process involves optimizing both discrete topology (edges) and continuous edge weights.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F$) between the generated and target state is the primary reward function; edge count ($|E|$) measures circuit complexity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The search space involves optimizing continuous edge weights (e.g., using L-BFGS-B) and discrete graph structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the target state can be represented by an optical graph structure, though it also handles cases where this is not strictly true. It contrasts with top-down methods that prune dense graphs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the bottom-up construction of specific entangled states (like $|GHZ(3, 2)angle$) using Grinch, show the convergence of the generative model over sampling steps, and compare performance across different edge budget constraints.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of quantum inverse design, details the limitations of existing top-down methods, proposes the bottom-up Grinch framework using GFlowNets, demonstrates its application to various entangled states and gates, and concludes with comparisons to other solvers.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Designing quantum optical experiments requires searching over discrete circuit topologies and continuous parameters, often with multiple realizations of the same target state. Graph-based methods commonly address this problem by optimizing a dense graph and pruning it toward a single circuit. We introduce \texttt{Grinch}, a bottom-up reward-driven generative framework that learns to sample optical graphs directly from fidelity-based rewards without relying on a pre-existing training dataset. We demonstrate the framework on multipartite entangled states, graph and cluster states, and nonlocal photonic-gate targets, obtaining multiple high-fidelity optical graphs. We identify asymptotic solutions for states that cannot be generated exactly by graphs, as well as nonlocal Toffoli gate constructions requiring two ancillas. Our work presents hardware-constraint objectives, which lead to alternative constructions of CNOT and Toffoli gates when connections between ancilla nodes are restricted. These findings illustrate a reward-driven approach to quantum optical inverse design, exploring alternative circuit topologies while directly incorporating connectivity constraints into the search process.

</details>

**Summary.** This work numerically and analytically investigates how surface roughness modifies the behavior of hard-particle fluids. The key finding is that the system's structural and dynamic properties can be universally described by mapping them onto an equivalent hard-disk system using an 'effective particle size.' This provides a powerful theoretical tool for simplifying complex, rough particle interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluids, the concept of mapping complex, rough interactions onto a simpler, effective model (hard disks) is analogous to coarse-graining or effective Hamiltonians used in quantum many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how particle surface roughness affects the static and dynamic properties of hard-particle fluids.

**Main result.** The structural and dynamic properties can be mapped onto equivalent hard-disk systems using an effective particle size, demonstrating a universal scaling behavior.

**Method.** The authors use numerical simulations and analytical calculations to derive an effective particle size and map the system's behavior onto standard liquid-state theories.

**Model / system.** The system models rough hard-particle fluids in 2D, where particles have surface asperities. Interactions are modeled using a Hertzian central force, and the system's behavior is analyzed by defining an effective particle radius ($R_{eff}$) and packing fraction ($\phi_{eff}$).

**Key observables.** Bond-orientational order parameter ($\psi_6$), diffusion constant ($D$), Lindemann ratio ($\gamma$), and the mean contact distance ($ar{d}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number density ($ho$), asperity radius ($R_a$), coverage ratio ($\kappa$), and the effective packing fraction ($\phi_{eff}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system behaves as a hard-core system, allowing for a mapping to hard-disk systems, and relies on small-roughness approximations for analytical derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate the collapse of structural ($\psi_6$) and dynamic ($D$) properties onto master curves when plotted against the effective packing fraction ($\phi_{eff}$), confirming the scaling law.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical model and defining the effective parameters, then using structural diagnostics ($\psi_6$) to show the master-curve collapse, and finally extending this mapping to dynamic properties like the diffusion constant ($D$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We numerically investigate how particle surface roughness affects the static and dynamic properties of a hard-particle fluid across a wide range of densities, rho. These simulations of a simple model of granular systems reveal that, although the amplitude and coverage of surface corrugation significantly influence the rho-dependence of system properties, the qualitative behavior of this dependence remains unchanged. These findings can be described quantitatively by introducing an effective particle size, which enables a direct mapping of the rough particle systems to equivalent hard-disk systems. Analytical calculations provide an explicit form of this mapping, and allow us to predict the static and dynamic properties of rough particles using standard liquid-state theories for hard disks.

</details>

**Summary.** This work extends the Sample-based Krylov Quantum Diagonalization (SKQD) technique from ground-state calculations to compute single-particle Green's functions for correlated systems. By sampling multiple particle-number sectors and using a Lanczos continued fraction, the method accurately reproduces spectral features across phase transitions. This advancement promises to enhance the feasibility of using quantum hardware for complex DMFT impurity solvers.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique directly addresses the computational bottleneck in simulating correlated materials by adapting a quantum diagonalization method to calculate spectral functions, which are crucial for understanding electronic excitations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of calculating single-particle Green's functions for strongly correlated impurity models, which is significantly harder than calculating ground states.

**Main result.** The proposed SKQD method successfully reconstructs the spectral function for the SIAM across the metal-insulator transition, suggesting it enables DMFT impurity solvers on near-term quantum hardware with larger bath sites.

**Method.** It generalizes the sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization (SKQD) method by sampling unitary Krylov subspaces in $N\pm 1$ particle-number sectors and reconstructing the Green's function via a Lanczos continued fraction.

**Model / system.** The study applies the method to the particle-hole-symmetric single-impurity Anderson model (SIAM) in chain geometry, which serves as the impurity solver for Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT).

**Key observables.** Single-particle Green's function and its spectral density $A(\omega)$, showing features like quasiparticle residue and Mott gap.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U$), system size ($L$), and the ratio of retained subspace size to full Hilbert space dimension ($d/D$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on classically evaluating sector-connecting overlaps and assumes the quantum device is only needed for short-time evolution sampling.

**Figures summary.** Figures systematically analyze the convergence and accuracy of the SKQD method by plotting the spectral function error versus the fraction of the ground sector retained, across varying interaction strengths ($U$) and system sizes ($L$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need to generalize SKQD from ground states to Green's functions by sampling $N\pm 1$ sectors. It details the Lanczos reconstruction and then presents systematic numerical results showing convergence across different physical regimes (e.g., metal-insulator transition).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We generalize the sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization (SKQD) method from ground-state calculations to the evaluation of single-particle Green's functions. By constructing and sampling unitary Krylov subspaces in the N +/- 1 particle-number sectors and evaluating all sector-connecting overlaps classically, the approach reconstructs the Green's function via a Lanczos continued fraction while retaining the shallow-circuit, ancilla-free character of SKQD. The quantum device is required only to prepare and sample short-time evolutions. Applied to the particle-hole-symmetric single-impurity Anderson model in chain geometry, with the discrete bath representation used in dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT), the method recovers the spectral function using a relatively small fraction of the full Hilbert space. Across a range of interaction strengths that spans the metal-insulator transition, the main spectral features are reproduced. These results suggest that SKQD-based Green's-function calculations may allow DMFT impurity solvers with a larger number of bath sites on near-term quantum hardware than is currently practical.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops an instantaneous-frame theory to accurately model parametric two-qubit gates in superconducting qubits, overcoming limitations found in traditional idle-basis approaches. By using the instantaneous eigenbasis, the authors provide a compact description of couplings governed by the Berry connection. This theory achieves quantitative agreement with numerical simulations, enabling the design of high-fidelity gates in the strongly driven regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, physically intuitive framework for analyzing driven quantum dynamics in superconducting qubits, moving beyond simplified approximations to handle fast, non-adiabatic gate operations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Conventional theories for parametric two-qubit gates fail in the large-amplitude regime required for fast gate operations.

**Main result.** The dynamics are more accurately described using an instantaneous eigenbasis, which provides a compact, quantitative theory matching exact simulations.

**Method.** The authors derive equations of motion using the instantaneous eigenbasis, governed by the non-Abelian Berry connection, to describe coherent rotations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on superconducting quantum processors using tunable couplers (DTC, CSDTC, STC) to implement parametric two-qubit gates like iSWAP gates.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity, exchange coupling ($\Omega$), effective ZZ coupling ($\phi_{ZZ}$), and population trajectories in both idle and instantaneous eigenbases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large-amplitude drive regimes, fast gate durations (< 50 ns), and the flux bias $\Theta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The instantaneous eigenbasis approach is used as a natural moving frame, and approximations are made in deriving the effective couplings.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare population trajectories in the idle vs. instantaneous basis, and summarize total error, leakage error, and ZZ error vs. drive amplitude for different coupler architectures.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the failure of idle-basis models, then introduces the instantaneous-frame theory using the Berry connection, and finally applies this theory to calculate and optimize gate fidelity for specific superconducting architectures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Parametric two-qubit gates using tunable couplers are a promising approach to scalable superconducting quantum processors. However, conventional theories formulated in the idle eigenbasis break down in the large-amplitude regime required for fast gate operations. Here, we show that parametric gates are more naturally and accurately described as coherent rotations between instantaneous eigenstates rather than idle eigenstates. This approach may appear counterintuitive, since instantaneous eigenbases are usually associated with adiabatic dynamics. However, they also form a natural moving frame for driven dynamics, where the non-Abelian Berry connection governs transitions and provides a compact description of the exchange and effective $ZZ$ couplings. We show that this theory achieves quantitative agreement with exact numerical simulations across representative tunable-coupler architectures, including double-transmon, capacitively shunted double-transmon, and single-transmon couplers. The description remains accurate even in regimes where idle-frame descriptions fail, while substantially reducing the reliance on computationally demanding exact simulations. These results establish an instantaneous-frame theory of parametric gates beyond conventional idle-frame models and provide a new perspective on driven quantum dynamics.

</details>

### [Near-optimal synthesis of non-Gaussian phase gates via qubit-oscillator Rabi control](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09132v1)

**Authors:** Zhen Yang, Shan Jin, Zi-Wen Liu, Xiaoting Wang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.09132v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper presents a near-optimal, analytically derived method to synthesize non-Gaussian phase gates necessary for universal continuous-variable quantum computation. By utilizing Rabi control in qubit-oscillator systems, the authors achieve a polylogarithmic scaling in the required interaction time relative to the target error. This establishes a powerful, scalable primitive for implementing complex quantum algorithms, such as solving PDEs.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, analytically derivable, and resource-efficient method to implement complex, non-Gaussian gates essential for advanced CV quantum algorithms, bridging theoretical gate requirements with physical control schemes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of engineering non-Gaussian phase gates, which are crucial but difficult to implement for universal continuous-variable (CV) quantum computation.

**Main result.** They developed a near-optimal synthesis scheme for polynomial phase gates using qubit-oscillator Rabi control, achieving a total interaction time scaling of $O(\log(1/\epsilon)^{c})$.

**Method.** The synthesis relies on approximating the target non-Gaussian phase gate using sequences of analytically constructed Rabi pulses, leveraging techniques related to Generalized Quantum Signal Processing (GQSP).

**Model / system.** The system is a hybrid qubit-oscillator platform where the qubit mediates effective nonlinear dynamics of the oscillator via Rabi coupling. The target gates are polynomial phase gates $e^{iT p(\hat{X})}$.

**Key observables.** Total Rabi interaction time ($T_{	ext{tot}}$), fidelity ($F_{sol}$), and the required success probability ($p_{	ext{succ}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Target error ($\epsilon$), polynomial degree ($R$), and the number of modes ($D$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approximation is performed on a finite Fock subspace, and the method is near-optimal, though a residual $\log(1/\epsilon)$ gap remains between upper and lower bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figures show numerical convergence of errors for representative CV evolutions and illustrate the successful reproduction of the antisymmetric spatial profile for a 2D regularized biharmonic equation.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general scaling for synthesizing polynomial phase gates, proves the near-optimality by matching the upper and lower bounds on $T_{	ext{tot}}$, and then applies this framework to implement complex core unitaries required for solving linear partial differential equations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Gaussian gates remain a key bottleneck for universal continuous-variable (CV) quantum computation because the nonlinearities they require are difficult to engineer. To address this challenge, we develop an efficient qubit-oscillator Rabi synthesis scheme for polynomial phase gates, with a total interaction time that scales polylogarithmically with the inverse target error \(\varepsilon\). Specifically, for a class of readily preparable initial states, we show that a degree-\(R\) phase gate can be approximated by an analytically constructed Rabi sequence with total time \(O(\log^{(R-1)/2+o(1)}(1/\varepsilon))\). This construction requires no numerical optimization and therefore extends naturally to arbitrarily large multimode systems. We further establish a total-time lower bound of \(Ω(\log^{(R-1)/2}(1/\varepsilon))\), showing that the synthesis is near optimal. As applications, we use this scheme to simulate representative CV quantum dynamics and implement a CV quantum algorithm for solving linear partial differential equations. These results establish qubit-oscillator Rabi control as an efficient, analytically compilable, and near-optimal primitive for CV quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the performance limitations of Subcarrier-Wave Quantum Key Distribution (SCW QKD) when subjected to noise from classical DWDM traffic via spontaneous Raman scattering. By developing a detailed mathematical model, the authors compare continuous wave and pulsed operation modes. They conclusively show that operating in a pulsed regime significantly enhances both the secure key rate and the maximum transmission distance compared to the CW regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems as it models decoherence and noise (SpRS) from a realistic, high-power classical environment (DWDM) and proposes an operational regime (pulsed) to mitigate its detrimental effects on quantum communication.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how spontaneous Raman scattering (SpRS) noise, generated by classical Dense Wavelength Division Multiplexing (DWDM) channels, degrades the performance of Subcarrier-Wave Quantum Key Distribution (SCW QKD) systems.

**Main result.** The pulsed operational regime significantly outperforms the continuous wave (CW) regime, achieving both a higher secure key generation rate and a greater maximum achievable distance in the presence of this noise.

**Method.** A mathematical model is developed to calculate the Quantum Bit Error Rate (QBER) and the secure key generation rate by incorporating the noise power from SpRS into the standard QKD analysis framework.

**Model / system.** The system is an SCW QKD protocol operating over a single-mode optical fiber co-propagating with classical DWDM channels. The analysis compares performance in both continuous wave and pulsed laser regimes.

**Key observables.** Quantum Bit Error Rate (QBER), secure key generation rate, and maximum achievable distance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Classical receiver sensitivity (ranging from -23 dBm to -48 dBm), DWDM parameters ($\xi = 0.18 	ext{ dB/km}$, $\Delta\lambda = 15 	ext{ GHz}$, $N_{ch} = 40$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes SpRS noise is evenly distributed in time for calculating the mean photon number, and it focuses on channels within the narrow spectral C-band.

**Figures summary.** Figure 5 compares the maximum possible operating distance versus classical channel receiver sensitivity for both CW and Pulsed modes, demonstrating the superior performance of the pulsed regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper first develops the mathematical framework to model SpRS noise impact, then analyzes the QBER and key rate for both CW and pulsed regimes, culminating in a comparison showing the pulsed regime's advantage across varying receiver sensitivities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper we study performance of the subcarrier-wave quantum key distribution system (SCW QKD) in the presence of spontaneous Raman scattering (SpRS) noise generated by classical channels of dense wavelength division multiplexing (DWDM) network within a single-mode optical fiber. We present the mathematical model for evaluation of the quantum bit error rate (QBER) and the secure key generation rate with the SpRS noise taken into account. We consider two regimes of the SCW QKD system: the continuous wave regime that uses continuous wave laser and the pulsed regime. For these regimes, performance of the system is analyzed depending on receiver sensitivity of classical DWDM. It is found that the pulsed regime outperforms the continuous wave regime in both the secure key generation rate and the maximum achievable distance.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a Schwinger boson perturbative theory to study the phase diagram and spin dynamics of the spin-$S$ Kitaev-Heisenberg model. By using the pure Kitaev model as a starting point and applying RPA, the authors map out quantum spin liquid boundaries for various spins. The results show that the QSL phase is highly sensitive to the spin magnitude, providing crucial insights into frustrated magnetism in correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of Schwinger boson theory to higher-spin systems ($S>1/2$) provides advanced tools for analyzing quantum magnetism, which is a core area of strongly correlated electron physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a theoretical framework to map out the phase diagram and understand the dynamical spin response of spin-$S$ Kitaev-Heisenberg magnets near quantum spin liquid (QSL) states.

**Main result.** The study determines phase boundaries separating QSL regimes from magnetically ordered phases for various spins ($S=1/2, 1, 3/2, 2$), showing that QSL regions shrink rapidly with increasing $S$.

**Method.** The authors employ a Schwinger boson perturbative framework, utilizing the spin-liquid saddle point of the pure Kitaev model as the unperturbed state and applying the Random-Phase Approximation (RPA).

**Model / system.** The system is the spin-$S$ Kitaev-Heisenberg model on the honeycomb lattice, described by a Hamiltonian involving Kitaev and Heisenberg couplings, parameterized by an angle $\phi$.

**Key observables.** Phase boundaries, the dressed dynamical spin structure factor, and the static RPA susceptibility.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spin quantum number $S$ ($1/2, 1, 3/2, 2$), and the coupling ratio $j=J/K$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework relies on exploiting the Klein duality to decompose the Hamiltonian into an unperturbed (duality-invariant) term and a perturbation for the RPA treatment.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the honeycomb lattice structure and various classical spin configurations (FM, AFM) defining ordering wave vectors. Figure 2 details the components used in the RPA response calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model and the Schwinger boson approach. It then uses Klein duality to set up the RPA calculation, determining phase boundaries for different $S$. Finally, it computes the spin dynamics, observing continuum features and spectral weight softening near magnetic instabilities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a Schwinger boson perturbative framework for the spin-$S$ Kitaev-Heisenberg model. The spin-liquid saddle point of the pure Kitaev model is used as the unperturbed state, and magnetic instabilities and dynamical spin correlations are evaluated around this saddle point. We decompose the Hamiltonian exactly into two parts by exploiting the Klein duality intrinsic to the Kitaev-Heisenberg model. We perform the random-phase approximation by taking the part invariant under the duality transformation as the unperturbed term and treating the remaining term, which changes sign under it, as the perturbation. We determine the phase boundaries separating the quantum spin-liquid regimes from the adjacent magnetically ordered phases for $S=1/2$, $1$, $3/2$, and $2$. The spin-liquid regions shrink rapidly with increasing $S$ and become very narrow at $S=2$. We also compute the spin dynamics of the spin-$S$ Kitaev-Heisenberg model, focusing on $S=1$, and find that the dressed dynamical spin structure factor retains a broad two-spinon continuum at finite energies, while the low-energy spectral weight softens at the ordering wave vectors of the adjacent magnetic phases. Our framework thus enables thermodynamic-limit calculations of spin dynamics near magnetic instabilities.

</details>

**Authors:** Dario Bressanini  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.08453v1>
