**Summary.** This paper reviews the history of optimizing wave functions using local energy variance in VMC. It argues that standard variance minimization fails for modern neural network wave functions due to heavy-tailed local energy distributions. The authors benchmark robust alternatives, such as MAD and $L_{-4}$, showing they outperform variance minimization in different physical regimes of the $	ext{H}_2^+$ molecule.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it directly addresses the numerical stability and theoretical foundation of modern Neural Quantum States (NQS) methods, providing concrete alternatives to standard energy minimization when dealing with complex, non-Gaussian local energy distributions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental challenge of selecting the optimal functional for optimizing trial wave functions in Variational Monte Carlo (VMC) and Neural-Network VMC (NN-VMC). This is necessary because standard variance minimization assumptions fail for modern wave functions.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that while energy minimization is standard, alternative robust cost functions like Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD) or specialized L-infinity functionals outperform variance minimization for specific regimes of the $	ext{H}_2$ potential energy curve.

**Method.** The work involves re-tracing the historical development of variance optimization principles and benchmarking several robust cost functions (MAD, Cauchy loss, $L_{-4}$) against the standard energy expectation value on a benchmark system.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the electronic structure calculation for molecular systems, benchmarked specifically on the $	ext{H}_2^+$ molecular ion across its full potential energy curve. The methodology is applicable to general quantum systems solved via VMC/NN-VMC.

**Key observables.** Variational energy ($E$), local energy variance ($\sigma^2$), Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD), and the values of alternative functionals like $L_{-4}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The internuclear distance ($R$) of the $	ext{H}_2^+$ system, which dictates the local energy distribution tails.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard variance minimization implicitly assumes a Gaussian local energy distribution, which is incorrect for Coulombic systems exhibiting $E^{-4}$ power-law tails.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but the benchmark compares the performance of different functionals (variance vs. MAD vs. $L_{-4}$) across the potential energy curve.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a historical review, tracing the development of variance optimization from the 1930s, followed by a modern analysis showing the failure of the variance estimator due to heavy tails, and concluding with a quantitative benchmark comparing robust functionals.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit the almost century-old question of which functional of the local energy best optimizes a trial wave function, a problem of central importance in Variational Monte Carlo (VMC) and, more recently, in Neural-Network VMC (NN-VMC). While variance optimization dates back to the 1930s, the high statistical noise and heavy-tailed local energy distributions inherent to modern neural-network wave functions have renewed interest in this approach. We retrace its long and largely forgotten history here, showing its direct relevance to modern Neural Quantum States (NQS) frameworks. Minimizing the variance (an $L^2$ norm) implicitly assumes a Gaussian local energy distribution: an unjustified assumption. For Coulombic systems, the local energy distribution exhibits $E^{-4}$ power-law tails, causing the Central Limit Theorem to fail for the variance estimator. This instability can be mitigated by robust cost functions: the Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD, an $L^1$ norm), the Cauchy loss, or the $L_{-4}$ functional, which features a tail analytically designed to match the $E^{-4}$ exponent. We benchmark these functionals on $H_2^+$, an exactly solvable system at every internuclear distance, using the Guillemin-Zener wave function across the full potential energy curve. While energy minimization by construction yields the lowest energy, variance minimization is surpassed at every $R$ by alternative functionals: MAD proves superior in the bonding region, while $L_{-4}$ performs best in the dissociation regime.

</details>

(b) Haldane model with hexagon defect, Eq. 15, capturing spin- orbit coupling to a magnetic moment. (c) Each VR defect produces a pair of in-gap impurity bound states |ψR ℓ⟩with differing vorticity ℓ= 1, 2. When the states hybridize at finite impurity density they generate an effective low energy theory tℓℓ′ RR′ with QWZ-like direction-dependent off-diagonal complex hopping e±iθ, producing TIB topological phase transitions.</sub>

In each panel horizontal axis is defect strength h (where h = 0 is the clean model H0) and vertical axis is an H0 parameter (A for QWZ, t2 for Haldane). The disorder averaged Bott index (color scale) shows clear topological phase transitions in both the trivial (a,f) and topological (b,g) phases of the clean model H0, with H0 gap ∆= 0.1 near its Dirac cone phase transition (u = −2 or m = 3 √</sub>

**Summary.** This work explores how localized defects can fundamentally alter the topology of electronic systems, leading to the formation of Topological Impurity Bands (TIBs). By analyzing impurity-induced bound states, the authors derive an effective low-energy theory applicable to lattice models like the Haldane model. This provides a local, defect-mediated route to realizing topological quantum phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of disorder-induced topology, particularly through localized defects, provides a concrete mechanism for realizing topological phases that might be inaccessible via uniform disorder averaging, which is relevant for understanding engineered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how localized defects, unlike long-wavelength disorder, can induce novel topological phase transitions in quantum materials.

**Main result.** A finite density of defects can generate Topological Impurity Bands (TIBs), leading to new topological phase transitions even when the clean system is trivial.

**Method.** The theory employs T-matrix calculations to find impurity bound states and derives an effective low-energy Hamiltonian by considering the hybridization of these states.

**Model / system.** The study applies to lattice models like the Qi-Wu-Zhang and Haldane models, analyzing the effect of a local mass defect impurity potential on the electronic structure.

**Key observables.** Topological Impurity Bands (TIBs), Bott index, Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR), and the vorticity ($\ell$) of impurity bound states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Defect density (e.g., 3%), critical impurity strength ($h_c$), and the nature of the disorder potential.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard TAI theory's reliance on perturbative averaging is insufficient for localized defects; the analysis assumes the impurity term breaks certain symmetries while preserving others.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates TIB formation via impurity bound states with differing vorticity; Figure 2 shows numerical evidence of TIB transitions matching analytical predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from establishing the limitations of standard disorder theory to deriving the TIB mechanism using bound states, and finally applying this theory to specific lattice models (QWZ, Haldane) to predict observable topological transitions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The effects of disorder on topological phases of matter are typically either preservation of topology or its destruction. For example, the quantum Hall effect's quantization and chiral edge mode remain robust until a strong-disorder percolation transition exits the topological phase. Disorder can also enlarge a preexisting topological phase, yielding a topological Anderson insulator (TAI). However, the reliance of TAI and other theoretical treatments on perturbatively averaging out long-wavelength randomness suggests that additional effects might appear when the disorder involves localized defects. Here we show that a finite density of randomly distributed defects can generate various new topological phase transitions. This route to disorder-induced topology can be viewed as the formation of topological impurity bands (TIB) and is applicable even if the initial clean model is a trivial band insulator. The theory relies on an orbital-dependent vorticity of impurity bound states, which we derive analytically near a generic Dirac cone phase transition as well as using the Chern insulator lattice models of Qi-Wu-Zhang and Haldane. Their numerically computed phase diagrams at 3% defect density show robust topological transitions in distinct TIB and TAI regimes, suggesting a local route to topological phases.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes how noise from classical DWDM channels degrades Subcarrier-Wave Quantum Key Distribution (SCW QKD) systems. By calculating QBER and key rates, the authors demonstrate that placing the quantum channel at 1310 nm (O-band) significantly outperforms the C-band, primarily due to reduced nonlinear noise, which is crucial for practical network deployment.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and quantum networks, as it provides quantitative guidelines for integrating quantum communication protocols into existing, high-capacity classical infrastructure.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the performance limitations of Subcarrier-Wave Quantum Key Distribution (SCW QKD) when integrated into a classical optical transport network utilizing Dense Wavelength Division Multiplexing (DWDM).

**Main result.** Quantum channel allocation at the 1310 nm wavelength (O-band) is superior to the C-band because it mitigates nonlinear noise effects, leading to better achievable distances despite higher fiber attenuation.

**Method.** The study involves theoretical calculations of the Quantum Bit Error Rate (QBER) and the secure key generation rate by modeling various noise sources.

**Model / system.** The system models SCW QKD operating over an optical fiber shared with multiple classical DWDM channels. Noise sources analyzed include Spontaneous Raman Scattering (SpRS), Four-Wave Mixing (FWM), and Linear Channel Crosstalk (LCXT).

**Key observables.** Quantum Bit Error Rate (QBER), secure key generation rate, and various noise power levels ($P_{ram}, P_{ijk}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fiber length (L), attenuation ($\xi$), number of classical channels ($N_{ch}$), and the quantum channel allocation wavelength (1310 nm vs. C-band).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of output power for noise calculations and models the impact of noise sources on the overall detector click probability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of noise sources (SpRS, FWM) and the secure key generation rate on fiber length and the number of classical channels for both O-band and C-band configurations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the impact of classical DWDM noise on SCW QKD, deriving expressions for noise powers, calculating QBER and key rates, and comparing performance metrics across different wavelength allocations (O-band vs. C-band).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper we study the performance of subcarrier-wave quantum key distribution (SCW QKD) in the presence of classical channels of optical transport network utilizing dense wavelength division multiplexing (DWDM). We consider the impact of spontaneous Raman scattering noise as well as the four-wave mixing and channel isolation efficiency. We calculate quantum bit error rate as well as the secure key generation rate of SCW-QKD protocol for different parameters of DWDM system and quantum channel allocations. Our calculations show, that quantum channel allocation at the wavelength of 1310 nm is preferable to allocation at C-band due to lower nonlinear noise, despite fiber's larger attenuation at this wavelength.

</details>

### [Accelerating Fourier--Motzkin elimination: redundancy removal and the choice of variable elimination order](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07960v1)

**Authors:** Shashaank Khanna  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.07960v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.07960_figures/2609.07960_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 3: Number of redundant inequalities produced and number of non-redundant inequalities retained at each elimination step, for four systems arising from causal structures, under the order found by Algorithm 2 and under a fixed order. Panels (a)–(d) correspond to the four instances.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced computational techniques to accelerate Fourier-Motzkin elimination, a method for projecting polyhedra described by linear inequalities. By proposing a smart variable elimination order rule, the authors achieve significant speedups over standard methods, making the analysis of complex entropic constraints from causal structures computationally feasible.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core topic is computational geometry/optimization, the application to entropic constraints derived from causal structures has implications for quantum information theory by providing tools to analyze marginalization over latent variables in complex physical models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational bottleneck of Fourier-Motzkin (FM) elimination, which is used to find the inequality description of the projection of a polyhedron onto a subset of its coordinates.

**Main result.** A novel variable elimination order rule was proposed that significantly reduces the running time (by factors of 6 to 25) compared to fixed-order elimination, especially for large systems arising from entropic constraints.

**Method.** The authors analyze redundancy removal techniques (LP testing vs. Imbert's test) and propose a look-ahead rule for selecting the variable elimination order to minimize the number of intermediate inequalities.

**Model / system.** The mathematical framework involves polyhedra defined by linear inequalities, with specific applications to obtaining entropic constraints derived from causal structures via marginalization over latent variables.

**Key observables.** The number of redundant vs. non-redundant inequalities retained at each step, and the wall-clock running time of the elimination process.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of variables and the number of inequalities in the initial system (e.g., >100 variables, >250 inequalities for causal structures).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the projection is full-dimensional for some results, and it notes that the optimal ordering problem is analogous to an NP-complete problem.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the projection of a polyhedron in 3D and the comparison of inequality counts and running times between fixed-order and look-ahead elimination strategies for causal structure instances.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the mathematical background of FM elimination and redundancy testing, then details the flaws in combining these tests, and finally proposes and analyzes the variable elimination order rule using random polytopes and large causal structure examples.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fourier-Motzkin elimination computes an inequality description of the projection of a polyhedron onto a subset of its coordinates by eliminating one variable at a time. It is used in several areas of optimisation and computer science, and it is a standard way of obtaining the entropic constraints of a causal structure, where the marginalisation over the latent variables produces such a projection. Its limitation is the growth of the intermediate systems of inequalities, which can be doubly exponential in the number of eliminated variables even though the projection itself grows only as a single exponential. In practice the computational overload of the method therefore depends on two choices: how the redundant inequalities are removed after each step, and the order in which the variables are eliminated. We consider both. We first show, by an explicit example, that Imbert's redundancy test cannot be interleaved with redundancy removal by linear programming. We show that the two methods, however, can be combined soundly if the derivation records used by Imbert's test are re-initialised after every step at which linear programming is used. We then propose a rule for choosing the elimination order of the variables that gives a significant computational advantage, however, at the cost of increased resource usage. We demonstrate this advantage on some random polytopes, where the rule reduces the running time by factors of between 6 and 25 compared with the same elimination under a fixed order. For entropic descriptions of causal structures, with more than 250 inequalities and more than 100 variables to eliminate, our rule keeps the number of inequalities handled at each step one to two orders of magnitude lower than a fixed order.

</details>

**Authors:** Artur F. Izmaylov  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.08170v1>

The ansatz circuits need not be controlled because the logical idle state |0n⟩is verified to be invariant under the executed tapered ansatz circuits up to phase. The ancilla gate Pa(−δ) compensates any difference between the two idle-state phases and is omitted when that difference is zero modulo 2π. The CSWAP network maps both branches onto register A, where ancilla X/Y and system-Pauli measurements recover the real and imaginary transition components.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces CASH-QSE, a novel quantum algorithm for electronic structure calculations that separates the energy calculation into a classically solvable active-space component and a quantum-corrected component. By enforcing orthogonality and solving the problem via a generalized eigenvalue problem, it achieves chemical accuracy with significantly reduced quantum circuit depth and sampling requirements compared to existing VQE methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a sophisticated, resource-aware quantum algorithm for quantum chemistry, directly addressing the resource scaling and measurement overhead that limits the application of VQE to strongly correlated systems, which is a major focus in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the bottleneck in Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) methods by developing a way to calculate quantum corrections to electronic energies without re-measuring the energy contribution already determined by a classical reference state.

**Main result.** The proposed CASH-QSE method achieves chemical accuracy while keeping the largest required quantum circuits manageable (a few hundred CNOT gates) and significantly reducing the required sampling cost compared to standard VQE approaches.

**Method.** CASH-QSE expands the wave function as a combination of a classically calculated Active-Space (CAS) reference state and orthogonal quantum states constructed in the remaining correlation subspace. The energy is then solved via a generalized, non-overlapping Hermitian eigenvalue problem.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to electronic structure calculations for small molecules, specifically $	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$ and $	ext{N}_2$, across bond-stretching coordinates. The formalism relies on partitioning the Hilbert space into a classically tractable CAS subspace and orthogonal quantum subspaces.

**Key observables.** Energy ($E$), CAS coefficients, circuit depth (measured in CNOT gates), and the ratio of required quantum sampling shots (compared to ADAPT-VQE).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chemical accuracy, bond-stretching distances (e.g., 0.96 Å to 3.0 Å for $	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$), and the size of the required quantum circuit (e.g., 70-CNOT network for $	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the remaining correlation can be exactly decomposed into a set of mutually orthogonal, structurally constrained quantum subspaces, allowing the global energy minimization to be treated as a linear Ritz problem.

**Figures summary.** Table VIII compares the required quantum sampling resources (shots) for $	ext{H}_2	ext{O}/	ext{cc-pVDZ}$ at various bond lengths, showing the significant reduction in shots achieved by CASH-QSE relative to ADAPT-VQE.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the core problem, details the mathematical formalism involving the first-defect decomposition to create an orthogonal basis, describes the iterative optimization procedure involving orbital relaxation and CAS coefficient updates, and concludes with benchmark results on molecular systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The wave function prepared by a variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) can contain a substantial classically tractable component, yet its energy contribution is sampled on the quantum device. Representing the wave function as a combination of a classical reference and separately prepared quantum states allows the reference energy to be evaluated without quantum sampling. However, near-linear dependence among these components can amplify measurement errors and destabilize the resulting energy estimate. We introduce Classical Active-Space Hybrid Quantum Subspace Expansion (CASH-QSE), which retains a complete-active-space self-consistent field (CASSCF) reference classically and uses occupation structure to construct quantum states exactly orthogonal to the reference and to one another. The energy follows from an ordinary Hermitian eigenvalue problem without overlap measurements, while the same occupation constraints simplify the measured operators. Using full configuration interaction to guide component selection, we test CASH-QSE along H$_2$O and N$_2$ bond-stretching coordinates spanning weakly to strongly correlated regimes. The benchmarks use STO--3G for both molecules and a restricted cc-pVDZ orbital space for H$_2$O. CASH-QSE reaches chemical accuracy while limiting the largest complete measurement circuits to a few hundred all-to-all logical controlled-NOT gates. In favorable cases, it also reduces the idealized final-energy sampling cost by several orders of magnitude relative to VQE with an adaptive derivative-assembled pseudo-Trotter ansatz (ADAPT-VQE).

</details>

**Summary.** This paper mathematically interprets non-causal quantum computation using category theory, linking it to postselected quantum teleportation. It proves that the logical consistency of these circuits is equivalent to specific trace conditions on the underlying process matrices. Ultimately, it shows this consistency manifests physically as a uniform probability distribution ($1/d^2$) in the postselected Bell measurement outcomes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides deep mathematical tools (category theory) to analyze the fundamental constraints of causality and information flow in quantum computation, which is highly relevant for understanding resource theories and computational limits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a rigorous categorical interpretation of non-causal quantum computation models, specifically connecting them to the physical process of postselected quantum teleportation.

**Main result.** It establishes that classical logical consistency in these non-causal circuits is mathematically equivalent to the postselected Bell outcome having a uniform probability of $1/d^2$ for all classical inputs.

**Method.** The authors employ a categorical framework using traced monoidal categories ($	ext{MatR}^{\ge 0}$) to define and analyze the process's consistency conditions.

**Model / system.** The model involves non-causal circuits, which are analyzed via a categorical trace operation ($	ext{Tr}_C(P)$) applied to a stochastic map $P$. This is physically mapped onto a process involving postselection on a maximally entangled Bell state.

**Key observables.** The stochastic nature of the traced process ($	ext{Tr}_C(P) \in 	ext{Stoch}$), the trace of the induced loop transition matrix ($	ext{tr}(H_a) = 1$), and the probability of the postselected Bell outcome ($1/d^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The loop dimension $d = |C|$, which dictates the required probability $1/d^2$ for classical consistency.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the process $P$ is stochastic for the trace to be well-defined in the context of the category $	ext{Stoch}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the categorical framework, proves the equivalence between the categorical trace being stochastic and the loop transition matrix having trace 1 (Theorem 1), and then extends this to show the equivalence between this condition and the specific probability distribution arising from postselected Bell measurements (Theorem 3).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper gives a categorical interpretation of Baumeler \& Wolf's logically consistent non-causal circuits, connecting them to postselected quantum teleportation. Looped feedback is represented by a trace in the category of non-negative matrices, and it is shown that the traced process is stochastic precisely when the induced loop transition matrix has trace $1$ for every external input, a condition shown to be equivalent to a unique fixed point for the loop for each input to a deterministic circuit. A classical non-causal circuit is represented by a measure-and-prepare quantum channel with an internal register utilising a maximally entangled Bell-state with postselection. The main result is that classical logical consistency is equivalent to the postselected Bell outcome having, for loop dimension $d$, probability exactly $1/d^2$ for each classical input distribution. The subsequent normalised conditional output then agrees exactly with the classical categorical trace. This work identifies a class of quantum Bell-postselection constructions whose conditional evolution maintains linear dependence on classical input distributions.

</details>

**Authors:** Kenny Chen, Mina Doosti, Ryan Sweke, Chirag Wadhwa  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.08864v1>

In this work we focus...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a general framework for distributed quantum inference, focusing on certifying unknown quantum states under strict communication limitations. It rigorously characterizes the required quantum resources, proving that shared randomness is crucial for achieving optimal complexity in state certification. The results establish fundamental limits for distributed quantum computation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous characterization of resource trade-offs (e.g., quantum vs. classical communication, public vs. private randomness) in distributed quantum tasks provides fundamental limits for quantum networking and distributed quantum computation protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses distributed quantum inference, specifically characterizing the complexity of quantum state certification—determining if an unknown state $ho$ equals a target state $\sigma$ or if they are far apart—under communication constraints.

**Main result.** It establishes the copy complexity for state certification, showing that shared randomness is necessary for optimal performance, and developing near-optimal private-coin algorithms matching the lower bounds.

**Method.** The work uses information-theoretic techniques to derive both upper bounds (algorithms) and lower bounds (proofs) on the required quantum resources (copy complexity) for the certification task.

**Model / system.** The model is the $(n_c, n_q, R, E)$-model for distributed quantum inference, where $m$ nodes each hold a copy of $ho$ and communicate with a central node $N_c$ via limited classical bits ($n_c$) and qubits ($n_q$).

**Key observables.** Copy complexity, trace distance ($\|ho - \sigma\|_1$), and the success probability of the certification test.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimension $d$, error threshold $\epsilon$, communication limits $n_c$ and $n_q$, and the randomness source $R$ (public vs. private).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the case where there is no communication allowed between the distributed nodes themselves, only one-way communication to the central node.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the $(n_c, n_q, R, E)$ model, detailing the information flow from distributed nodes to the central node under different randomness and entanglement assumptions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the general framework and the specific state certification problem. It then derives complexity bounds for the public-coin and private-coin settings, establishing the necessity of shared randomness, and concludes by presenting near-optimal algorithms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a framework for distributed quantum inference under communication constraints. In our model, $m$ distributed nodes each receive one copy of an unknown $d$-dimensional quantum state $ρ$, before communicating via a constrained one-way communication channel with a central node, which aims to infer some property of $ρ$. This framework generalizes the classical distributed inference framework introduced by Acharya, Canonne, and Tyagi [COLT 2019], by allowing quantum resources such as quantum communication and shared entanglement.   Within this setting, we focus on the fundamental problem of quantum state certification: Given a complete description of some state $σ$, decide whether $ρ=σ$ or $\|ρ-σ\|_1\geq ε$. Additionally, we focus on the case of limited communication between distributed nodes and the central node: we assume each communication channel is limited to only $n_c$ bits and $n_q$ qubits with $n_c + n_q \leq \log d$. When all nodes can make use of a shared source of randomness, we show that the copy complexity of distributed state certification is $Θ(\frac{d^2}{2^{n_q} 2^{n_c/2}ε^2})$. We further demonstrate that shared randomness is necessary to achieve the above complexity, by proving an $Ω(\frac{d^3}{4^{n_q} 2^{n_c} ε^2})$ lower bound in the $\textit{private-coin}$ setting. Moreover, we develop a private-coin algorithm that matches this bound up to a $\sqrt{\log d}$ factor, showing this complexity is near-optimal. Together, our work establishes a general framework for distributed quantum inference with communication constraints and characterizes the complexity of distributed state certification with limited communication.

</details>

(b) χnorm for several values of α, with the particle number set to N = L2 −αL. For α &lt; 1, the Fermi level crosses the edge states and the divergent diamagnetic response persists, whereas at α = 1 the Fermi level reaches the top of the valence band and the susceptibility tends to a finite positive value.</sub>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the orbital diamagnetism in Chern insulators using lattice models, finding that the susceptibility diverges linearly with system size when open boundary conditions are applied. This giant diamagnetism is robust against disorder and is attributed to the topological nature of the chiral edge states, distinguishing it sharply from the finite response predicted by bulk theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The robust, size-dependent topological response observed here provides a clear, measurable signature (divergent diamagnetism) that distinguishes topological edge physics from bulk properties, which is a key concept in characterizing topological phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether orbital diamagnetism can distinguish between topologically non-trivial (Chern insulator) and trivial massive Dirac insulators, focusing on the role of chiral edge states.

**Main result.** Both Chern insulators and massless Dirac systems exhibit giant diamagnetism that scales linearly with system size under open boundary conditions, a feature absent in the bulk calculation or trivial insulators.

**Method.** The authors use numerical calculations of the normalized orbital susceptibility derived from the total energy dependence on magnetic flux, comparing open and periodic boundary conditions.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a lattice massive Dirac Hamiltonian, specifically the Qi–Wu–Zhang (QWZ) model, applied to Chern insulators and related Dirac systems on a square lattice.

**Key observables.** Orbital diamagnetic susceptibility ($\chi_{	ext{orb}}$ or $\chi_{	ext{norm}}$), and its scaling with system size ($L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($L$), magnetic flux ($\phi$), mass term ($m$), and disorder strength ($w$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes zero temperature and fixed particle number, and relies on comparing finite-size calculations (OBC) against bulk formulas (PBC).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram of the QWZ model, the system-size dependence of $\chi_{	ext{norm}}$ for different mass regimes, and the robustness of the diamagnetism against disorder.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the context of divergent diamagnetism in massless Dirac systems, then applying the method to Chern insulators under OBC, contrasting this with PBC results, and finally analyzing the persistence of the divergence under symmetry breaking or changes in filling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional massless Dirac systems, exemplified by graphene, are known to exhibit a divergent orbital diamagnetic susceptibility that scales linearly with system size. Motivated by viewing a chiral edge state as one half of an enlarged analogue of a benzene ring, we study Chern insulators under open boundary conditions and find the same divergent scaling. This giant diamagnetism is robust against disorder, revealing its topological nature. Our results further suggest a profound connection to the divergent diamagnetism of massless Dirac systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper computationally studies how the range of molecular interactions influences the critical behavior of a fluid system using an interconverting lattice model. By varying the interaction range, the authors demonstrate that the system's critical points evolve, showing a systematic convergence toward mean-field theory predictions. This work provides a detailed framework for understanding the crossover between short-range and long-range critical phenomena in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the study of critical phenomena and phase transitions in lattice models provides foundational insights into how microscopic interactions dictate macroscopic behavior, which underpins many quantum many-body theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how varying the range of intermolecular interactions affects the nature of fluid multicriticality.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a clear convergence of simulation results toward mean-field predictions as the interaction range increases, revealing different critical archetypes depending on the interaction range.

**Method.** The research employs both mean-field theory analysis and extensive three-dimensional Monte Carlo simulations.

**Model / system.** The model is an interconverting lattice model (DBC model) designed to exhibit both Ising-like liquid-gas criticality and symmetric fluid tricriticality. The Hamiltonian includes terms for intermolecular interactions and interconversion energy.

**Key observables.** Phase coexistence, critical exponents, phase diagrams (showing LGCP, $\lambda$-EP, and TCP), and density/composition profiles.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The primary variable is the interaction range, controlled by the number of interacting neighbors ($Z_n$), which is systematically varied from 6 to 388.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often focuses on the degenerate case ($	ilde{e} = 	ilde{s} = 0$), and the mean-field theory is known to be exact only in the infinite range limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare phase diagrams and coexistence lines for different $Z_n$ values against mean-field predictions, showing convergence as $Z_n$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model and the role of interaction range, details the simulation methodology (MC moves, observables), presents results showing the dependence of critical behavior on $Z_n$, and concludes by comparing findings to theoretical limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The range of intermolecular interactions plays a central role in determining the nature of phase behavior and critical phenomena. It is well established through studies of the Ising model that as interaction range increases, Monte Carlo simulations progressively approach meanfield predictions as the effects of critical fluctuations are suppressed. In this work, we investigate how varying interaction range influences fluid multicriticality using an interconverting lattice model that exhibits both Ising-like liquid-gas criticality and symmetric fluid tricriticality (similar to that in the superfluid $^4$He-$^3$He mixture). This minimal model serves as a representative system for exploring the evolution of competing critical points within a generic framework. We analyze the model using both meanfield theory and three-dimensional Monte Carlo simulations while systematically varying the number of interacting neighbors, $Z_n$, from 6 to 388. We find that the system with nearest-neighbor interactions ($Z_n=6$) reveals only two types of multicritical behavior, while for larger interaction ranges, four distinct archetypes emerge. We demonstrate the convergence of the simulation results to those of the meanfield theory as the number of interacting neighbors tends to infinity, and we discuss the results within the framework of crossover critical phenomena.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work refines the understanding of spin dynamics in alkali-metal vapors by deriving separate slowing-down factors for longitudinal and transverse spin components in the SERF regime. The authors show that standard single-factor models overestimate relaxation rates at high polarization. These findings are vital for improving the accuracy of atomic magnetometers used in fundamental physics measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative refinement of the effective Bloch description for alkali-metal spin dynamics, which is crucial for accurately modeling high-precision atomic magnetometers and comagnetometers.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the discrepancy in describing alkali-metal spin dynamics in the SERF regime by analyzing the nuclear slowing-down factors for longitudinal versus transverse spin components.

**Main result.** Longitudinal and transverse spin dynamics are governed by distinct slowing-down factors at finite polarization, invalidating the single-factor description used in standard Bloch equations.

**Method.** The analysis employs perturbation theory on the linearized density-matrix dynamics, treating the spin-exchange interaction as the unperturbed system.

**Model / system.** The system involves alkali-metal vapors undergoing spin-exchange optical pumping (SEOP) in the SERF regime, modeled using the density-matrix formalism and effective Bloch equations.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal and transverse spin relaxation rates (1/T1, 1/T2), and the polarization-dependent slowing-down factors ($q_L(p)$ and $q_T(p)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron spin polarization ($p$), magnetic field strength ($B$), and the ratio of various relaxation rates (e.g., $\gamma_e B_z/R_{	ext{se}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an axially symmetric configuration and uses perturbation theory valid to specific orders in small rate ratios.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference tables providing explicit, closed-form polynomial expressions for the second-order coefficients ($	ext{S}_{\alphaeta}$) governing the field-dependent corrections to the transverse SERF eigenvalue.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general Bloch description to a detailed density-matrix analysis, deriving distinct factors for longitudinal and transverse dynamics, and culminates in calculating the second-order field corrections to the transverse SERF eigenvalue.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nuclear slowing-down factors account for the sharing of angular momentum between the electron and the nucleus in the effective Bloch description of alkali-metal spin dynamics in the spin-exchange-relaxation-free (SERF) regime. For collinear optical pumping and magnetic field, we find that longitudinal and transverse spin dynamics are characterized by different polarization-dependent slowing-down factors. Transverse dynamics are governed by the conventional factor $q(p)$, with $p$ the electron spin polarization, whereas longitudinal relaxation is governed by $q(p)+p\,dq(p)/dp$. The two factors coincide at zero polarization but differ substantially at high polarization, where the single-factor description overestimates the underlying pumping and relaxation rates inferred from longitudinal transients by factors approaching two to four, depending on the nuclear spin. We further derive closed-form expressions, valid at arbitrary polarization, for the residual spin-exchange relaxation of the transverse spin components at finite magnetic field along the pumping axis. This contribution is quadratic in the magnetic field and can become comparable to the zero-field linewidth at fields well within the SERF regime. The results are obtained by perturbation theory on the density-matrix dynamics linearized around the stationary state and are verified by numerical solutions of the microscopic density-matrix equation. These findings refine the effective Bloch description of alkali-metal spin dynamics and are directly relevant to atomic magnetometers and alkali-metal--noble-gas comagnetometers, including those used in precision searches for physics beyond the Standard Model.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a mean-field theory for the Kondo lattice to explain the emergence of spatially modulated Kondo hybridization waves (KHW). By modeling the order parameter with a wavevector $\mathbf{Q}$, the authors predict that the $\mathbf{Q}=(0,\pi)$ phase generates uniaxial stripe modulations and anisotropic conductivity. This provides a unified theoretical foundation for understanding these novel ordered states in heavy fermion materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, microscopic theoretical framework connecting abstract many-body physics (Kondo lattice) to specific, measurable experimental signatures (STM modulation, transport anisotropy) in complex correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the experimental observation of spatially modulated Kondo hybridization wave (KHW) order in heavy fermion superconductors, challenging the assumption of uniform hybridization.

**Main result.** A microscopic theory successfully predicts the stable $\mathbf{Q}=(0,\pi)$ KHW phase, which yields uniaxial stripe modulation matching STM data in $	ext{UTe}_2$ and $1	ext{T}/1	ext{H-TaS}_2$, and predicts anisotropic transport as a fingerprint.

**Method.** The authors developed a theory using the large-$N$ fermionic mean-field approximation applied to the canonical square-lattice Kondo lattice model.

**Model / system.** The system is the Kondo lattice model, described by a Hamiltonian involving conduction electrons and local moments. The theory treats the Kondo hybridization order parameter $V_i$ as spatially modulated: $V_i = V_0 + V_1 e^{i\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{R}_i}$.

**Key observables.** Kondo hybridization gap modulation (uniaxial stripe), in-plane conductivity anisotropy ($\sigma_{xx} 
eq \sigma_{yy}$), quasiparticle band structure, and Local Density of States (LDOS).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ordering wavevector $\mathbf{Q}$, the Kondo coupling strength $J$, and the conduction electron filling $n_c$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the $\mathbf{Q}$-dependent ansatz for the hybridization order parameter $V_i$ and the use of the large-$N$ mean-field approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spatial modulation of the order parameter, the resulting four-band quasiparticle structure for $\mathbf{Q}=(0,\pi)$, and the corresponding DOS/LDOS features.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mean-field theory for the modulated Kondo lattice, derives the self-consistent equations for the order parameters $V_0$ and $V_1$, maps the ground-state phase diagram, and finally calculates spectroscopic and transport signatures for the key $\mathbf{Q}=(0,\pi)$ phase.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent scanning tunneling microscopy experiments have discovered emergent spatially modulated Kondo hybridization wave (KHW) order in the heavy fermion superconductor UTe$_2$ and the artificial Kondo lattice system 1T/1H-TaS$_2$, challenging the conventional paradigm of spatially uniform Kondo hybridization in heavy fermion physics. Here, we develop a microscopic theory for KHW order based on the canonical square-lattice Kondo lattice model within the large-$N$ fermionic mean-field approximation. We systematically identify stable modulated KHW phases and establish their ground-state phase diagram. The prominent $\boldsymbol{Q}=(0,π)$ KHW phase yields uniaxial stripe modulation of the Kondo hybridization gap, which faithfully reproduces the spatial modulation pattern observed in UTe$_2$. Moreover, its inherent unit-cell-doubling modulation precisely accounts for the spectroscopic features measured in 1T/1H-TaS$_2$. We further predict a characteristic in-plane conductivity anisotropy that serves as a definitive transport fingerprint to discriminate KHW states with distinct ordering wavevectors. Our work provides a microscopic foundation for the newly observed KHW order and establishes a unified theoretical framework for understanding emergent modulated hybridization phenomena in heavy fermion materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the electronic and topological properties of the CSSH ladder using a tight-binding model. By calculating topological invariants and analyzing electronic transport, the authors demonstrate a fundamental link between the system's topology, the formation of flat bands, and its measurable transport response. This provides a comprehensive theoretical framework for understanding localization and topological transitions in such quantum lattices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong connection drawn between topological invariants (a bulk property) and measurable transport signatures (a real-space observable) provides a powerful, experimentally relevant diagnostic tool for characterizing topological phases in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the interplay between electronic, topological, and transport properties in the Creutz-Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (CSSH) ladder.

**Main result.** The research establishes a direct connection showing that topological properties, the formation of flat bands, and electronic transport signatures are intrinsically linked in the CSSH ladder.

**Method.** The analysis employs a tight-binding model solved via the Green's function formalism, calculating topological invariants and electronic transmission.

**Model / system.** The system is the Creutz-Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (CSSH) ladder, modeled by a tight-binding Hamiltonian exhibiting chiral symmetry and time-reversal symmetry, placing it in the BDI Altland–Zirnbauer class.

**Key observables.** Energy spectrum, Local Density of States (LDOS), electronic transmission, and the topological invariant (nu).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hopping parameters ($t_h, t'_h, t_d, t'_d$), and the system's position within the parameter space defining different topological phases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the preservation of global BDI symmetries for the stability of the topological invariant and initially focuses on the absence of a magnetic field.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the real-space structure, the evolution of the band structure through different topological phases ($
u=0, 1, 2$), and the signature of reflection matrix eigenvalues in the parameter space.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first defining the model and calculating the topological invariant, then mapping out the phase diagram using gap-closing conditions, and finally confirming the results by analyzing electronic transport and the emergence of Bound States in the Continuum (BICs).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we investigate the electronic, topological, and transport properties of a Creutz-Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (CSSH) ladder. Using a tight-binding model within the Green's function formalism, we calculate the energy spectrum, local density of states (LDOS), and electronic transmission. We first determine the energy spectrum of the CSSH ladder and analyze the different topological phases present in the system, identifying one trivial phase and three distinct nontrivial regions. We then study electronic transport and show that the transmission reproduces the different topological phases through characteristic transport signatures. Finally, we derive the conditions for the emergence of non-topological flat bands and demonstrate that these bands also provide the necessary conditions for the formation of bound states in the continuum (BICs). Our results establish a direct connection between the topological properties, flat-band formation, and electronic transport in the CSSH ladder.

</details>

**Summary.** This work extends the Polyakov bootstrap technique to Boundary Conformal Field Theories (BCFTs) to solve for crossing equations. By analyzing the flow between Neumann and Dirichlet boundary conditions, and by applying the method to the 3D Ising special transition, the authors derive new, consistent predictions for critical exponents that match existing lattice data.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of bootstrap methods to boundary critical phenomena provides powerful, non-perturbative constraints on the spectrum of boundary operators, which is crucial for understanding critical behavior in confined geometries.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper extends the truncated Polyakov bootstrap formalism to Boundary Conformal Field Theories (BCFTs) to find approximate solutions for BCFT crossing equations.

**Main result.** The formalism successfully relates the flow of BCFT data between different boundary conditions (Neumann to Dirichlet) and provides a bootstrap for the 3D Ising special transition, showing agreement with lattice results.

**Method.** It uses the analytic functional framework and the truncated Polyakov bootstrap by minimizing a loss function derived from functional sum rules to determine unknown BCFT parameters.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on BCFTs, which describe critical phenomena near a boundary. Specific applications include the flow between Generalized Free Fields (GFF) with Neumann and Dirichlet boundary conditions, and the 3D Ising special transition.

**Key observables.** Boundary operator dimensions ($\hat{\Delta}_n$), bulk OPE coefficients ($\mu^2_{\Delta n}$), and the structure of the 2-point function decomposed into bulk and boundary conformal blocks.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The flow parameter $\epsilon$ (used to connect dimensions from free theory to the 3D Ising limit, $\epsilon=1$), and deformation parameters ($\gamma_n, \delta_n, ho_n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the existence of a functional basis for correlators exhibiting the boundedness property, and the use of a 'truncated' bootstrap due to the crossing equation not being positive semi-definite.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the flow of boundary operator dimensions ($\hat{\Delta}_n$), OPE coefficients ($\mu^2_{\Delta n}$), and other parameters as the boundary condition changes from Neumann to Dirichlet. A final table compiles the calculated boundary spectrum for the 3D Ising case.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the formalism by applying it to the Neumann-to-Dirichlet flow, establishing the method. It then applies this machinery to bootstrap the 3D Ising special transition by performing a dimensional flow analysis using Padé approximants to connect the free theory limit ($\epsilon 	o 0$) to the critical point ($\epsilon=1$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We extend the recently proposed truncated Polyakov bootstrap to boundary conformal field theories (BCFTs). The formalism uses the analytic functional framework to find approximate solutions of BCFT crossing. We show that a generic nonperturbative solution can be identified by relating it to a family of deformations around generalized free fields (GFF) with a fixed boundary condition. We show this by tracking the flow of BCFT data from GFF with Neumann boundary condition to that with Dirichlet. The same method is then used for the 3d Ising special transition, using the perturbative Wilson-Fisher description as the deformation to identify it. The BCFT data obtained are in remarkable agreement with existing lattice results where available, and are otherwise new.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a rigorous framework for estimating quantum state properties using multiple measurements. It proves that the quantum U-statistic is the optimal estimator for polynomial functionals, achieving the ultimate Cramér-Rao efficiency limit. This work provides deep mathematical insights into the precision limits of quantum measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core is statistical theory, the rigorous derivation of the asymptotic efficiency limit using U-statistics provides powerful, general tools for analyzing measurement precision in quantum estimation problems relevant to quantum sensing and metrology.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the problem of achieving unbiased and asymptotically efficient estimation of scalar-valued polynomial functionals of an unknown quantum state $\rho$ from multiple independent copies.

**Main result.** It proves that the quantum U-statistic is the unique unbiased, permutation-invariant estimator, and its asymptotic variance is governed by the quantum Cramér-Rao bound, establishing efficiency.

**Method.** The analysis employs quantum U-statistics, functional gradient methods, and the quantum Hoeffding decomposition to derive variance expansions.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum states $\rho$ acting on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, and the estimation targets are polynomial functionals $f(\rho)$ of these states.

**Key observables.** The functional gradient $\nabla f(\rho)$, the quantum U-statistic estimator $\text{Osym}_n$, and the Bures $\chi^2$-divergence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of copies $n$, and the order of the polynomial functional.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis primarily focuses on permutation-invariant estimators and assumes the functional is a polynomial in $\rho$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the equivalence between the first-order marginal kernel and the functional gradient, proves the uniqueness of the quantum U-statistic, derives the universal variance expansion matching the QCRB, and applies the framework to the Bures $\chi^2$-divergence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study unbiased estimation of scalar-valued polynomial functionals of quantum states from independent copies. We establish an equivalence between the first-order marginal of a permutation-invariant finite-copy observable and the functional gradient. We then prove that, among unbiased permutation-invariant estimators, the quantum U-statistic is the unique extension to an arbitrary number of copies. We further derive a universal variance expansion in which the leading $1/n$ term is determined by the variance of the functional gradient, while higher-order contributions are of order $O(1/n^2)$. This leading variance coincides with the multiparameter quantum Cramér-Rao limit, establishing asymptotic efficiency of quantum U-statistics. We also characterize the higher-order scaling at points where the variance of the first-order gradient vanishes. As an application, we analyze the Bures $χ^2$-divergence and show that a spectral lower bound on the reference state is sufficient but not necessary for bounded-variance estimation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses inelastic neutron scattering and theoretical modeling to determine the symmetry of the magnetic order in hematite. By measuring the momentum-dependent splitting of magnons, the authors conclusively establish a $g$-wave symmetry for the altermagnetic order parameter. This finding positions hematite as a promising platform for advanced, symmetry-selective magnonic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The precise mapping of magnetic order parameter symmetry via magnon spectroscopy is a powerful tool for understanding complex magnetic ground states, which has implications for designing materials with tailored spin-charge coupling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To quantitatively determine the momentum-space symmetry of the altermagnetic order parameter in hematite ($\alpha$-Fe$_2$O$_3$).

**Main result.** The magnon splitting exhibits a $g$-wave symmetry, vanishing along nodal directions and maximizing off the nodes, confirming the altermagnetic nature of the order parameter.

**Method.** The study combines inelastic neutron scattering (INS) measurements with Linear Spin-Wave Theory (LSWT) calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is hematite ($\alpha$-Fe$_2$O$_3$), an altermagnet, modeled using an effective Heisenberg Hamiltonian that incorporates an asymmetry ($\Delta$) between two symmetry-inequivalent long-range exchange paths ($J^{(1)}_{13}$ and $J^{(2)}_{13}$).

**Key observables.** Altermagnetic magnon splitting, $\Delta(Q) = |E_{\uparrow}(Q) - E_{\downarrow}(Q)|$, and the chiral response $S_{+-}(Q, \omega) - S_{-+}(Q, \omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The splitting magnitude ($\approx 100$ meV) and the momentum dependence ($\Delta(	heta)$) are key diagnostic tools.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed splitting is dominated by the asymmetry between the two long-range exchange interactions, and that the magnon splitting can be mapped to the order parameter symmetry.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the exchange network, the momentum dependence of the splitting (showing nodal vs. off-nodal behavior), and comparisons between experimental INS data and LSWT calculations for both altermagnetic and conventional AFM models.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem, presents the theoretical model incorporating symmetry-breaking exchange terms, details the experimental INS measurements, and validates the $g$-wave symmetry by comparing the measured angular dependence against LSWT predictions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets combine the vanishing net magnetization of antiferromagnets with momentum-dependent spin splitting. Magnon band splitting provides a direct probe of altermagnetic order and may enable chirality-selective magnon transport, yet the momentum-space symmetry of this splitting has not been determined quantitatively. Here we use inelastic neutron scattering to map the momentum dependence of altermagnetic magnon splitting in hematite ($α$-Fe$_2$O$_3$). The splitting vanishes along nodal directions and reaches maxima off the nodes, revealing the $g$-wave symmetry of the altermagnetic order parameter. These results agree with linear spin-wave theory calculations based on the altermagnetic model, which further identify the nondegenerate branches as magnons of opposite chirality and trace the splitting to symmetry-inequivalent long-range exchange interactions. Our results provide the first quantitative determination of the momentum-space symmetry of altermagnetic chiral magnons. These findings, together with hematite's high magnetic ordering temperature and low magnon damping, establish it as a promising platform for low-dissipation, symmetry-selective magnonic applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a quantum-inspired heuristic to solve the MaxCut problem using a sparse Walsh/PCE encoding. It reformulates the problem's objective function using expectation values of Pauli observables, calculated efficiently via sparse autocorrelation. The method demonstrates superior performance and speed compared to established classical search algorithms on benchmark graphs.

**Why it may be interesting.** While quantum-inspired, the core contribution lies in developing a highly efficient, differentiable classical simulation technique (sparse autocorrelation) to approximate a hard combinatorial optimization problem, which is valuable for developing novel classical solvers for quantum problems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the NP-hard MaxCut problem by formulating a quantum-inspired relaxation approach.

**Main result.** The proposed sparse Walsh/PCE method outperforms random search and tabu search on multiple benchmark instances, achieving high approximation ratios and the lowest average runtime.

**Method.** It relaxes the MaxCut objective function by representing binary variables through expectation values of diagonal Pauli/Walsh observables, computed efficiently using sparse Walsh autocorrelations.

**Model / system.** The problem is mapped to an Ising-type objective function, which is then approximated using a sparse Pauli-Correlation Encoding (PCE) framework based on Walsh operators.

**Key observables.** Approximation ratios (AR) on Gset instances (G1, G6, G12, G18), and average runtime ($	au$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The method uses a sparse support of only 801 active parameters for 18 qubits, representing 0.306% of the full Walsh space.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the sparse Walsh/PCE representation provides a sufficient and efficient approximation for the MaxCut objective, followed by a final bitflip local search.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference Tables II and III, which compare the mean Approximation Ratio ($ar{	ext{AR}}$) and average runtime ($	au$) of the three methods (Walsh/PCE, Random Search, Tabu Search) across tested instances.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from the general PCE framework to a specialized, computationally efficient, and structured Walsh-based encoding, detailing the mathematical formulation, presenting comparative results against classical heuristics, and discussing future hardware implementation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a quantum-inspired Walsh/PCE solver for MaxCut based on sparse Pauli-correlation encodings. Instead of assigning one qubit or one variable to each graph vertex directly, the method represents relaxed binary variables through expectation values of diagonal Pauli/Walsh observables. These correlators are computed classically from sparse Walsh autocorrelations, producing a compact differentiable relaxation of the MaxCut objective. We evaluate the method on selected Gset instances, G1, G6, G12, and G18, and compare it with random search and tabu search over 10 independent seeds. The proposed model uses $801$ active parameters, corresponding to only $0.306\%$ of the full Walsh space over $18$ qubits. After a final bitflip local search, Walsh/PCE achieves approximation ratios of $0.99033 \pm 0.00226$ on G1, $0.95647 \pm 0.01604$ on G6, $0.96007 \pm 0.00951$ on G12, and $0.92964 \pm 0.02202$ on G18, outperforming both baselines on all tested instances. The method also yields the lowest average runtime in all cases. These results suggest that sparse Walsh/PCE representations provide an efficient quantum-inspired route for MaxCut and may be further extended to hardware-based estimation of Pauli/Walsh correlators.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes how the ground state wave function of an $N$-particle atomic system behaves when the particles separate into two distinct clusters. By establishing precise asymptotic formulas and error bounds, it provides deep mathematical insight into the physics of weakly coupled, many-body quantum systems in the large separation limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools for understanding the separation of degrees of freedom in complex quantum many-body systems, which is crucial for modeling processes like dissociation or fragmentation in atomic and molecular physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the asymptotic behavior of positive ground states for $N$-particle atomic Coulomb Hamiltonians when the system is separated into two distinct clusters.

**Main result.** The authors derive precise asymptotic formulas showing that the ground state wave function $\psi$ approaches a product form involving the ground state of the remaining $N-1$ particles, with error bounds decaying as $|x_N|^{-2}$ or faster.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced mathematical techniques, including the use of Feynman-Kac formulas, comparison theorems, and establishing Schrödinger inequalities to bound the difference between the true ground state and its asymptotic approximation.

**Model / system.** The system models $N$-particle atomic Coulomb interactions, governed by a Hamiltonian $H_N$ involving kinetic energy and Coulomb repulsion terms. The focus is on the two-cluster limit where one particle moves far away from the remaining $N-1$ particles.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the $N$-particle ground state wave function ($\psi$), its one-particle density ($ho$), and the ground state of the reduced system ($\varphi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends critically on the separation distance $|x_N| 	o \infty$ and the exponent $\alpha$ defining the spatial region of the remaining particles ($0 < \alpha < 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results hold both below the essential spectrum and at threshold, relying on the existence of ground states and specific decay properties of the wave functions in the asymptotic regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the general asymptotic behavior (Theorem 1.1) to proving rigorous error bounds (Theorem 1.5), utilizing intermediate lemmas that establish necessary inequalities (e.g., Schrödinger inequalities for $\sqrt{ho}$) and approximations based on projection operators.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the asymptotic behavior of positive ground states of $N$-particle atomic Coulomb Hamiltonians in a two-cluster region. For the $N$-particle ground state $ψ$, its one-particle density $ρ$, and $(N-1)$-particle ground state $φ$, we show that for $0<α<1$, as $|x_N|\to\infty$, $ψ(x_1,\dots,x_N)/\sqrt{ρ(x_N)}= φ(x_1,\dots,x_{N-1})+O(|x_N|^{-2+α} \ln |x_N|)$ uniformly in the region $|x_i|\le |x_N|^α$. In the region $|x_i|\le R_0\ln |x_N|$, the convergence rate is $O(|x_N|^{-2} (\ln |x_N|)^2)$. We also establish an $O(|x_N|^{-2})$ bound on the squared $L^2$ error. These results hold both below the essential spectrum and at threshold.

</details>

CAS-CI is the standard CI in that space. The two pTC curves are the same CI on the pTC Hamiltonian, with the 3-body operator either treated explicitly or replaced by the 2C mean field of Sec. II D.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a method to simplify complex, high-order electron correlation Hamiltonians derived from projective transcorrelation (pTC). By employing the two-body cumulant (2C) approximation, the computationally prohibitive three-body terms are reduced to effective one- and two-body operators. This significantly lowers the computational scaling while maintaining high accuracy for molecular energy calculations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, mathematically rigorous pathway to tame high-order correlation effects in electronic structure theory, making previously intractable calculations feasible for larger systems or quantum simulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is the computational cost associated with high-order, three-body electron correlation terms arising from similarity transformations like projective transcorrelation (pTC).

**Main result.** The authors successfully approximate the problematic 3-body term using the two-body cumulant (2C) approximation, effectively reducing the required operator rank from potentially $M^6$ to two-body terms.

**Method.** The method involves deriving effective one- and two-body interactions by taking partial derivatives of the approximated 3-body energy with respect to lower-order reduced density matrices.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to electronic structure calculations, specifically using the pTC method on molecular systems (e.g., N2) and assessed on the HEAT set for benchmarking.

**Key observables.** Atomization and reaction energies, dissociation energy of N2, and the reduction in Pauli string count on a qubit register.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The approximation relies on the 2C cumulant expansion, which discards connected three-particle correlation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the 2C approximation to the three-particle reduced density matrix is sufficiently accurate to allow the effective Hamiltonian to be written in terms of one- and two-body operators.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention comparisons of calculated energies (e.g., N2 dissociation energy) against experimental values, showing the small error introduced by the 2C approximation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the pTC Hamiltonian, details the necessity of approximating the 3-body term, derives the 2C approximation for this term, and concludes by benchmarking the resulting effective Hamiltonian on chemical accuracy benchmarks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Projective transcorrelation recovers the short-range electron correlation by a similarity transformation with a geminal function $f(r_{12})$, at the cost of an effective Hamiltonian containing a 3-body term. We replace the term by an effective operator of rank at most two, obtained from the two-body cumulant (2C) approximation to the three-particle reduced density matrix. The 2C 3-body energy is written without cumulants, and the effective one- and two-body interactions are derived as its partial derivatives with respect to the reduced density matrices. The truncation is assessed on the atomization and reaction energies of the HEAT set with CCSD(T), and on the CAS-pTC model, whose downfolded Hamiltonian is held on a qubit register with the number of Pauli strings reduced from the sixth power of the orbital count to the fourth.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the collective behavior of AI agents interacting on a public wiki, finding that their actions are not due to complex coordination but rather to a simple rule: they copy whatever they see in proportion to its visibility. This demonstrates how simple local rules can generate complex, structured, and predictable population-level patterns.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of emergent collective behavior driven by simple local rules (like proportional copying) is highly relevant to understanding complex systems in physics, such as pattern formation, consensus dynamics, or information propagation in physical media.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine how much of the collective behavior of AI agents in a simulated 'wild' environment can be explained by the simple mechanism of 'copying' what they observe.

**Main result.** The collective structure of agent behavior—including page selection, naming conventions, and writing styles—is overwhelmingly governed by local, proportional copying mechanisms based on immediate environmental exposure.

**Method.** The authors analyze a public wiki dataset, building minimal, parameterized copying models for three distinct agent decisions (where to write, what to name, how to word) and fitting these models to observed data.

**Model / system.** The system is a simulated public wiki used by AI agents during a timed test. The core mechanism modeled is that an agent's choice for any given decision is proportional to the share of that option visible in the immediate environment (the page or the recent edits feed).

**Key observables.** Heavy-tailed distribution of agents per page, frequency of name components, and the probability of adopting a specific writing form based on its local share.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The single free parameter in each minimal copying model, which quantifies the strength of the copying influence relative to inherent agent bias.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the 'cheapest' or most dominant strategy for all decisions is to copy what is visible, and that the observed behavior is a property of the population rather than individual intent.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that page selection follows the share in the recent feed (recency mechanism), name formation copies pieces from the feed, and writing conventions copy forms proportionally from the page content.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes three independent decisions (location, naming, formatting) using the same core principle: local proportional copying based on visible context (page content vs. recent feed).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In June 2026, thousands of AI agents found that a small public wiki would accept edits from inside their sandboxes, and started using it to help one another pass a timed test. Each agent lived for about an hour and remembered nothing afterwards. Nobody asked them to cooperate, and the wiki had not been built for them. The complete record of what they wrote is public, and it is unusually informative, because it preserves not only what each agent wrote but what that agent could see before writing. We use it to follow the three decisions an agent had to make on arrival: where to write, what to call itself, and how to word its message. One rule governs all three. An agent takes an option with a probability close to the share of that option in what it can see, and the share that matters is the one on the page in front of it, then the one in the stream of recent edits, and only weakly anything older. Three minimal copying models, one per decision and with a single free parameter each, reproduce the heavy-tailed distribution of how many agents met on a page, the frequency of the pieces from which the agents built their names, and the patchwork of pages that are internally consistent and different from one another. Copying whatever the environment happens to show is enough to produce most of the collective structure of this population. It is also what makes such a population easy to steer, since whoever writes first, or writes while the others are quiet, sets the convention for everyone who comes later.

</details>
