### [Exact Phase-Space Rotation in the Trapped Quantum Calogero Model](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08987v1)

**Authors:** Akash Sarkar  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.08987v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.08987_figures/2609.08987_page2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Low-resolution page preview, page 2</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.08987_figures/2609.08987_page3.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Low-resolution page preview, page 3</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2609.08987_figures/2609.08987_page4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Low-resolution page preview, page 4</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a microscopic phase-space description for the trapped quantum Calogero model, showing that the system's dynamics correspond to a rigid rotation in phase space. By analyzing the evolution of phase-space density moments, the authors rigorously prove that the trapped Hamiltonian is the unique conserved quantity in the quadratic sector. This provides a fundamental understanding of the model's integrability in the presence of external confinement.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between the exact phase-space dynamics (rigid rotation) and the identification of the Hamiltonian as the unique conserved quantity provides a powerful, non-trivial consistency check for the model's dynamics, which is highly relevant for understanding integrable many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a microscopic phase-space description for the quantum Calogero model when subjected to an external harmonic confining potential.

**Main result.** The dynamics are shown to be a rigid rotation in phase space with period 2\pi/Omega, realizing the isochronous dynamics, and the Hamiltonian is identified as the unique conserved quadratic combination of phase-space moments.

**Method.** The authors construct a Hermitian Wigner operator and derive the exact evolution equation for its expectation value, $ho$, in phase space.

**Model / system.** The system is the quantum Calogero model, specifically in the trapped version with a harmonic confining potential. The Hamiltonian involves harmonic confinement terms and pairwise interactions.

**Key observables.** The phase-space density $ho(x, t, \lambda)$ and its moments, which form rotating multiplets.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The harmonic confinement frequency $\Omega$, and the interaction strength $\ell(\ell-\hbar)$. The limit $\Omega 	o 0$ is also analyzed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the quantum Lax-pair structure and the derivation of the evolution equation for the Wigner operator.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the phase-space evolution equation for the Wigner operator, demonstrating rigid rotation. It then analyzes the conservation laws by examining the time evolution of degree-two moments to confirm that the trapped Hamiltonian is the unique conserved quadratic combination.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a microscopic phase-space description of the quantum Calogero model in the presence of an external harmonic confining potential. Building on the quantum Lax-pair structure, we construct a Hermitian Wigner operator whose expectation value obeys the exact phase-space evolution equation d_t rho + lambda d_x rho - Omega^2 x d_lambda rho = 0 for arbitrary initial states and to all orders in the interaction strength. The resulting dynamics is a rigid rotation in phase space with period 2 pi/Omega, providing a microscopic realization of the isochronous dynamics of the trapped Calogero model. We further show that the moments of the phase-space density form rotating multiplets rather than independent conserved quantities. In particular, within the quadratic sector, the unique conserved combination is proportional to the trapped Hamiltonian, providing a nontrivial consistency check of the construction. In the limit Omega -> 0, the equation reduces to the exact free-streaming equation of the untrapped model.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a quantum circuit framework to simulate polymerization kinetics by encoding the non-unitary rate matrix into a unitary quantum operation. By developing explicit block encodings using sparse-oracle and LCU methods, the authors achieve an exponential memory reduction, demonstrating a path toward simulating high-dimensional chemical reaction networks on quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a direct, resource-estimated quantum circuit blueprint for simulating complex, high-dimensional chemical dynamics, which is a key application area for quantum simulation algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Simulating the complex, exponentially growing state space of polymer polymerization kinetics using quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors developed concrete quantum circuit foundations using block encodings (Sparse-Oracle and LCU) that achieve an exponential memory saving of O(log N) qubits relative to the system size N.

**Method.** They encode the non-unitary rate matrix A governing the kinetics into a sub-block of a larger unitary operator, utilizing techniques like sparse-oracle constructions and Linear Combination of Unitaries (LCU) decomposition.

**Model / system.** The system models polymerization kinetics, specifically single-monomer (Model A) and two-monomer copolymerization (Model B), governed by linear ODEs (du/dt = Au). The state space grows exponentially with chain length.

**Key observables.** Reproduced classical time evolution for reactivity ratios; resource estimates (qubit counts, gate counts) for the encoding circuit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The matrix dimension N, the number of system qubits $n_{sys} = \lceil\log_2 Nceil$, and the gate counts scaling up to $10^4 - 10^5$ gates at $n_{sys} \sim 10^3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation is limited to linear kinetic equations (constant monomer concentration) and the encoding only addresses the matrix representation, not the full solver complexity.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the chain-growth process, the m-index labeling scheme, and circuit diagrams for the encoding oracles (e.g., Ocol for Model B).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical problem, details the mathematical formulation ($rac{du}{dt} = A u$), develops and compares two encoding strategies (Sparse-Oracle vs. LCU) for Model A and Model B, validates the encodings via simulation, and concludes with resource analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Predicting how molecular weight distribution and monomer sequence evolve during polymerization is central to polymer science, yet classical approaches face a trade-off between molecular resolution and computational cost: for copolymers, the number of distinguishable species grows exponentially with chain length. Quantum computing offers a potential alternative, provided the non-unitary rate matrices governing the kinetics can be embedded into unitary quantum circuits, a task known as block encoding. Here we construct explicit block-encoding circuits for two kinetic models of living polymerization: Model A, single-monomer polymerization, whose lower-bidiagonal rate matrix is encoded via a sparse-oracle construction and a two-term linear combination of unitaries (LCU) decomposition; and Model B, two-monomer copolymerization, where a bijective labeling of polymer species by an integer index (the m-index) yields a structured sparse matrix encoded via either a five-term LCU or a sparse-oracle construction. Numerical simulations with the sparse-oracle encodings reproduce the classical time evolution for reactivity ratios drawn from reported olefin copolymerization systems spanning near-random ($r_1 r_2 \simeq 1$) and blocky ($r_1 r_2 > 1$) microstructures, and the LCU encodings are verified by explicit reconstruction of the encoded matrix block. Resource estimation shows that both implementations require only $O(\log N)$ qubits in the matrix dimension $N$ (an exponential memory saving over the classical state space), with gate counts growing gradually, reaching $10^4$ to $10^5$ gates at $10^3$ system qubits. These results establish a concrete quantum circuit foundation for simulating polymerization kinetics on fault-tolerant quantum hardware, and a first step toward exploiting exponential state-space compression for high-dimensional polymer reaction networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This review systematically surveys the theory and practice of quantum data encoding, which is essential for running quantum algorithms. It details multiple encoding methods—such as state preparation, QRAM, and block encoding—and analyzes their associated resource costs like circuit depth and size. The work serves as a vital guide for both newcomers and experts in quantum computing hardware and algorithm design.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial theoretical roadmap for researchers aiming to implement quantum algorithms, especially those involving complex data structures or simulation inputs, by quantifying the required quantum resources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Encoding classical data (like vectors or matrices) into quantum states or circuits is a fundamental and critical bottleneck for achieving practical quantum advantage.

**Main result.** The review provides a comprehensive survey of various encoding techniques, including quantum state preparation, QRAM utilization, and block encoding, detailing their respective circuit complexities.

**Method.** The paper surveys theoretical concepts and algorithms, analyzing resource costs (circuit size, depth, T-count) associated with different data loading models.

**Model / system.** The analysis is based on the standard qubit-based circuit model, focusing on encoding classical information into quantum states or unitary operations.

**Key observables.** Circuit size, circuit depth, T-count, and the fidelity of the resulting quantum state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity scales with the size of the classical data (e.g., vector length N or matrix dimension).

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes classical data are deterministic and fully known in advance, and it is limited to the standard qubit model.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the conceptual flow from classical bits to quantum qubits via various encoding processes like state preparation and block encoding.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a comprehensive review, systematically covering quantum state preparation, unitary synthesis, QRAM implementations, structured data encoding (sparse data, Boolean functions), and block-encoding generalizations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Encoding classical data into quantum systems is a foundational step in the execution of nearly all quantum algorithms, and a critical bottleneck in realizing practical quantum advantage. This review provides a comprehensive account of the concepts, algorithms, and practical considerations associated with quantum data encoding. We trace the development from its early conceptual foundations to recent advances, considering commonly used access models, such as quantum state preparation, unitary synthesis, QRAM and block encoding. We survey the circuit size, depth, space-time tradeoffs, as well as non-Clifford resources required for fault-tolerant implementation. We also discuss the roles of different access models in quantum algorithms. Special attention is given to structured data, such as sparse data, Boolean functions and data represented by tensor networks. This review bridges theory and applications, serving both as a pedagogical guide for newcomers and as a reference for active researchers. We also highlight the pivotal role of quantum data encoding in quantum computing and provide insights into future directions that will enable quantum advantage.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally investigates the magnetic phases in the Kondo lattice materials Ce5MGe2. By measuring transport and magnetic properties, the authors distinguish between ferromagnetic ordering in Ce5RhGe2 and a complex ferrimagnetic ground state in Ce5IrGe2. The work highlights how subtle changes in the transition metal element can drastically alter the magnetic ground state in these strongly correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides detailed experimental evidence of magnetic ground states (FM vs. FIM) in heavy fermion systems, which are canonical examples of strongly correlated electron physics, relevant for understanding exotic quantum phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the magnetic phases and evolution of Kondo lattice materials, specifically Ce5RhGe2 and Ce5IrGe2, to understand the interplay between magnetism and electronic properties.

**Main result.** Ce5RhGe2 exhibits ferromagnetic ordering, while Ce5IrGe2 displays complex behavior, including two successive magnetic transitions and a ferrimagnetic ground state at low temperatures.

**Method.** The research employed systematic measurements on single crystals, including electrical resistivity, specific heat, magnetization, and magnetic susceptibility, across varying temperatures and magnetic fields.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the series of Kondo lattice compounds Ce5MGe2 (M = Co, Rh, Ir), which crystallize in the orthorhombic Pnma structure.

**Key observables.** Curie temperature (Tc), magnetic transition temperatures (TM1, TM2), magnetization plateaus (e.g., Ms/5, Ms/3), and the electronic specific heat coefficient (gamma0).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (T), applied magnetic field (H), and the chemical substitution element (M = Co, Rh, Ir).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed magnetic transitions and electronic properties are governed by the competition between local moments and conduction electrons.

**Figures summary.** Figures show resistivity, specific heat, and magnetization measurements across the series, illustrating magnetic transitions and field-dependent plateaus.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the structural and magnetic properties of Ce5MGe2, detailing the magnetic transitions observed in each compound, particularly contrasting the FM behavior of Ce5RhGe2 with the complex FIM-like state of Ce5IrGe2.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Single crystals of Ce$_5$RhGe$_2$ and Ce$_5$IrGe$_2$ have been systematically investigated by electrical resistivity, specific heat, and magnetization measurements. Together with Ce$_5$CoGe$_2$, all three compounds crystallize in the orthorhombic \emph{Pnma} structure, with the lattice parameters increasing monotonically from Co to Rh to Ir, consistent with the effect of negative chemical pressure. Magnetization measurements along the three principal crystallographic axes identify the \emph{a} axis as the easy magnetization direction throughout the series. Ce$_5$RhGe$_2$ exhibits ferromagnetic ordering with a Curie temperature of approximately 11.5 K and shows magnetic behavior closely resembling that of Ce$_5$CoGe$_2$. In contrast, Ce$_5$IrGe$_2$ undergoes two successive magnetic transitions at $T_{\rm M1}=12.7$ K and $T_{\rm M2}=11.8$ K, and there are multiple metamagnetic transitions under magnetic fields, giving rise to magnetization plateaus at fractions of the saturation magnetization $M_{\rm s}$ of approximately $M_{\rm s}/5$ and $M_{\rm s}/3$. The low-field metamagnetic transition along the easy axis shifts to lower field with decreasing temperature, and eventually a pronounced hysteresis loop is observed about zero-field, establishing that Ce$_5$IrGe$_2$ exhibits a ferrimagnetic ground state at the lowest measured temperatures.

</details>

**Authors:** Brandon Augustino, Shouvanik Chakrabarti, Enrico Fontana, Dylan Herman, Junhyung Lyle Kim, Guneykan Ozgul, Nadezhda Voronova  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.09035v1>

**Summary.** This work establishes tight, near-optimal quantum lower bounds for solving convex optimization problems using quantum oracles. By developing a novel technique based on Fourier-rank, the authors show that solving these problems requires a query complexity scaling as $\Omega(n / \log n \log \log n)$. This resolves long-standing open questions regarding the quantum speedup for optimization.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous characterization of quantum query complexity for fundamental optimization problems provides deep insights into the computational power of quantum algorithms in practical settings.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Establishing near-optimal quantum query lower bounds for high-accuracy convex optimization over explicit families of n-dimensional ellipsoids.

**Main result.** The quantum query complexity for this optimization problem is characterized by a lower bound of $\Omega\left(rac{n}{\log n \log \log n}ight)$ membership queries, up to logarithmic factors.

**Method.** The proof utilizes a novel polynomial method based on Fourier-rank, which is applied to derive lower bounds for determinant computation and subsequently reduces this hardness to the convex optimization setting.

**Model / system.** The model is a black-box quantum query model where the objective function and feasible set (ellipsoids) are accessed via oracles (membership, phase, or gradient).

**Key observables.** Query complexity (number of oracle calls), additive objective error ($\Theta(n^{-2})$), determinant value, and minimum eigenvalue.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality ($n$), accuracy ($\epsilon$), and the condition number ($\kappa$) for gradient bounds.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The lower bounds are established for specific hard families (e.g., centered ellipsoids) and the analysis relies on advanced tools from functional analysis and Fourier analysis in the continuous setting.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Fourier-rank based lower bound for determinant computation, applies this to membership queries for ellipsoids to solve the optimization lower bound, and separately derives gradient-query lower bounds for strongly convex functions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish a near-linear quantum query lower bound for high-accuracy convex optimization over an explicit family of $n$-dimensional ellipsoids. We focus on linear optimization with an explicitly given objective, where the feasible set is accessed through a membership oracle. We show that any algorithm that, for every unit linear objective, returns an exactly feasible point with additive objective error $Θ(n^{-2})$ requires $Ω\!\left(\frac{n}{\log n\,\log\log n}\right)$ membership queries. The same lower bound can be shown to hold if the returned point is only required to be approximately feasible, within $Θ(n^{-2})$ distance from the feasible set. This resolves, up to logarithmic factors, an open question posed by Chakrabarti, Childs, Li, and Wu~(\textit{Quantum}, 2020) and by van Apeldoorn, Gilyén, Gribling, and de Wolf~(\textit{Quantum}, 2020). Coupled with the upper bounds in these papers, the query complexity of high-accuracy convex optimization is characterized tightly up to logarithmic factors. The proof is built around a lower bound for determinant computation that is derived via a novel polynomial method based on Fourier-rank. In the continuous matrix phase-query model, computing the determinant of a real $n\times n$ matrix requires at least $n/2$ matrix-vector product queries. The construction also yields an $Ω(n)$ phase-query lower bound for estimating the minimum eigenvalue of a real symmetric $n\times n$ matrix to additive accuracy $Θ(n^{-2})$. These results extend the determinant and minimum-eigenvalue lower bounds of Childs, Hung, and Li~(ICALP 2021) from finite fields to the real-valued setting. Based on the same constructions, we also prove a near-optimal gradient-query lower bound for constant-accuracy optimization of smooth and strongly convex functions.

</details>

### [No information transmission through quantum channels above capacity](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08998v1)

**Authors:** Hao-Chung Cheng, Marco Tomamichel  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.08998v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.08998_figures/2609.08998_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. The asymptotic coding transition. Darker curves represent longer codes. Direct coding theorems drive the optimal error to zero below capacity, whereas a strong converse drives it to one above capacity. The corresponding exponents quantify the approach to the limiting step. No behavior at the transition point itself is asserted.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves exponential strong converse theorems for quantum and classical communication over arbitrary quantum channels. By utilizing integral representations of Rényi information, the authors demonstrate that any attempt to transmit information above the channel's capacity fails exponentially fast. This work provides fundamental, sharp limits on quantum information processing capabilities.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous characterization of capacity limits via strong converse theorems provides fundamental, non-trivial bounds on what is physically possible for information processing using quantum resources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish an exponential strong converse theorem for information transmission through quantum channels, proving that communication rates exceeding the channel capacity fail catastrophically.

**Main result.** The authors prove that at rates beyond the quantum or classical capacity, the entanglement-generation fidelity and success probability decay exponentially with the number of channel uses.

**Method.** The proof adapts the classical Arimoto strategy, crucially employing integral representations of Rényi information measures to establish asymptotic continuity bounds for Rényi capacities.

**Model / system.** The analysis concerns arbitrary finite-dimensional memoryless quantum channels, considering both classical and quantum information transmission protocols.

**Key observables.** Capacity ($C$ or $Q$), entanglement-generation fidelity ($F_{eg}$), and the success probability for classical communication.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Rényi order ($\alpha > 1$) and the transmission rate ($r$) relative to the capacity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core technical steps involve bounding fidelity using the sandwiched Rényi divergence and adapting the Leung–Smith telescoping argument.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the asymptotic coding transition, distinguishing between weak converse (fixed error) and strong converse (error tending to one) behavior.

**Paper structure.** The structure builds from defining the problem (strong converse) to introducing the mathematical machinery (Rényi measures, integral representations), deriving the converse bounds for both quantum and classical settings, and finally proving the equivalence of various rate bounds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the capacity of a quantum channel demarcates a phase transition: while reliable transmission below capacity is always possible, any attempt to transmit information above it fails catastrophically. Specifically, we prove exponential strong converse theorems for unassisted quantum and classical communication over arbitrary finite-dimensional memoryless quantum channels. At rates beyond the respective capacity, the entanglement-generation fidelity and the success probability for classical communication decay exponentially with the number of channel uses. This rules out transmission above capacity even when one tolerates arbitrarily large errors. Our proof follows the classical Arimoto strategy, augmented by a crucial new ingredient: integral representations of Rényi information measures that lead to asymptotic continuity bounds for Rényi capacities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the unusual positive temperature coefficient of magnetic anisotropy in $\epsilon$-Fe$_2$O$_3$. By combining Mössbauer spectroscopy, XAS, and RIXS, the authors propose that the effect arises from a $d^6L$ groundstate stabilized by metal-ligand hybridization and bond disproportionation. This work highlights hybridization as a key mechanism for tuning magnetic properties in correlated oxide materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed, multi-technique investigation into how hybridization mechanisms can induce strong spin-orbit coupling effects in transition metal oxides, which is central to understanding correlated electron physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to explain the origin of a positive temperature coefficient of anisotropy ($dK/dT > 0$) in $\epsilon$-Fe$_2$O$_3$, a behavior unusual for magnetic materials.

**Main result.** The positive $dK/dT$ is attributed to the stabilization of a $d^6L$ groundstate in the tetrahedral Fe sites, driven by metal-ligand hybridization and bond disproportionation.

**Method.** The research combines multiple experimental techniques, including temperature-dependent Mössbauer spectroscopy, X-ray Absorption Spectroscopy (XAS), and Resonant Inelastic X-ray Scattering (RIXS).

**Model / system.** The system under investigation is $\epsilon$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ and its Cr-doped counterpart, focusing on the electronic structure changes associated with different Fe local environments ($T_d$, $O_h$).

**Key observables.** Temperature dependence of anisotropy ($dK/dT$), Mössbauer parameters ($\delta$, $\Delta$, $B_{hf}$), and XAS spectral weight changes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range (125-200 K), doping concentration (Cr), and the electronic configuration ($d^6L$ vs. $d^6+d^6L_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the unusual magnetic behavior can be mapped onto specific changes in the local electronic structure, particularly involving metal-ligand hybridization.

**Figures summary.** Figures show temperature-dependent magnetic coercivity and XAS spectra for both pure and doped samples, alongside maps and plots detailing temperature-dependent hyperfine parameters and RIXS excitations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the anomalous $dK/dT > 0$ behavior, then using Mössbauer and XAS to probe site-specific electronic changes upon doping, and finally employing RIXS to confirm the role of metal-ligand hybridization and bond disproportionation in stabilizing the $d^6L$ state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Positive anisotropy temperature coefficients ($dK/dT>0$) usually arise from hybridization between magnetic 3d states and strongly spin-orbit-coupled (SOC) subsystems. Yet $ε$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ shows $dK/dT>0$ (125-200~K) without an obvious SOC partner. We study pure and Cr-doped (with weakened Fe-O hybridization) $ε$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ across the transition from low temperature incommensurate to high-anisotropy magnetic phases. We identify a $d^6L$ groundstate in $ε$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ while reduced hybridization yields $d^6 + d^6L^2$ in the Cr-doped system, highlighting metal-ligand hybridization as a route to tune magnetic, electronic and orbitronic properties.

</details>

### [Promises should be taken seriously: On relativization with promise problems](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07945v1)

**Authors:** David Miloschewsky, Supartha Podder, Dorian Rudolph  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.07945v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.07945_figures/2609.07945_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Three different types of oracle access to promise problems.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the complex theoretical issue of how computational power changes when moving from standard language problems to promise problems, especially when using black-box oracles. The authors show that standard complexity results fail to transfer to the promise setting, establishing fundamental separations between classes like $	ext{PromiseBQP}$ and $	ext{PromiseP}$. It provides deep insights into the structure of quantum and classical complexity hierarchies under these generalized computational models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, the rigorous analysis of computational models and the limitations of standard complexity results (like those transferring from languages to promises) informs the fundamental limits of quantum computation, which is crucial for understanding the feasibility of quantum algorithms in physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the computational differences between standard language problems and promise problems when using black-box oracle access, particularly concerning how unconstrained 'off-promise' inputs affect complexity classes.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that results proven for languages do not necessarily transfer to promise problems, establishing separations like $	ext{PromiseBQP}^O 
ot\subseteq 	ext{PromiseP}^O_{	ext{/poly}}$ for a specific oracle $O$. Furthermore, it establishes that $	ext{PromiseYQP}^* = 	ext{PP}$ under certain assumptions.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced techniques from complexity theory, defining and comparing various oracle access semantics (robust, loose) and adapting proof techniques, such as those used for the Quantum-Classical Polynomial Hierarchy, to the promise setting.

**Model / system.** The model is purely theoretical, focusing on abstract computational complexity classes ($	ext{P}, 	ext{BQP}, 	ext{PP}$, etc.) and their generalizations to promise problems ($	ext{PromiseP}, 	ext{PromiseBQP}$). The 'oracle' represents an external computational source whose behavior is constrained by the problem's promise.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the containment and separation relationships between various complexity classes (e.g., $	ext{PromiseBQP}$ vs. $	ext{PromiseP}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the definitions of oracle access semantics (robust vs. loose) and the structure of the promise sets ($\Pi_{	ext{yes}}, \Pi_{	ext{no}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on constructing specific oracles $O$ to demonstrate separations, and the results often depend on the specific definition of oracle access used (e.g., robust queries).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the three types of oracle access (Smart, Robust, Loose) to promise problems, comparing the required success probabilities for different query types.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the necessity of defining oracle access carefully (Observation 1.1). It then proves separations between language and promise settings (Result 1), improves bounds on the Quantum-Classical Polynomial Hierarchy using loose access (Result 2), and finally establishes relationships between specialized promise classes like $	ext{PromiseYQP}^*$ and $	ext{PromisePostBQP}^*$ (Result 3).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Relativization is concerned with comparing computational models with black-box access to an oracle. For promise problems, black-box access is not canonical due to inputs outside of the promise being unconstrained. We study two semantics for such access. Under robust queries, a machine must correctly answer regardless of the completion of the problem,, while loose access requires that the internal choices of a machine do not change based on off-promise queries.   Our first result separates the language and promise settings. Namely, we construct an oracle $O$ such that $\mathsf{P}^O = \mathsf{BQP}^O = \mathsf{AWPP}^O$, but $\mathsf{PromiseBQP}^O\not\subseteq\mathsf{PromiseP}^O_{\mathsf{/poly}}$. In particular, $\mathsf{BPP}^O = \mathsf{BQP}^O$, but $\mathsf{PromiseBQP}^O \neq \mathsf{PromiseBPP}^O$, showing that results for languages need not transfer to promises. Next, we use loose queries to strengthen the upper bound on the Quantum-Classical Polynomial Hierarchy from $\mathsf{P}^{\mathsf{PP}^{\mathsf{PP}}}$ to $\mathsf{QCPH} \subseteq \mathsf{BP\cdot PP} \subseteq \mathsf{PromiseBPP}^{\mathsf{PP}}$. The same proof also shows $\mathsf{PP}^\mathsf{PromiseBQP} = \mathsf{PP}$. Additionally, we show that $\mathsf{PromiseBQP}$, even when given quantum advice, is self-low under robust queries. Finally, we exhibit an obstruction to transferring language-level counting results to promise classes. Although $\mathsf{AWPP}$ and $\mathsf{APP}$ are low for $\mathsf{PP}$, a corresponding promise analogue would collapse the counting hierarchy as $\mathsf{GapP} \subseteq \mathsf{FP}^{\mathsf{PromiseAWPP}}$. This motivates the introduction of $\mathsf{PromisePostBQP^*}$, which restricts $\mathsf{PostBQP}$ to input-indepencent postselection. By showing that it is low for \PP, we obtain $\mathsf{PP}^{\mathsf{PromiseYQP^*}} = \mathsf{PP}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a moment-based methodology to quantify the nonclassicality of quantum states using the Margenau--Hill quasiprobability distribution. By focusing on low-order moments rather than full distribution reconstruction, the authors establish a hierarchy of rigorous lower bounds for the negativity. This provides a practical, experimentally accessible framework for characterizing quantum features.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, measurable, and computationally tractable diagnostic tool (moment-based) to quantify nonclassical features of quantum states, which is highly relevant for characterizing quantum resources in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to quantify the nonclassicality associated with the Margenau--Hill quasiprobability (MHQ) distribution without needing to reconstruct the full distribution.

**Main result.** A moment-based framework is established, yielding a hierarchy of rigorous lower bounds for the MHQ negativity, with the fourth-moment bound being the strongest among even-moment bounds.

**Method.** The authors utilize the moments (both standard and absolute) of the MHQ distribution, deriving lower bounds for the negativity using techniques related to R\'enyi entropy and moment-matrix conditions.

**Model / system.** The study concerns quantum states ($ho$) on a $d$-dimensional Hilbert space, specifically analyzing the MHQ distribution derived from two observables, $A$ and $B$, defined by spectral projectors.

**Key observables.** Logarithmic MHQ negativity, low-order moments ($m_n, e_n$), and the moment-matrix condition for positivity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order of the moment ($r$ or $2n$), and the dimensionality of the Hilbert space ($d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the relevant moments admit exact multi-copy representations, allowing estimation via interferometry or classical shadow techniques.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the MHQ distribution and the concept of nonclassicality via its negativity. It then develops the moment-based quantification, deriving the lower bound hierarchy, and finally applies these results to qubits to find tight bounds and optimal thresholds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quasiprobability distributions offer a useful way of describing nonclassical features of quantum systems through their departure from classical probability theory. In this work, we investigate the quantification of nonclassicality associated with the Margenau--Hill quasiprobability (MHQ) distribution using its moments, without requiring reconstruction of the full distribution. First we introduce the logarithmic MHQ negativity as a quantifier of nonclassicality, and then derive a hierarchy of rigorous lower bounds in terms of low-order moments. Within the resulting hierarchy, the fourth-moment bound is the strongest among the bounds based on even moments. For qubits, we further derive a tight upper bound on the exact MHQ negativity and identify the corresponding extremal state. We also obtain an optimal positivity threshold based on the fourth moment for arbitrary pairs of qubit observables. Finally, we show that the relevant moments admit exact multicopy representations, providing a route to their estimation using interferometry or classical shadow techniques. Our results establish a moment-based framework for extracting quantitative information about MHQ negativity from a finite set of low-order observables.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Q-WAVE, a novel hybrid quantum-machine learning method designed to calculate accurate ground-state wavefunctions for strongly correlated molecules. It combines limited quantum circuit sampling with CISD information, using a Variational Autoencoder to intelligently augment the basis set. The approach demonstrates state-of-the-art accuracy and scalability on challenging systems like $	ext{Cr}_2$.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it proposes a machine learning-driven pathway to overcome the exponential scaling bottleneck in quantum chemistry, offering a scalable alternative to pure quantum simulation for complex molecular systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Accurately computing the ground-state wavefunction for many-body systems, particularly in strongly correlated regimes where the required determinant space exceeds the capacity of finite quantum sampling.

**Main result.** The proposed Q-WAVE method achieves sub-millihartree accuracy for $	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$ and $	ext{N}_2$ dissociation and demonstrates chemical accuracy for a 60-qubit $	ext{Cr}_2$ system, surpassing standard quantum sampling techniques.

**Method.** It introduces Q-WAVE, a hybrid approach combining determinants sampled via SqDRIFT Krylov circuits and CISD determinants, which are then used to train a $eta$-annealed Variational Autoencoder (VAE) to generate the full wavefunction support structure in a continuous latent space.

**Model / system.** The work applies to molecular Hamiltonians (e.g., $	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$, $	ext{N}_2$, ethylene, $	ext{Cr}_2$) by representing the electronic state via Slater determinants. The core computational challenge is managing the exponentially large Hilbert space in strongly correlated regimes.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energies, wavefunction support structure, and comparison against full CI and $	ext{CCSD}(	ext{T})$ benchmarks.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The method relies on the VAE's latent space exploration, the initial seed from CISD, and the quantum sampling fidelity from SqDRIFT circuits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the wavefunction's structure can be effectively learned and augmented by mapping sampled determinants into a continuous latent space via the VAE.

**Figures summary.** Table V summarizes final energies and subspace sizes for representative systems ($	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$, $	ext{N}_2$, $	ext{C}_2	ext{H}_4$, $	ext{Cr}_2$), comparing Q-WAVE results against other established methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of existing quantum and classical methods for strongly correlated systems. It then details the Q-WAVE framework, combining hardware sampling (SqDRIFT) with deterministic seeds (CISD) and using a $eta$-annealed VAE for iterative, generative expansion of the variational basis. Finally, it presents benchmark results on various molecules.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) has emerged as a promising route for quantum-centric supercomputing, relying on classical diagonalization of the molecular Hamiltonian within a hardware-sampled determinant subspace. However, its accuracy degrades in strongly correlated regimes where the relevant determinant space exceeds what finite-shot sampling can capture. In this work, we introduce Quantum Wavefunction Augmentation via Variational Autoencoders (Q-WAVE), a hybrid method that combines determinants sampled via SqDRIFT Krylov circuits and configuration interaction singles and doubles (CISD) determinants with generative machine learning. Using a custom $β$-annealed variational autoencoder (VAE) model, Q-WAVE iteratively expands this basis toward the variational ground state. The VAE learns the wavefunction's primary support structure from the combined hardware and CISD seed in a continuous latent space, generating new dominant determinants beyond any fixed excitation hierarchy. The resulting compact wavefunction exceeds what can be extracted from raw hardware samples alone. We demonstrate sub-millihartree accuracy compared to full configuration interaction for $\text{H}_2\text{O}$ and $\text{N}_2$ dissociation. Finally, we establish Q-WAVE's scalability on a 52-qubit ethylene system (achieving sub-millihartree accuracy versus CCSD(T)) and a highly correlated 60-qubit $\text{Cr}_2$ stress test that attains chemical accuracy upon a final perturbative correction.

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental work uses Resonant Inelastic X-ray Scattering to probe high-order phonon excitations in the cuprate superconductor La2-xSr_xCuO4. The findings reveal selective coupling to even-ordered phonons, which deviates from simple theoretical expectations. This suggests exotic electronic behavior, potentially involving locally paired quasiparticles, that influences the superconducting state.

**Why it may be interesting.** The investigation into high-order phonon coupling and its selective nature provides crucial experimental constraints on theoretical models of pairing mechanisms in high-Tc superconductors, which are central to strongly correlated electron physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to uncover a new regime of electron-phonon coupling in the cuprate superconductor La2-xSr_xCuO4 by analyzing high-order phonon excitations.

**Main result.** The researchers observed sharp, non-dispersive peaks corresponding to two-phonon and four-phonon excitations, suggesting anomalous electron-phonon coupling beyond the standard Franck-Condon picture.

**Method.** The primary method employed was Resonant Inelastic X-ray Scattering (RIXS) at the O K-edge, complemented by comparison with Raman spectroscopy.

**Model / system.** The physical system is La2-xSr_xCuO4, a prototypical p-type cuprate superconductor. The analysis focuses on the coupling between charge carriers and lattice vibrations, particularly the in-plane Cu-O bond stretching mode.

**Key observables.** Phonon excitation energies (e.g., 85 meV, 180 meV, 330 meV), doping dependence of these excitations, and the comparison of RIXS spectra to Raman spectra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hole doping concentration (x), which dictates the electronic state, and the energy scale of the phonon modes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The anomalous coupling suggests possibilities like the existence of locally paired quasiparticles or bipolarons, moving beyond simple harmonic oscillator models.

**Figures summary.** Figures show X-ray absorption spectra identifying electronic bands (UHB, ZRS) and RIXS color plots mapping low-energy excitations versus momentum for various dopings, contrasting with Raman spectra.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents RIXS measurements across different doping levels, identifying specific phonon features ($\alpha, eta, \gamma$) and tracking their evolution. It compares these findings to established Raman data to argue for an anomalous coupling mechanism.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In crystals and molecules with strong coupling between the charge carriers and atomic vibrations, high order harmonics of the vibrational excitations can be observed with intensity following the Franck-Condon envelope. Here, we uncover a new regime of electron-phonon coupling in La2-xSrxCuO4 using resonant inelastic X-ray scattering. In the undoped compound, we find sharp peaks at approximately 85$\pm$5 meV, 180$\pm$7 meV, and 330$\pm$5 meV with no observable dispersion, and an absence of appreciable intensity from other modes observed in Raman spectroscopy in this energy range. The 180 meV and 330 meV excitations have energies approximately consistent with two-phonon and four-phonon in-plane Cu-O bond stretching mode, suggesting that the resonantly excited valence electrons strongly couple to this mode. These excitations exhibit significant doping dependence, eventually becoming unresolvable at 10% hole doping, where the low energy charge excitations are dominated by dispersive plasmons. The observation of selective coupling to even-ordered phonons and stark contrast with the Raman spectra indicate anomalous electron-phonon coupling beyond the Franck-Condon picture. One intriguing possibility is the existence of locally paired quasiparticles in insulating cuprates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper derives strong, quantitative lower bounds on the size of semidefinite programs needed to approximate the set of separable quantum states. By connecting these bounds to the concept of approximate disentanglers, the authors provide a powerful tool to study the computational complexity of quantum information tasks, improving upon previous results in the field.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical tools (SDP complexity bounds) to quantify the computational separation between quantum complexity classes, which is fundamental to understanding the limits of quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish quantitative lower bounds on the size of Semidefinite Programs (SDPs) used to approximate the maximum acceptance probability over separable quantum states, which is central to understanding the complexity class QMA(2).

**Main result.** They prove that the required SDP size grows superpolynomially with the dimension $d$ for constant approximation errors, improving previous bounds significantly.

**Method.** The proof combines the quantitative pseudo-density theorem of Lee, Raghavendra, and Steurer with the extended-formulation model of SDPs, relating the required size to the approximation error.

**Model / system.** The focus is on bipartite quantum systems ($\mathbb{C}^d \otimes \mathbb{C}^d$) and the set of separable quantum states, $	ext{Sep}(d:d)$. The context is quantum complexity theory, specifically QMA(2).

**Key observables.** The maximum acceptance probability $h_{Sep}(Q)$ and the required size ($r$) of the SDP approximation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$, the additive approximation error $a$, and the entanglement/disentangler errors $\epsilon, \delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the SDP extended-formulation model (HNW) and the existence of an approximate disentangler $\Lambda$ whose failure to perfectly represent the separable set is quantified by the error $a$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives lower bounds on the SDP size required to approximate the separable set, first for the general SDP formulation and then specifically by relating it to the structure imposed by approximate disentanglers.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove quantitative lower bounds on the semidefinite extension complexity of the set of separable quantum states on $\mathbb{C}^d\otimes\mathbb{C}^d$. We consider semidefinite programs (SDPs) that approximate the maximum acceptance probability of a measurement over separable states, the optimization problem underlying QMA(2). In the extended-formulation model of Harrow, Natarajan, and Wu (HNW), all measurements share a common feasible region and an objective-independent embedding of product states that exactly reproduces their acceptance probabilities. For every $0<θ<2/7$, there are constants $c_θ,a_θ>0$ such that, for sufficiently large $d$, any such SDP with uniform additive error $0<a\le a_θ$ has size at least $d^{c_θ\min\{a^{-1/3},d^θ\}}$. The bound applies at sufficiently small constant error, is superpolynomial in $d$ whenever $a=o(1)$, and becomes $d^{Ω(d^θ)}$ when $a\le d^{-3θ}$, improving HNW's quasipolynomial bound at inverse- square error. The same bound holds for any SDP-representable convex set of states that contains all separable states and lies within trace distance $a$ of them, giving a quantitative counterpart to Fawzi's theorem that the separable set has no exact semidefinite representation. Our proof combines the quantitative pseudo-density theorem of Lee, Raghavendra, and Steurer with explicit block-positive operators and Chebyshev amplification. Our main results are supported by Lean proofs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses spectral graph theory to analyze the dynamics of the Grover walk on specific graph families. It proves that the only connected graphs exhibiting a 6-periodic Grover walk are the Dutch windmill graphs $D(t)_3$ and the uniform theta graphs $\Theta(t,2)$. The work provides a complete spectral characterization for $\Theta(t, 2)$ based on its normalized adjacency matrix spectrum.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between the spectral properties of graph Laplacians/Adjacency matrices and the periodicity of quantum walks provides a powerful tool for characterizing quantum systems on structured media.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to spectrally characterize the uniform theta graph $\Theta(t,2)$ and classify all connected graphs that exhibit a 6-periodic Grover walk.

**Main result.** The only connected 6-periodic graphs are the Dutch windmill graphs $D(t)_3$ and the uniform theta graphs $\Theta(t,2)$. Furthermore, $\Theta(t, 2)$ is uniquely determined by the spectrum of its normalized adjacency matrix.

**Method.** The analysis combines spectral graph theory (using the normalized adjacency and Laplacian matrices) with the mathematical properties of the Grover walk's time evolution operator $U(G)$.

**Model / system.** The model is the Grover walk, a discrete-time quantum walk, analyzed on finite, simple graphs $G$. The dynamics are governed by the time evolution matrix $U(G)$.

**Key observables.** The spectrum of the normalized adjacency matrix $T(G)$ and the periodicity $	au$ of the Grover walk ($U(G)^	au = I$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameters $t$ and $n$ defining the specific graph structures $\Theta(t,n)$ and $D_n^{(t)}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the graphs are connected and finite, and relies on relating the periodicity of the quantum walk directly to the eigenvalues of $T(G)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of the graphs (e.g., $\Theta(t, n)$, $D(t)_n$) and the action of the time evolution operator $U$ on basis vectors on the graph's arcs.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the spectral characterization of $\Theta(t, 2)$ and the periodicity of $D_n^{(t)}$ and $\Theta(t, n)$. It then uses the 6-periodicity condition to constrain the spectrum of $T(G)$, leading to a classification theorem that identifies $D(t)_3$ and $\Theta(t, 2)$ as the only possibilities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We characterize the uniform theta graph $Θ(t,2)$ by the spectrum of its normalized adjacency matrix, or equivalently, by the spectrum of its normalized Laplacian matrix. We also investigate the periodicity of Grover walks on nonregular graphs, which is closely related to the eigenvalues of the normalized adjacency matrix and those of the time evolution matrix of the Grover walk. We show that the Dutch windmill graph $D_n^{(t)}$ is $2n$-periodic and that the uniform theta graph $Θ(t,n)$ is $(2n+2)$-periodic. Furthermore, we completely determine the connected $6$-periodic graphs and prove that they are precisely $D_3^{(t)}$ with $t \geq 2$ and $Θ(t,2)$ with $t \geq 1$.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on the observation of successive phase transitions in a $\nu=3/2$ Fractional Quantum Hall state within a GaAs quantum well, driven solely by an in-plane magnetic field. The system transitions from a Composite Fermion liquid through a soft-gap state to a robust, hard-gap FQH state. This demonstrates that topological order can be engineered by manipulating the electron's k-space Fermi contour using external magnetic fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observation of field-induced topological transitions in a highly correlated electron system like the FQH state provides a clean experimental platform to study how external fields can engineer topological order, which has implications for understanding exotic quantum states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the successive phase transitions occurring in a Fractional Quantum Hall (FQH) state at filling fraction $\nu=3/2$ when driven by an in-plane magnetic field ($B_{\parallel}$).

**Main result.** The system undergoes two distinct transitions: from a Composite Fermion (CF) liquid to a soft-gap FQH state, followed by a transition to a hard-gap, robust FQH state, accompanied by a daughter state at $\nu=19/13$.

**Method.** Measurements of magnetoresistance ($R_{xx}$) and Hall resistance ($R_{xy}$) are performed on an ultra-high-mobility GaAs quantum well by varying the in-plane magnetic field and tilt angle.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a two-dimensional electron gas (2DEG) in a GaAs quantum well, exhibiting correlated electron behavior characteristic of the Fractional Quantum Hall Effect. The theoretical framework involves composite fermion theory and analyzing the splitting of the Fermi contour in k-space.

**Key observables.** FQH plateaus, resistance jumps, gap energy ($\Delta''$), and the evolution of $R_{xx}$ and $R_{xy}$ as a function of $B_{\parallel}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling factor $\nu=3/2$, in-plane magnetic field $B_{\parallel}$ (transition points at $\sim 12.2$ T and $\sim 14.7$ T), and the tilt angle $\theta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the in-plane field causes a k-space Fermi contour splitting, driving the transition from a CF liquid to a topologically distinct FQH state.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the evolution of $R_{xx}$ and $R_{xy}$ with tilt angle and $B_{\parallel}$, specifically highlighting the two transition points at $\nu=3/2$ and the associated daughter state at $\nu=19/13$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents data showing the initial CF liquid state, followed by the first transition to a soft-gap FQH state, and finally the second transition to a hard-gap, robust FQH state, supported by gap measurements and theoretical interpretations of the k-space physics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report an even-denominator fractional quantum Hall state at ν = 3/2 induced entirely by in-plane magnetic field B_|| in an ultra-high-mobility GaAs quantum well. As B_|| increases, the system undergoes two successive transitions: from a composite fermion liquid to a soft-gap FQH state (B_||~12.2 T), then via a topological phase transition to a hard-gap robust FQH state (B_||~14.7 T), accompanied by a daughter state at ν = 19/13. We present systematic data, and discuss a possible scenario in interpreting these findings. Our work demonstrates that topological order may be engineered through k-space Fermi contour splitting under an in-plane magnetic field.

</details>

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces and applies the rugosity metric, derived from quantum texture, to study quantum phase transitions in the Heisenberg XXZ spin chain. The authors demonstrate that rugosity provides highly sensitive signatures at critical points, particularly highlighting the unique behavior at the BKT transition ($\Delta=1$). This establishes rugosity as a powerful, simple diagnostic tool for probing the structure of quantum correlations across the phase diagram.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of a geometric/information-theoretic measure (rugosity) to characterize fundamental physical transitions (QPTs) provides a novel, potentially universal diagnostic tool for strongly correlated quantum matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to use the quantum texture, specifically the rugosity metric, as a simple and effective diagnostic tool to probe quantum phase transitions (QPTs) in quantum spin systems.

**Main result.** Rugosity exhibits distinct, sharp signatures at both the first-order ($\Delta=-1$) and BKT ($\Delta=1$) transition points of the Heisenberg XXZ model, showing sensitivity to the underlying spin-correlation hierarchy.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical analysis, utilizing the rugosity metric derived from quantum texture, and analyzes finite-size scaling behavior across the phase diagram.

**Model / system.** The primary system is the Heisenberg XXZ model defined on a spin chain, characterized by the anisotropy parameter $\Delta$. The Hamiltonian is studied in the context of its known QPTs at $\Delta=-1$ and $\Delta=1$.

**Key observables.** Rugosity ($R$), symmetry-resolved rugosity ($R_Q$), and the extensive coefficient ($\alpha$) derived from finite-size scaling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The anisotropy parameter $\Delta$, which tunes the system between different magnetic phases, and the system size $N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that rugosity is a powerful witness for QPTs, and that the finite-size scaling can be accurately modeled by $R(N) = \alpha N + eta \ln N + c$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of rugosity on $\Delta$ for different magnetization sectors, showing parity effects at $\Delta=-1$ and a peak at $\Delta=1$. Scaling analysis confirms the dominance of the extensive term $\alpha N$ near transitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of quantum texture and rugosity, applies it to the XXZ model, analyzes the results by comparing global vs. symmetry-resolved rugosities across the phase diagram, and concludes by interpreting the observed signatures at the critical points.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The idea of quantum texture has been recently proposed and used as a tool for quantifying coherences and for quantum gate identification. In this work we offer a study on its usage to quantum phase transitions, demonstrating the rugosity metric as a simple tool for effective phase-transition probing. We establish the link between rugosity in the computational basis and the hierarchy of spin correlators, and analyze rugosities defined in the global ground-state and in ground-states belonging to different magnetization sectors (to which we refer to as global vs symmetry-resolved rugosities) to study the phase diagram of the Heisenberg XXZ model. We find distinct rugosity signatures at both transition points. In particular, a sharp feature appears at $Δ=1$ already for small systems, revealing a pronounced sensitivity of the correlation hierarchy encoded by the texture to this point. Since the BKT transition coincides with the isotropic $SU(2)$ point of the XXZ model, this behavior may reflect a particular sensitivity of rugosity to the structure of the spin-correlation hierarchy at isotropy.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a resource-efficient method for performing logical fanout operations in distributed quantum computing by leveraging transversal operations on error-corrected code blocks. By analyzing the resource costs and simulating the logical error rates, the authors demonstrate that this approach significantly improves fault tolerance over naive implementations. It provides a systematic framework for building complex, high-fanout quantum circuits across multiple physical nodes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on computation, the analysis of error propagation and resource scaling in distributed quantum networks is highly relevant to understanding noise management in physical quantum hardware platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the resource-efficient implementation of logical fanout operations, which are crucial primitives for fault-tolerant distributed quantum computing.

**Main result.** The authors propose using transversal logical operations on encoded blocks to construct distributed fanout circuits that significantly reduce the required non-local communication compared to direct implementations.

**Method.** They develop a systematic method by exploiting the structure of quantum error-correcting codes (like the BB-code) and analyzing the resulting physical gate requirements, comparing GHZ-based versus CNOT-based resource costs.

**Model / system.** The model is distributed quantum computing across multiple nodes, utilizing quantum error-correcting code blocks (specifically the Bivariate-Bicycle or BB-code) to encode logical qubits.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate (LER), circuit-depth, entanglement requirements (Bell pairs vs. GHZ states), and physical gate decomposition.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rate ($p$), inter-node ebit noise ($p_{	ext{ebit}}$), and the number of nodes (e.g., 4 or 8).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison of resources assumes the availability of multi-party GHZ states; the analysis is circuit-level and depends on the fidelity of syndrome extraction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the distributed fanout operation and the complex, multi-step construction of global gates like $	ext{GCZ}_{16}$ over multiple nodes, showing the required reordering of local and inter-node operations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the problem (logical fanout) to proposing a transversal gadget, analyzing resource costs (Table I), simulating the performance (LER vs. PER plots), and finally applying the technique to construct complex global gates like $	ext{GCZ}$ circuits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a resource-efficient approach for implementing logical fanout operations in fault-tolerant distributed quantum computing using transversal operations on quantum error-correcting code blocks. Logical fanout, comprising multiple controlled-NOT operations from a common control qubit to target qubits located at remote nodes, is an important primitive for distributed quantum computation but can require substantial non-local communication when implemented directly between encoded blocks. We exploit the structure of encoded blocks and the availability of transversal logical operations to construct distributed fanout circuits that reduce the required non-local operations while preserving the logical action of the fanout operation. The construction is developed for encoded quantum information and illustrated using Bivariate Bicycle (BB)-code blocks. We analyze the resulting physical gate, entanglement, circuit-depth, and ancilla requirements. The approach provides a systematic method for implementing large logical fanout operations across distributed error-corrected quantum processors. Also, we study a distributed implementation of the global gate GCZ involving logical qubits (encoded using BB-code blocks), exploiting the concurrency in transversal distributed fanouts.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the application of Trapdoor Functions (TDFs) to quantum cryptographic primitives such as secure key leasing and copy protection. The authors construct novel, robust cryptographic schemes for PKE-SKL and SDE that solve the critical vulnerability of key destruction. The work advances the theoretical understanding of TDFs in quantum security contexts.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the topic is purely theoretical cryptography, the underlying mathematical rigor and the focus on information-theoretic security concepts (like those derived from quantum principles) are highly relevant to the theoretical foundations of quantum information science.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper extends the study of Trapdoor Functions (TDFs) to quantum cryptographic primitives like secure key leasing (TDF-SKL) and copy protection (TDF-CP). It aims to develop robust cryptographic schemes based on these concepts.

**Main result.** The authors provide constructions for TDF-SKL and TDF-CP, and subsequently construct robust Public Key Encryption with Secure Key Leasing (PKE-SKL) and Single-Decryptor Encryption (SDE) schemes that resist key-destruction attacks.

**Method.** The work involves formalizing new definitions for TDF-SKL and TDF-CP, providing constructions based on assumptions like the LWE problem, and employing advanced cryptographic proof techniques (e.g., hybrid games, averaging arguments) to establish security.

**Model / system.** The model is purely theoretical, focusing on quantum cryptographic primitives inspired by the no-cloning theorem. It deals with abstract concepts like quantum trapdoors, key leasing, and copy protection functionalities.

**Key observables.** Security notions such as IND-VRA (Indistinguishability under Verifiable Random Access) and CPA+ anti-piracy security, alongside the reusability of quantum decryption keys.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The security parameter $\lambda$ and polynomial functions $p(\lambda)$ are used in the security proofs.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The constructions rely on the Learning With Errors (LWE) assumption, indistinguishability obfuscation (iO), and the existence of specific pseudorandom generators.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 describes the algorithm $B^*$, which is used in the security proof to simulate an experiment and compare outputs to establish the security bound for TDFSKL.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces TDF-SKL and TDF-CP definitions, presents constructions for both, and then applies these to build robust PKE-SKL and SDE schemes, culminating in detailed security proofs for these robust schemes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Inspired by the no-cloning theorem in quantum theory, a variety of quantum cryptographic primitives with unclonable functionalities, such as secure key leasing and copy protection, have been proposed and attracted significant attention. However, trapdoor functions (TDFs), fundamental primitives in public-key cryptography, have not been extensively studied in these areas. In this work, we initiate a study of TDFs in both secure key leasing and copy protection settings. We first introduce the definition of TDFs with secure key leasing (TDF-SKL), which enables leasing and deleting of quantum trapdoors. We formalize TDF-SKL both with and without domain sampler, and give a construction of TDF-SKL without domain sampler based on the LWE assumption and a construction of TDF-SKL with domain sampler based on any standard PKE schemes combined with hinting pseudorandom generators [Koppula and Waters, CRYPTO 2019]. Next, we define TDFs with copy protection (TDF-CP), where the inversion functionality is copy protected by a quantum trapdoor. We establish a construction of TDF-CP assuming indistinguishability obfuscation and the LWE assumption, following a modular framework of copy protection proposed by Ananth and Behera [CRYPTO 2024]. We also present applications of TDF-SKL and TDF-CP. Existing constructions of public-key encryption with secure key leasing (PKE-SKL) and single-decryptor encryption (SDE) suffer from a critical vulnerability: quantum decryption keys may be destroyed after decrypting maliciously chosen ciphertexts. We construct PKE-SKL schemes and SDE schemes with robust quantum decryption keys that remain reusable after decrypting arbitrary ciphertexts from TDF-SKL and TDF-CP, respectively.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally and theoretically investigates quantum confinement in bulk $	ext{MoS}_2$ using ARPES and DFT. The key finding is the observation of discrete quantum well states whose energy spacing follows a linear trend, suggesting a parabolic potential rather than the expected quadratic dependence. This confirms that mechanical exfoliation creates a quantum-confined multilayer stack suitable for studying fundamental quantum physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observation of confinement in a solid-state material like $	ext{MoS}_2$ provides a platform to study fundamental quantum phenomena, such as intersubband transitions, in a controllable, layered solid-state system.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to experimentally observe and theoretically characterize quantum confinement states (QWSs) within the electronic structure of bulk $	ext{MoS}_2$.

**Main result.** ARPES measurements reveal QWSs at the $\Gamma$ point, confirming vertical quantum confinement, which exhibits a linear energy dependence on the quantum well index $n$, suggesting a parabolic-like potential.

**Method.** The research combines Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) experiments with Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations to analyze the electronic band structure.

**Model / system.** The physical system is bulk $	ext{MoS}_2$, where confinement is induced by the mechanical exfoliation process, effectively creating a multilayer stack detached from the bulk.

**Key observables.** Quantum well states (QWSs) at the $\Gamma$ point, absence of $k_z$ dispersion, strong intensity modulation with photon energy, and the linear binding energy dependence of QWSs vs $n$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon energies ($h
u = 40, 70, 100 	ext{ eV}$), quantum well index ($n$), and the layer thickness/decoupling distance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The confinement arises from the mechanical exfoliation process, creating reflective boundaries at the top and bottom interfaces of the multilayer stack.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the $	ext{MoS}_2$ structure, ARPES intensity curvature along high-symmetry directions, and model fits showing the linear energy spacing of QWSs against a half-parabolic potential.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from experimental observation of QWSs via ARPES, confirming confinement, to theoretical modeling using DFT to explain the linear energy spacing and the physical mechanism of multilayer decoupling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We experimentally observe quantum confinement states in bulk MoS$_2$ using Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES). The band structure at the $\overlineΓ$ point reveals quantum well states (QWSs) linked to vertical quantum confinement of the electrons, confirmed by the absence of dispersion in k$_z$ and a strong intensity modulation with photon energy. Notably, the binding energy dependence of the QWSs vs $n$ does not follow the quadratic dependence of a two-dimensional electron gas. Instead, a linear behaviour is observed that is consistent with a parabolic-like quantum well. This confinement arises from the mechanical exfoliation preparation method, which leads to the detachment of a multilayer stack from the underlying bulk. This is confirmed by Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations. The quantum confinement in bulk-like MoS$_2$ not only offers the opportunity to explore intersubband transitions to exploit optical properties but also provides a means to study fundamental quantum phenomena in multilayer stacks of different thicknesses.

</details>

**Authors:** Christopher M. Stanley  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.08913v1>

**Summary.** This work tackles the challenge of accurately calculating the heat capacity of $	ext{ZrC}$ at high temperatures by employing the Beyond Quasi-Harmonic (BQH) method. The BQH approach successfully captures anharmonic phonon contributions missed by simpler quasi-harmonic models. By adding a necessary electronic correction, the calculated heat capacity aligns closely with established, fully anharmonic theoretical benchmarks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state thermodynamics, the rigorous comparison of anharmonic methods (BQH vs. QHA) provides advanced insights into how anharmonicity modifies bulk thermodynamic properties, which is relevant for modeling complex quantum systems under extreme conditions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Accurately calculating the molar heat capacity at constant pressure, $C_p(T)$, from first principles, especially when anharmonic vibrational effects are significant.

**Main result.** The Beyond Quasi-Harmonic (BQH) method, when combined with an electronic heat-capacity correction, brings the calculated molar heat capacity into close agreement with fully anharmonic theoretical benchmarks up to approximately 1200 K.

**Method.** The BQH method uses a prepared-supercell first-principles approach to extract anharmonic vibrational energy directly from DFT energy evaluations, which is then supplemented by a first-order electronic correction.

**Model / system.** The study uses rocksalt Zirconium Carbide ($	ext{ZrC}$) as a high-temperature benchmark material, which is technologically important and electronically conductive.

**Key observables.** Molar heat capacity at constant pressure, $C_p(T)$; anharmonic vibrational energy contribution ($E_{	ext{anharmonic}}'$); electronic heat-capacity correction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High temperatures (up to 1500 K); electronic density of states at the Fermi level ($D(E_F)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The electronic correction is treated as a first-order approximation using the Sommerfeld form, assuming the DOS is smooth near $E_F$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 qualitatively illustrates the BQH/prepared-supercell energy extraction, showing the excess energy ($\Delta E'$) as the anharmonic vibrational contribution.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the challenge of calculating $C_p(T)$ due to anharmonicity, details the BQH method and its comparison against CALPHAD and QHA benchmarks, and concludes by showing the agreement achieved after adding the electronic correction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Molar heat capacity at constant pressure, C_p (T), is a central thermodynamic quantity in materials science, but it remains difficult to calculate accurately from first principles when anharmonic vibrational effects become important. In this work, rocksalt ZrC is used as a high-temperature benchmark for the Beyond Quasi-Harmonic (BQH) method, a prepared-supercell first-principles approach that extracts anharmonic vibrational energy directly from density-functional-theory energy evaluations. The calculated BQH molar heat capacity is compared with CALPHAD reference values, a fully anharmonic thermodynamic-integration calculation, a calculated C_v (T) curve from the present work, and a quasi-harmonic calculation. The BQH curve gives a substantial improvement beyond C_v (T) and beyond the quasi-harmonic approximation. Because ZrC is electronically conductive, an electronic heat-capacity correction was also estimated from the density of states at the Fermi level and added to the vibrational BQH result. This correction brings the calculated molar heat capacity into close agreement with the fully anharmonic theoretical benchmark through approximately 1200 K. These results show that the BQH method fully captures important anharmonic phonon contributions missing from ordinary quasi-harmonic calculations.

</details>
