**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes polariton condensates, showing that the interaction with the optical reservoir can induce a self-trapping mechanism. This mechanism allows the condensate to form a stable, compact state with converging currents, even when the pump power is below the standard condensation threshold. The findings are crucial for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics and potential applications in neuromorphic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium dynamics in cavity quantum electrodynamics systems, providing a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how reservoir coupling can stabilize exotic, non-trivial spatial structures in bosonic condensates.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the formation and stability of self-trapped states in polariton condensates, contrasting them with the conventionally expected ballistically expanding state.

**Main result.** Self-trapped states, driven by local reservoir depletion, can form stable, compact condensates even below the linear condensation threshold, exhibiting converging polariton currents.

**Method.** The analysis employs the extended Gross-Pitaevskii Equation (eGPE) model, utilizing stability analysis (Bogoliubov-de Gennes) and examining stationary solutions in the deeply nonlinear regime.

**Model / system.** The system models nonequilibrium exciton-polariton condensates, treated as a two-component system coupled to an incoherent optical reservoir, governed by coupled evolution equations.

**Key observables.** Condensate density, polariton currents (converging vs. expanding), condensate energy, and the existence/stability of self-trapped vs. ballistic solutions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Condensation threshold ($p_{th}$), pump spot size ($R$), self-interaction strength ($\xi$), and reservoir coupling parameters ($\epsilon, a$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as the Gaussian ansatz for stationary modes and the adiabatic elimination of the reservoir in certain limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare self-trapped (hole-burning) vs. ballistic states, show stability diagrams mapping stable/unstable regions, and illustrate the evolution of polariton currents and density profiles.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the eGPE model, deriving simplified dynamical equations, performing stability analyses (BdG), and finally mapping out the phase diagram showing the coexistence and stability of the self-trapped state below the linear threshold.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonequilibrium bosonic condensates of exciton-polaritons generated by tightly focused incoherent optical beams are typically expected to form a stationary ballistically expanding state with the notable exception of experimentally observed compact self-trapped states [Phys. Rev. Lett. 123, 047401 (2019)]. We show that the back-action of the condensate on the optically pumped excitonic reservoir via bosonic stimulated scattering can cause local reservoir depletion, producing a steady condensate confined in a self-induced potential trap. Notably, this self-trapped state is formed by persistent converging rather than radially expanding polariton currents and exists even below the conventionally defined condensation threshold. This state remains steady over nanosecond-scale times and is eventually ejected from the self-induced trap due to a bullet-type instability, enabling spiking neuromorphic dynamics in networks of polariton condensates.

</details>

**Summary.** This review surveys the capabilities of Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM) over two decades, emphasizing that the probe is an active participant in nanoscale processes. It details measurements ranging from nanoparticle coarsening to single-molecule interactions, demonstrating the ability to map intermolecular forces across the entire spectrum from weak dispersion to strong covalent bonding.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses the boundary between measurement and manipulation in nanoscale physics, which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems where the environment (the probe) cannot be ignored.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reviews decades of work on Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM), focusing on the central theme that the probe acts as an active participant rather than a passive observer when probing nanoscale interactions.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that mechanical tip-sample interactions can drive processes like nanoparticle coarsening and allow for the measurement of intermolecular potentials across a vast force range, from van der Waals to covalent bonds.

**Method.** The study combines experimental AFM measurements (e.g., force spectroscopy, time-lapse imaging) with theoretical analysis, including fitting data to established potential models like the Morse or Lennard-Jones potentials.

**Model / system.** The physical systems include adsorbed molecules ($	ext{C}_{60}$, NTCDI) on crystalline surfaces ($	ext{Si}(111)$) and metallic nanocontacts. The methodology spans characterizing pair potentials, observing self-organized assembly, and applying machine learning to automate probe control.

**Key observables.** Characteristic spatial correlation length ($L(t)$), intermolecular potential $U(z)$, force curves, and conductance changes during contact formation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Force range (van der Waals to covalent), temperature (e.g., 5 K, 77 K), and length/correlation scales (single bond limit to macroscopic assemblies).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interpretation of molecular contrast and potential variations is often complicated by the probe's influence, requiring careful deconvolution of intrinsic vs. probe-induced effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate time-lapse AFM images of Au nanoparticle coarsening, Fourier transform analysis confirming self-similar coarsening, and force curves used to map the pair potential of molecules like $	ext{C}_{60}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a comprehensive review, tracing the historical development of AFM capabilities, detailing specific experimental results on coarsening and pair potentials, and concluding with perspectives on advanced control methods like machine learning.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In their landmark paper introducing the atomic force microscope [Phys. Rev. Lett. \textbf{56}, 930 (1986)], Binnig, Quate, and Gerber presciently anticipated that the technique would ultimately be capable of probing interactions running the gamut from weak van der Waals interactions to strong covalent bonding. They also highlighted that the tip-sample forces central to AFM are present, and often highly influential, in scanning tunnelling microscopy; indeed, this realisation directly inspired the invention of the force microscope. In this perspective for the \textit{Forty Years of AFM} special issue, we review selected aspects of two decades of work from our group at the University of Nottingham that span the force range highlighted by BQG and are united by a common, central theme: the probe as active participant rather than passive observer. Our selection of results also covers length- and correlation-scales from the microscopic right down to the single chemical bond limit, tracking a spectrum of interactions from van der Waals/Hamaker forces, through hydrogen bonding, to covalent bonds and, finally, atom-by-atom assembly of metal clusters via vertical tip-sample transfer. Echoing BQG's own observations on the prevalence of probe-sample forces in STM, we also discuss recent evidence that tip-induced heterogeneity underpins first-passage dynamics in molecular diffusion and highlight the challenges in acquiring non-invasive measurements of diffusion barriers for adsorbed molecules that are readily perturbed by the probe. We close with a perspective on machine learning's growing role in automating tip-driven atomic and molecular manipulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that twisted bilayer germanene can act as a tunable platform for realizing electronic kagome lattices. By analyzing the electronic structure using DFT and STM, the authors distinguish between semiconducting and metallic moiré phases. The metallic phase hosts an emergent decorated kagome lattice, confirming the material's potential for studying strongly correlated physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to engineer correlated electronic states like flat bands using large-angle moiré patterns in a stable 2D material platform is highly relevant for simulating strongly correlated electron models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to realize and characterize electronic flat bands and correlated phases using artificial kagome lattices in atomically controlled 2D materials.

**Main result.** The paper shows that twisted bilayer germanene can host an emergent decorated kagome electronic lattice in its metallic phase, providing a platform for engineering such states at large twist angles.

**Method.** The study combines Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (STM) measurements with ab-initio Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations to analyze electronic structure.

**Model / system.** The physical system is commensurate twisted bilayer germanene on Ge2Pt, which is compared to theoretical tight-binding models of conventional and decorated kagome lattices.

**Key observables.** Electronic structure (semiconducting vs. metallic), band gap size, empty state resonance energy (1.45 eV), and symmetry (C3 symmetry).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angles (21.8° and 27.8°), band gap ($\sim 0.2$ eV), and the presence of sublattice parity (odd/even).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the electronic properties are dictated by the sublattice parity of the twisted bilayer structure, and that the kagome flat band is robustly formed by $d$-orbitals.

**Figures summary.** Figures show STM images and differential conductivity for both semiconducting and metallic phases, alongside DFT band structures illustrating the gap opening and the flat band resonance.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the electronic distinction between odd ($\sqrt{7} 	imes \sqrt{7}$) and even ($\sqrt{13} 	imes \sqrt{13}$) twisted bilayers. It then focuses on the metallic even phase, identifying the emergent decorated kagome lattice via STS and DFT, and finally models this lattice using tight-binding theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Artificial kagome lattices provide a route to electronic flat bands, geometric frustration, and correlation driven phases, but their realization in atomically controlled two-dimensional materials remains scarce. Here, we show that commensurate twisted bilayer germanene on Ge2Pt produces two electronically distinct large-angle moiré phases. Scanning tunneling microscopy measurements and density functional theory calculations reveal that commensurate twisted bilayers that are odd under an exchange of sublattices are semiconducting, whereas the twisted bilayers with an even parity are metallic. The twisted bilayers with an even parity host an empty state resonance that exhibits an emergent decorated kagome structure with a C3 symmetry. These results establish large-angle twisted germanene as a platform for engineering kagome-like electronic states in a buckled two-dimensional material.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a fabrication breakthrough for superconducting Josephson junctions using a cross-type geometry. By replacing traditional lift-off processes with Chemical-Mechanical Polishing (CMP) for planarization, the authors achieved high yield and excellent uniformity on a wafer scale. This advancement is critical for the reliable scaling of superconducting quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly relates to the physical realization of superconducting quantum circuits, providing crucial engineering data on device fabrication necessary for scaling quantum processors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for scalable, high-quality Josephson tunnel junctions (JJs) for superconducting electronics, overcoming limitations associated with traditional window-type JJs.

**Main result.** A novel fabrication scheme using Chemical-Mechanical Polishing (CMP) successfully produces high-yield, cross-type Nb/Al-AlOx/Nb JJs down to $1 \mu	ext{m} 	imes 1 \mu	ext{m}$ on a wafer scale.

**Method.** The core method involves depositing the superconducting trilayer, embedding it in $	ext{SiO}_2$, and then using CMP to planarize the excess oxide, replacing slower and less reliable lift-off processes.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a cross-type Josephson tunnel junction ($	ext{Nb/Al-AlO}_x/	ext{Nb}$), which is a fundamental building block for superconducting quantum devices like qubits and SQUIDs.

**Key observables.** IV-characteristics, Fraunhofer patterns, critical current ($I_c$), normal state resistance ($R_N$), and junction area ($A_{JJ}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Junction areas down to $1 \mu	ext{m} 	imes 1 \mu	ext{m}$; high yield ($>90\%$); specific CMP parameters (e.g., slurry type, pressures).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the smooth profile edges created by CMP are appropriate for modeling the current distribution in the junction.

**Figures summary.** Figures show schematic fabrication steps highlighting CMP use, SEM images of planarized junctions, light microscope views of cross-type JJs, and electrical measurement setups (e.g., four-wire measurement).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the fabrication process, emphasizing the CMP planarization step over lift-off. It then presents electrical characterization using IV curves and magnetic field measurements (Fraunhofer patterns) to confirm high quality and scalability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Josephson tunnel junctions (JJs) are the fundamental building blocks of today's most advanced superconducting electronic components, such as qubits and superconducting quantum interference devices (SQUIDs). Given the ongoing demand for scalability of these devices on the wafer-scale, it is crucial to control the fabrication process as precisely as possible to ensure uniform quality and reproducibility. Today, window-type JJs are often used in the production of DC SQUIDs and while they are reliable and reproducible, they suffer from alignment inaccuracies caused by photolithography and unavoidable parasitic capacitances, thus limiting the energy sensitivity of DC SQUIDs. These problems can be circumvented by producing JJs with cross-type geometry, which allows for smaller junction areas and eliminates parasitic capacitances. Here we discuss the fabrication of Nb/Al-AlOx/Nb-based cross-type JJs, where the trilayer is embedded in sputter-deposited SiO$_2$ to allow for planarisation of the structures and to ensure the reliable insulation of the sidewalls of the bottom electrode. Instead of lift-off processes that require a lot of time and potentially leave unwanted wings along the edges of microstructures behind that might compromise subsequent layers, we use chemical-mechanical polishing (CMP) for the removal of excess SiO$_2$. This is not only much faster, but produces smooth and uniform surfaces, which in turn effectively improves the reliability of our JJ production process resulting in a high yield of over $90\,\%$ on wafer-scale. In addition, we discuss the influence of fabrication details on the quality and electrical properties of our JJs with different junction areas down to $1\,\mathrm{μm} \times 1\,\mathrm{μm}$ distributed across the wafer and extract the junction specific quality parameters from their IV-characteristics and their Fraunhofer patterns.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses molecular dynamics simulations to study how the initial thermal state of supercooled water dictates its freezing rate. By analyzing the structural memory stored in the hydrogen-bond network, the authors demonstrate that structural motifs act as kinetic bottlenecks, leading to a memory-driven freezing process analogous to the Mpemba effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit use of generalized Langevin equations and memory kernels to describe non-equilibrium phase transitions in a classical system provides a rich analogy for open quantum systems where environmental coupling leads to memory effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the thermal history (memory) influences the non-equilibrium crystallization kinetics of supercooled water.

**Main result.** The freezing time exhibits a non-monotonic dependence on initial temperature, suggesting a molecular analogue of the Mpemba effect, driven by structural memory encoded in hydrogen-bonded ring structures.

**Method.** Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations combined with the non-stationary generalized Langevin equation (nsGLE) framework to analyze memory kernels.

**Model / system.** The system is supercooled liquid water freezing into ice, modeled using the TIP4P/Ice potential. The analysis focuses on the evolution of hydrogen-bonded ring structures (3- to 12-membered rings).

**Key observables.** Freezing time ($t_F$), two-time memory kernels $K(t', t)$, and the relative fractions of different ring types ($f_K$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Initial temperature ($T_i$), supercooling degree, and the specific ring motifs (especially five-membered rings).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of the direct-coexistence method and the application of the nsGLE framework to capture non-Markovian dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the direct coexistence setup, time evolution during freezing, and cross-correlations between different ring types as a function of initial temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the MD simulation setup, analyzing the structural motifs (HBNs) and their correlations, and finally applying the nsGLE framework to link these structural memories to the macroscopic, non-monotonic freezing kinetics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Freezing of supercooled water is a classic non-equilibrium problem, yet the influence of thermal history on crystallization remains unclear. Using molecular dynamics simulations with the TIP4P/Ice model, we investigate how the initial temperature $T_i$ shapes freezing following rapid quenching to 250 K. By monitoring the evolution of hydrogen-bonded ring structures, we find a non-monotonic dependence of the freezing time $t_F$ on $T_i$, with the slowest crystallization occurring near 300 K. Remarkably, this means that initially hotter water can freeze faster than cooler water, a molecular-scale analogue of the Mpemba effect. A non-stationary generalized Langevin equation framework shows that two-time memory kernels retain information about the system's thermal past, directly influencing crystallization dynamics. Structural analysis further reveals that five-membered rings act as kinetic traps, while correlations among ring types regulate the accessibility of ice-like motifs. These results uncover a molecular origin of memory-driven freezing and establish structural memory as a key driver of non-equilibrium phase transitions.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study models superfluorescence in perovskite superlattices, showing that interlayer tunneling introduces a new degree of freedom via miniband formation. This coupling causes the fundamental soliton solution to evolve into a stable breather state, providing a measurable signature of the interlayer coupling strength.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly connects solid-state physics (perovskites, superlattices) with nonlinear wave phenomena (solitons, breathers) in a quantum optical context, providing a mechanism (miniband formation) to generate complex, observable dynamics from simple structural periodicity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the origin of stable macroscopic quantum coherence at room temperature in quasi-2D perovskites, specifically focusing on how interlayer coupling modifies superfluorescence dynamics.

**Main result.** Interlayer tunneling induces miniband formation, which causes the fundamental soliton to exhibit breather dynamics—a coherent temporal redistribution of the exciton amplitude among the layers. This breather behavior is absent when tunneling is removed.

**Method.** The analysis extends the 2D nonlinear Schrödinger equation to a 3D nonlocal form for superlattices, using approximations like the adiabatic limit and Fourier transforms in momentum space.

**Model / system.** The system models Wannier exciton–LO phonon interactions in quasi-2D metal-halide perovskite superlattices. The dynamics are governed by a 3D nonlocal nonlinear Schrödinger equation derived from the exciton-phonon Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Breather dynamics (temporal oscillations of exciton amplitude), the fundamental soliton profile, and the resulting miniband dispersion.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interlayer coupling strength ($J$), number of layers ($	ilde{N}$), and the in-plane momentum ($q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the adiabatic approximation, the single-exciton wavefunction approximation, and assuming the ground-state population remains near unity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the radial profiles of the exciton amplitude $	ilde{C}(K_Z, R, t)$ for various layer numbers and show time evolution plots of the maximum amplitude and average coordinate $\langle Z(t) angle$ under varying tunneling strengths ($J$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the initial 2D description to a 3D nonlocal NLS equation for superlattices. It then analyzes the role of interlayer tunneling, showing how miniband formation introduces a phase modulation that manifests as coherent breather dynamics, contrasting this with the stationary soliton found when tunneling is zero.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently, a soliton mechanism for room-temperature superfluorescence in thin perovskite films has been proposed, with a fundamental soliton predicted to remain stable under LO phonon--exciton interactions. At the same time, superlattice architectures offer a route to enhancing superfluorescence in perovskites. Motivated by recent observations of room-temperature superfluorescence in periodic superlattices of quasi-2D metal-halide perovskites, we extend the 2D nonlocal nonlinear Schrödinger equation describing Wannier exciton--LO phonon interactions to superlattice structures, obtaining a 3D nonlocal nonlinear Schrödinger equation. We show that interlayer tunnelling gives rise to breather dynamics corresponding to a stable fundamental soliton in mixed coordinate--momentum space, with the coordinate parallel to the layers and the momentum perpendicular to them. The breather dynamics originate from miniband formation, which induces a momentum-dependent phase modulation of the soliton. In the absence of interlayer tunnelling, the breather dynamics disappear and the soliton becomes stationary. These results establish a direct connection between interlayer tunnelling, miniband formation and soliton dynamics, suggesting that breather behavior can provide a signature of interlayer tunnelling in quasi-2D perovskite superlattices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, general mechanism to create flat bands in moiré systems by exploiting strong diffractive hybridization between multiple physical fields. By modeling a single-layer optomechanical crystal, the authors show that increasing coupling monotonically suppresses the group velocity, allowing for the simultaneous, magic-configuration-free localization and coupling of both photonic and phononic modes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a general, tunable mechanism for engineering flat bands in coupled systems (optomechanics) without relying on the highly specific 'magic angle' tuning, which is a major theme in AMO and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing moiré flat-band approaches are limited to targeting only a single physical field and require specific, finely tuned 'magic configurations' for each field.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate a general, field-independent mechanism for achieving flat bands by utilizing strong diffractive hybridization among moiré-folded bands, enabling simultaneous localization and coupling of distinct fields like photons and phonons.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical modeling using an effective Hamiltonian derived from diffractive coupling terms with numerical simulations (FEM) and experimental measurements (PSD fitting).

**Model / system.** The system is a single-layer moiré optomechanical crystal formed by superimposing two periodic modulations with mismatched periods. The physics is governed by an effective Hamiltonian incorporating free and total diffraction coupling terms.

**Key observables.** Near-zero group velocity, band splitting, average group velocity vs. coupling strength, intrinsic mechanical frequency, and optomechanical coupling rate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Diffractive coupling strength (V), moiré lattice period, and the condition for flat bands ($V\omega/\Delta \gtrsim 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanism relies on diffractive hybridization on a single layer, contrasting with multilayer systems that require fine-tuning magic configurations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the moiré lattice formation, the coupling between forward/backward modes, the resulting multiple flat bands, and the monotonic suppression of the average group velocity as coupling increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the limitation of single-field magic configurations to developing an effective Hamiltonian based on diffractive coupling. It then analyzes the resulting band structure and group velocity trend, culminating in experimental demonstration of simultaneous photonic and phononic flat bands.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Moiré flat bands enhance localization and interactions through suppressed group velocity, but existing approaches largely target a single physical field because distinct excitations generally require different, finely tuned magic configurations. Here we introduce a flat-band mechanism based on strong diffractive hybridization among moiré-folded bands. Period-mismatched modulations open distinct coupling channels whose hybridization renormalizes the band dispersion. An effective Hamiltonian shows that increasing the diffractive coupling progressively suppresses the group velocity, driving the system toward a flat-band regime without field-specific magic configurations. This coupling-induced mechanism enables band flattening across distinct physical excitations. We demonstrate this mechanism in a single-layer moiré optomechanical crystal, where photonic and phononic flat bands are simultaneously realized, and their localized modes and optomechanical interaction are experimentally observed. Beyond photonic and phononic systems, this mechanism may extend to other wave and quasiparticle platforms, providing a general route to co-localizing and coupling distinct physical fields in moiré systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the first experimental observation of stable topological corner vortex solitons within a photonic fractal Higher-Order Topological Insulator. By using a Sierpiński gasket waveguide array, the researchers demonstrated that these solitons form without a power threshold, confirming the introduction of angular momentum into the physics of topological corner modes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges topological physics, nonlinear optics, and photonics, providing a concrete platform (photonic lattices) to study complex nonlinear wave phenomena like vortex solitons, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is realizing and studying stable topological corner vortex solitons in photonic fractal Higher-Order Topological Insulators (HOTIs), overcoming the limitation that previous corner solitons only exhibited trivial phase distributions.

**Main result.** The authors report the first experimental realization of stable, topological corner vortex solitons in a photonic fractal HOTI, demonstrating their thresholdless formation and exceptional robustness across a wide power range.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical analysis (solving the nonlinear Schrödinger equation) with experimental implementation using laser-written waveguide arrays to excite and measure the light confinement and phase structure.

**Model / system.** The system is a photonic fractal HOTI realized using an array of laser-written waveguides shaped like a Sierpiński gasket. The physics is governed by the nonlinear Schrödinger equation, and the topological properties are derived from the linear spectrum analysis.

**Key observables.** Output intensity distributions, phase structure (vortex singularity), and the existence of stable solitons across a broad power range.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The distortion parameter ($r$) of the fractal structure, the input power ($P$, tested from 0.01 MW to 3 MW), and the topological charge ($m=\pm 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes standard normalizations for power and relies on the ability to construct linear corner vortex modes from degenerate corner doublets.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the Sierpiński gasket structure, the linear spectrum analysis (eigenvalues vs. mode index), the bifurcation of soliton families from linear states, and experimental measurements confirming the intensity confinement and phase singularity of the vortex solitons at various powers.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework (linear spectrum analysis and soliton bifurcation) to presenting the experimental setup and results, culminating in the confirmation of thresholdless, topologically protected vortex states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantized vortices are ubiquitous in physics, spanning superconductivity, astrophysics, superfluid condensed matter systems, and nonlinear optics. Yet embedding vorticity into topologically protected nonlinear states has remained a major challenge, with all previously observed corner solitons in higher-order topological insulators (HOTIs) exhibiting only trivial phase distributions. Here, we report on the first realization of stable topological corner vortex solitons in a photonic fractal HOTI. Using an array of laser-written waveguides in the shape of Sierpiński gasket with a controllable distortion, we design linear topological vortex modes, from which nonlinear corner vortex solitons bifurcate. Moreover, we demonstrate that these solitons exhibit exceptional robustness across a broad power range and, unlike vortex solitons in topologically trivial lattices, form without a power threshold. Our results introduce the angular momentum degree of freedom into the physics of topological corner modes, opening prospects for topologically protected vortex-based photonics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, scalable platform for precisely controlling the spectral and temporal properties of broadband optical pulses. By integrating programmable photonics with nonlinear fiber optics and employing machine learning optimization, the authors can generate tailored ultrafast wavepackets on demand. This breakthrough overcomes the limitations of bulky conventional systems, enabling next-generation applications in quantum optics and advanced imaging.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly controllable platform for generating complex light fields, which is fundamental for realizing advanced quantum light sources or performing complex nonlinear spectroscopy.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving flexible and adjustable spectro-temporal shaping of broadband optical wavepackets for advanced applications like quantum information processing and ultrafast spectroscopy.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a scalable, on-demand method to tailor broadband ultrafast pulse patterns, achieving reconfigurable power control across a 400 nm bandwidth with picosecond resolution.

**Method.** The technique merges programmable integrated photonics with nonlinear fiber propagation, using machine learning (ML) algorithms to iteratively optimize the input pulse to achieve a desired output spectro-temporal profile.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes a programmable delay line (PDL) to condition the input pulse into a highly nonlinear fiber (HNLF) which generates a supercontinuum (SC). The output is characterized using advanced techniques like X-FROG.

**Key observables.** Spectrograms showing spectral and temporal content, and a Figure of Merit (FoM) used to guide optimization towards target spectro-temporal patterns.

**Important parameters / regimes.** 400 nm bandwidth, picosecond resolution, and the ability to control interpulse delays across hundreds of picoseconds.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Conventional spectro-temporal processing methods are bulky and lack scalability, necessitating the integrated photonic approach.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup involving the PDL, HNLF, and characterization stages, alongside examples of tailored spectro-temporal wavepackets (e.g., DT2, OV4) achieved through optimization.

**Paper structure.** The paper presents a framework combining programmable integrated photonics with nonlinear fiber optics. The core methodology involves using machine learning to optimize the input pulse to sculpt the broadband supercontinuum output, which is then verified via high-speed optical characterization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The ability to precisely shape the properties of light over multiple degrees of freedom constitutes the foundation of modern photonic architectures. Broadband sources with addressable spectro-temporal content are for instance critical for applications spanning biomedical imaging, material processing, lidar, and ultrafast spectroscopy, but also key enabling technologies in quantum information processing, high-capacity communications, and optical computing. Yet, flexible and adjustable spectro-temporal shaping so far remains a significant challenge, as it requires the simultaneous manipulation of both frequency and temporal domains across broad spectral bandwidths and extended timescales, from femtoseconds to nanoseconds. Importantly, conventional approaches to spectro-temporal processing typically rely on bulky and complex optical systems that lack scalability and flexibility, ultimately hampering their utility for applications requiring addressable multiphotonic processes. Here, we present a framework for spectro-temporal wavepacket control over a broad bandwidth, merging programmable integrated photonics with high-speed optical characterization. Leveraging machine leaning for tailoring nonlinear pulse propagation, we experimentally report scalable and on-demand shaping of broadband ultrafast pulse patterns. We demonstrate the efficacy of our method through reconfigurable power across 400 nm bandwidth with picosecond resolution, with promises for applications where precise control over light is paramount, in particular those needing versatile and controllable multiphoton excitations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the Edge Energy Fluctuation (EEF) theory to model friction in twisted vdW materials. It shows that the friction barrier is dictated by the specific geometry and orientation of the slider edge, leading to predictable friction anisotropy. This provides a powerful, tunable paradigm for designing superlubric devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter friction, the concept of energy barriers governed by boundary conditions and symmetry breaking in quasi-periodic systems has parallels with understanding dissipation or decoherence in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a unified theory to explain the friction scaling laws observed at the interfaces of twisted van der Waals (vdW) layered materials, moving beyond assumptions of periodic boundary conditions.

**Main result.** The friction is governed by the Edge Energy Fluctuation (EEF), which reveals a strong dependence on the edge's orientation, leading to predictable friction anisotropy and offering a method for friction engineering.

**Method.** The authors derive the sliding energy barrier by calculating an edge integral expression involving the moiré potential, analyzing how the edge geometry dictates the energy landscape.

**Model / system.** The system is twisted vdW layered materials forming moiré superlattices, where the sliding motion is modeled by considering the energy contribution from the incomplete moiré tiles at the slider edge.

**Key observables.** Friction scaling laws ($F \propto A^m$), the sliding energy barrier ($\Delta G$ or $\Delta E$), and the degree of friction anisotropy based on edge orientation ($	heta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle, slider size (A or R), and the edge orientation ($	heta$) relative to the moiré lattice vectors.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The energy barrier is dominated by the moiré edge, and the edge region can be approximated as a belt of finite width ($w$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the moiré edge and define the edge energy (EEF); one figure shows the potential energy landscape for circular sliders, and another demonstrates the friction anisotropy dependence on edge orientation.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the EEF concept, deriving the general edge energy integral, applying this to specific slider shapes (circular, straight), and finally establishing the implications for friction anisotropy and hierarchical edge engineering.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interlayer twisting in van der Waals (vdW) layered materials generates moire superlattices, unlocking unique opportunities for engineering their electronic and mechanical functionalities. Here, building on two key insights - the invariance of the potential energy of complete moire tiles during sliding, and the symmetry-breaking effect induced by the slider edge - we propose the concept of edge energy fluctuation (EEF) and demonstrate that the EEF governs the sliding energy barrier of finite-sized sliders. Interestingly, we found that even at a fixed twist angle and sliding direction, significant frictional anisotropy emerges when the slider edge is cut along different directions, resulting in distinct friction scaling laws. Furthermore, we present a friction engineering paradigm for twisted layered material interfaces through the deliberate introduction of hierarchical edges, offering a promising route for friction control in superlubric devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work models thermally activated phase slips in noncentrosymmetric superconducting wires, demonstrating that the resulting resistance exhibits magnetochiral anisotropy. This nonreciprocity stems from an asymmetry in the phase-slip barrier, which is directly linked to the superconducting diode effect. The analysis provides a parameter-free relationship between these two distinct non-equilibrium transport signatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between non-equilibrium transport phenomena (phase slips) and fundamental symmetries (magnetochirality) in superconducting systems provides insights into how microscopic spin-orbit coupling manifests in macroscopic transport measurements, relevant to open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a theory for thermally activated phase slips (TAPS) in superconducting wires lacking inversion symmetry, aiming to explain the resulting magnetochiral anisotropy.

**Main result.** The phase-slip resistance exhibits magnetochiral anisotropy, which arises from an asymmetry in the phase-slip activation barrier that is odd in both current and field. This anisotropy is exponentially amplified relative to the superconducting diode effect asymmetry.

**Method.** The analysis uses a generalized Ginzburg-Landau functional derived from the Keldysh nonlinear sigma model to analyze the phase-slip saddle point.

**Model / system.** The system is a diffusive superconducting wire patterned from a two-dimensional electron gas, incorporating Rashba spin-orbit coupling and an in-plane magnetic field. The theory analyzes the free energy functional containing standard terms and several odd-order Lifshitz invariants.

**Key observables.** Magnetochiral anisotropy (MCA) in phase-slip resistance, superconducting diode effect (SDE) efficiency ($\eta$), and the field-antisymmetric splitting of differential-resistance slopes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rashba coupling ($\alpha_R$), in-plane magnetic field ($h$), temperature ($T$), and the ratio of barrier height to temperature.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The wire is assumed to be thin compared to the coherence length and penetration depth, and the kinetic Lifshitz invariant is shown to be parametrically subleading in the exponent asymmetry.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the phase-slip saddle point trajectory in the complex plane for different magnetic field signs, showing the core riding on the helical ground state.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the theory using the GL functional, analyzes the phase-slip saddle point, derives the relationship between the MCA and the SDE via the barrier asymmetry, and confirms the independence of the result from ordering ambiguities in the appendices.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Resistive tails of narrow superconducting wires below the critical temperature are governed by phase slips, thermally activated near the transition and quantum at lower temperatures. We develop the theory of thermally activated phase slips in wires lacking inversion symmetry, modeled microscopically as a diffusive wire formed from a two-dimensional electron gas with Rashba spin-orbit coupling in an in-plane magnetic field. The phase-slip resistance acquires magnetochiral anisotropy: at fixed current it changes upon reversal of the field, and at fixed field upon reversal of the current, while the linear-response resistance remains field symmetric as required by Onsager reciprocity. The nonreciprocity originates from an asymmetry of the phase-slip activation barrier that is odd in both current and field. It is generated by the Lifshitz invariants of the free energy, the cubic gradient term and the momentum-odd quartic vertex, together with even gradient terms promoted to odd ones by the helical ground state, precisely the combination that determines the superconducting diode effect. The barrier asymmetry is therefore fixed by the diode efficiency of the same wire through a universal numerical ratio, with no adjustable parameters. The kinetic Lifshitz invariant, which makes relaxation and noise of the order parameter nonreciprocal, affects only the fluctuation prefactor at a parametrically subleading level. Because the asymmetry resides in the activation exponent, the magnetochiral signal is amplified relative to the diode asymmetry by the ratio of barrier height to temperature, and appears as a field-antisymmetric splitting of the exponential slopes of differential-resistance traces routinely used to identify thermally activated phase slips.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically studies the quantized transport of two-dimensional solitons composed of two interacting optical frequencies (FF and SH) within a periodic nonlinear crystal. It establishes that the transport mechanism, known as Thouless pumping, is robust and can be controlled by the phase mismatch and the commensurability of the underlying lattice modulation. The findings extend the concept of topological pumping to complex 2D, multifrequency nonlinear systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges nonlinear optics, topological physics, and condensed matter concepts (like Bloch bands and pumping), providing a robust theoretical framework for understanding topological phenomena in nonlinear wave systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the quantized transport (Thouless pumping) of two-dimensional multifrequency solitons.

**Main result.** Stable, quantized topological transport is demonstrated for 2D solitons in $\chi^{(2)}$ nonlinear materials, persisting even when the lattice modulation periods are incommensurate.

**Method.** The analysis uses the adiabatic approximation applied to coupled nonlinear wave equations, supplemented by linear spectral analysis and topological invariants like Chern numbers.

**Model / system.** The system involves two-dimensional quadratic solitons composed of coherently interacting fundamental frequency (FF) and second harmonic (SH) components propagating in a periodic $\chi^{(2)}$ material.

**Key observables.** Quantized center-of-mass displacement, power imbalance between FF and SH components, and the ratio of lattice periods converging to irrational numbers (e.g., the golden ratio).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phase mismatch ($eta$), soliton amplitude, and the ratio of longitudinal lattice periods ($Z_x/Z_y$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the adiabatic character of the pumping process and utilizes approximations based on the quasi-stationary solutions of the coupled nonlinear equations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the linear spectrum and the dependence of soliton profiles on phase mismatch ($eta$), and show the center-of-mass trajectories in the $(x, y)$ plane, demonstrating convergence towards irrational ratios.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the governing coupled nonlinear equations to analyzing the linear spectrum and topological invariants, then detailing the three pumping regimes based on soliton amplitude, and finally extending the analysis to incommensurate lattice modulation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider Thouless pumping of two-dimensional quadratic solitons composed from coherently interacting fundamental frequency (FF) and second harmonic (SH) components propagating in a periodic $χ^{(2)}$ material. The pumping is induced by two mutually sliding two-dimensional lattices defined by shallow transverse and longitudinal periodic refractive index modulation. Focusing on solitons in the semi-infinite gap, we find three distinct pumping scenarios: the absence of transport for small-amplitude solitons, non-quantized transport in a transient regime at intermediate amplitudes, and stable quantized transport for solitons with relatively large amplitudes. Different directions of the sliding velocity were investigated resulting in different trajectories of soliton center. The transition to the regime of quantized transport is found to depend strongly on phase mismatch between FF and SH waves that also influences stability properties of two-dimensional $χ^{(2)}$ lattice solitons. We also show that pumping with longitudinal periods in the x and y directions, whose ratio approximates an irrational number, induces quantized transport whose direction rapidly converges, with increase of the accuracy of the approximation, to a limiting pumping direction determined by this irrational number and corresponding to truly incommensurate longitudinal periods.

</details>

**Summary.** This experiment searches for ultralight axion-like dark matter by measuring the oscillating torque it induces on nuclear spins in a solid-state system. Using broadband SQUID magnetometry on polarized $^{207}	ext{Pb}$, the researchers successfully calibrated their setup and set stringent upper bounds on the axion-nuclear electric dipole coupling constant. The results demonstrate a highly sensitive technique for probing fundamental physics in the nanoelectron-volt mass range.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges condensed matter techniques (solid-state NMR, SQUID magnetometry) with fundamental particle physics searches (axion dark matter), providing a highly sensitive, low-frequency measurement platform relevant to quantum sensing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reports an experimental search for ultralight axion-like dark matter (ALPs) by looking for its coupling to the electric dipole moment of atomic nuclei.

**Main result.** The experiment established upper bounds on the coupling constant $|g_d| < 4	imes 10^{-4} 	ext{ GeV}^{-2}$ in specific frequency bands, demonstrating sensitivity to ALPs in the nanoelectron-volt mass range.

**Method.** The measurement uses solid-state nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) on polarized $^{207}	ext{Pb}$ spins, detecting the oscillating torque induced by the ambient axion field using a superconducting quantum interference device (SQUID).

**Model / system.** The system involves $^{207}	ext{Pb}$ spins polarized in a ferroelectric crystal, interacting with the axion field via an electric dipole moment coupling ($H_{EDM}$). The detection relies on measuring the induced oscillating magnetization ($M_{xy}$) coupled to the SQUID.

**Key observables.** The induced perpendicular, rotating magnetization ($M_{xy}$) detected as a time-varying magnetic flux coupled to the SQUID, and the system response function $\alpha = V/	heta$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Axion mass ranges searched: 19.5–20.5 and 21.5–22 neV. The upper bound on the coupling constant is $|g_d| < 4	imes 10^{-4} 	ext{ GeV}^{-2}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the standard halo model for the dark matter field and operates in the small-tip-angle regime for the NMR response.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the experimental setup involving the polarized sample, magnet, and SQUID. Figure 2 illustrates the pulsed NMR sequence used for calibration measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical basis of the EDM coupling, describes the experimental setup using SQUID magnetometry on polarized $^{207}	ext{Pb}$, outlines the calibration procedure using pulsed NMR, and finally presents the derived upper bounds on the axion-nucleon coupling constant.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the results of an experimental search for ultralight axion-like dark matter in the mass ranges 19.5--20.5 and 21.5--22 neV. The Cosmic Axion Spin Precession Experiment-electric probes the axion-nuclear electric dipole moment coupling. We perform solid-state nuclear magnetic resonance on $^{207}$Pb spin ensembles which reside in a polarized ferroelectric crystal. The background axion-like dark matter field induces an oscillating torque on the $^{207}$Pb spins via the electric dipole moment coupling $g_{d}$. The experiment is calibrated with pulsed magnetic resonance measurements covering two frequency bands (4.6--5.0 and 5.2--5.3 MHz) that correspond to the axion Compton frequencies associated with the identified axion mass ranges. By sweeping the leading magnetic field applied to the sample, we are able to detect the $^{207}$Pb nuclear magnetic resonance in these frequency ranges using a superconducting quantum interference device, inductively coupled to the sample with a broadband circuit. We establish the upper bounds $|g_{d}| < 4\times 10^{-4}$ GeV$^{-2}$ with 95% confidence in these frequency bands. Our results demonstrate the detection and small-tip angle calibration of low-field $^{207}$Pb nuclear magnetic resonance, allowing for sensitivity to axion-like dark matter in the nanoelectron-volt axion mass range.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the topological electronic structure of twisted bilayer-trilayer graphene, demonstrating that the system's band topology is highly tunable. By applying perpendicular electric fields and considering different stacking arrangements, the authors map out phase diagrams that control the Chern numbers of the narrow bands. This provides a theoretical roadmap for engineering topological materials using moiré heterostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to tune topological invariants (Chern numbers) using external fields like the electric field is a key concept in realizing artificial quantum Hall states, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems and correlated electron physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the low-energy band structure and topological properties of twisted bilayer-trilayer graphene, focusing on how these properties can be controlled.

**Main result.** The perpendicular electric field is identified as an efficient tuning knob for controlling the stability and topological transitions of the narrow Chern bands. The interplay between stacking, twist angle, and electric field allows for engineering tunable topological bands.

**Method.** The authors employed both tight-binding and continuum models to calculate the band structure and topological invariants (Chern numbers) across various physical parameters.

**Model / system.** The physical system is twisted bilayer-trilayer graphene, analyzed across four specific stacking configurations (e.g., AB--ABC). The models incorporate the effects of twist angle, perpendicular electric field, and self-consistent Hartree potential.

**Key observables.** Flat bands, valley Chern numbers, total Chern number, topological transitions (gap closing/reopening), and band structure evolution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), perpendicular electric field ($E$), and the self-consistent Hartree potential ($V_H$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the self-consistent Hartree approximation and assumes a C3z-symmetric relative registry.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides schematic atomic structures and moiré geometry for the key stacking configurations, such as AB–ABC and AB–ABA.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the agreement between tight-binding and continuum models, then systematically studies the evolution of flat bands and their Chern numbers under varying twist angles and electric fields, detailing two types of topological transitions. Finally, it maps out the topological phase diagrams.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the low-energy band structure and topology of twisted bilayer--trilayer graphene with four stacking configurations: AB--ABC, BA--ABC, AB--ABA, and BA--ABA. Using both tight-binding and continuum models, we first establish that the two approaches show good agreement in the band structure in low-energy regime. We then study the evolution of the flat bands and their valley Chern numbers as functions of twist angle, perpendicular electric field, and the self-consistent Hartree potential. At relatively large twist angles and under electric field, we find a topological transition between the narrow bands, with the total Chern number of the flat bands following the Chern number sum rules derived from the chiral-limit description. We also observe another type of topological transition when the flat bands hybridize with adjacent remote bands, where gap closing and reopening processes lead to Chern number and charge density transfer. By constructing topological phase diagrams in the space of twist angle and electric field, we show that the perpendicular electric field provides an efficient tuning knob for controlling the stability and transitions of the Chern bands. Finally, we find that the Hartree potential mainly induce weak band shifts and reshaping in the narrow bands. However, with a combination of Hartree potential and the electric fields, the narrow bands show rich topological phase diagram. Our results clarify the interplay between the stacking, twist angle and electric field in manipulating the narrow bands and their topology in twisted bilayer--trilayer graphene, and provide guidance for engineering topological narrow bands with tunable Chern numbers in realistic twisted multilayer graphene systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This study uses neutron diffraction and magnetometry to examine Prussian blue analogues, demonstrating that the presence and loss of interstitial water significantly control the material's structural and magnetic properties. The research pinpoints the role of correlated vacancies as a key structural determinant that dictates the magnetic ground state and transition pathways in these compounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work connects structural disorder (vacancies/water) directly to magnetic ground state selection and phase transitions, providing an example of how lattice dynamics can tune magnetic exchange interactions, which is relevant to correlated electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how structural changes, particularly those induced by water loss (dehydration) and external magnetic fields, govern the magnetic transitions in Prussian blue analogues (PBAs).

**Main result.** Water content is established as a critical parameter controlling both the structural stability (e.g., thermal expansion) and the magnetic ground state of these materials.

**Method.** The research combines neutron diffraction studies to probe structure and vacancy signatures with magnetic measurements and theoretical modeling using spin Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a family of ferro- and ferrimagnetic Prussian blue analogues (PBAs) with the general formula A_4[Fe(CN)6]2.7. The analysis models magnetic interactions using a minimal Heisenberg spin Hamiltonian to explain field-induced spin reorientations.

**Key observables.** Magnetic ordering temperature (Tc), magnetic structure (propagation vector k), coefficient of thermal expansion (CTE), and diffuse scattering features indicating vacancies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range (2–450 K), applied magnetic field (up to 5 T), and the concentration/presence of interstitial water/vacancies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The magnetic transition in MnFe is modeled as a near-compensation point between Mn–Fe and Mn–Mn interactions. The analysis assumes specific magnetic configurations (collinear vs. frustrated).

**Figures summary.** Figures show neutron diffractograms highlighting diffuse scattering from vacancies, temperature dependence of lattice parameters showing CTE crossovers, and M-H curves demonstrating field-induced spin reorientation.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes structural changes (diffraction, CTE) linked to water content, followed by detailed magnetic characterization (magnetization, magnetic structure determination via Rietveld refinement) to establish the role of water in controlling magnetic ground states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report a neutron diffraction study of the structural and magnetic properties of a family of ferro- and ferrimagnetic Prussian blue analogues (PBAs), $A_4$[Fe(CN)$_6$]$_{2.7}$ ($A$ = Co, Mn, Ni), Rb$_2$Ni$_4$[Fe(CN)$_6$]$_{3.3}$, and Mn$_4$[Cr(CN)$_6$]$_{2.7}$, as a function of temperature (2--450~K) and applied magnetic field. All bimetallic compounds of the Fm$\overline{3}$m family exhibit a broad diffuse feature at low scattering angle, which we identify, through comparison with the cation-stabilized Rb$_2$NiFe framework, as an intrinsic signature of correlated vacancies and their associated interstitial water. High-temperature diffraction reveals a continuous crossover from positive to negative thermal expansion in CoFe and MnFe upon dehydration, while NiFe remains structurally robust up to 450~K. At low temperature, all compounds order in a collinear ferrimagnetic state with propagation vector $\mathbf{k}=(0,0,0)$, except MnFe, which adopts a partially frustrated magnetic structure with $\mathbf{k}=(1,0,0)$. A moderate magnetic field of $B_c = 1.3$~T drives a spin reorientation in MnFe toward the collinear $\mathbf{k}=(0,0,0)$ ferrimagnetic state common to the other compounds; the same transition is independently induced by dehydration. A minimal Heisenberg model shows that this transition results from a near-compensation between antiferromagnetic Mn--Fe coupling and a geometrically frustrated antiferromagnetic Mn--Mn interaction on the face-centered-cubic Mn sublattice, placing MnFe in the vicinity of a magnetic compensation point. These results resolve a longstanding ambiguity in the interpretation of the Fe $K$-edge XMCD response of MnFe-based PBAs, and establish water content as a key parameter controlling both the structural and magnetic stability of this family of materials, with direct relevance to their use as battery electrodes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a rigorous thermomechanical framework for understanding how dense granular materials behave under stress. It shows that standard assumptions from continuum mechanics break down due to pervasive energy dissipation during contact sliding. The work emphasizes the need for advanced constitutive models that accurately capture the complex, non-smooth nature of granular material response.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical mechanics, the rigorous treatment of non-equilibrium energy dissipation, path dependence, and the failure of simple constitutive assumptions mirrors challenges in modeling non-equilibrium quantum systems or complex many-body dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a comprehensive thermomechanical framework to describe the isothermal, rate-independent constitutive behavior of dense granular materials.

**Main result.** The paper demonstrates that basic elastoplastic assumptions fail for granular materials, necessitating advanced constitutive models that account for pervasive dissipation and non-smooth energy landscapes.

**Method.** The authors utilize a combination of continuum mechanics theory, energy rate balance equations, and direct measurement techniques derived from Discrete Element Method (DEM) simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is dense granular material, modeled using a thermomechanical framework that incorporates internal variables for fabric (reversible stiffness) and structure (dissipation). The analysis is based on energy rate balance equations derived from the Helmholtz free energy density.

**Key observables.** Free energy ($\psi$), dissipation rate, stress ($oldsymbol{\sigma}$), and strain ($oldsymbol{\varepsilon}$), which are directly measurable in DEM simulations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Isothermal conditions, rate-independence, and the nature of internal variables (fabric and structure).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes rate-independence and isothermal conditions, but acknowledges that the free energy may not be smoothly differentiable, requiring the use of Gâteaux derivatives.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the energy rate balance, proposing internal variable sets for fabric and structure, testing these variables against DEM results, and finally comparing the findings against standard elastoplastic assumptions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The paper presents a broad thermomechanic framework for the isothermal rate-independent constitutive behavior of dense granular materials. The essential quantities in this framework are directly measurable in discrete element (DEM) simulations: free energy, dissipation, stress, and strain. The paper proposes that energy and dissipation are governed by two sets of internal variables: fabric variables that control the reversible stiffness and structure variables associated with internal sliding. The relevant fabric variables are identified and measured with simulations. Two hypotheses are considered for the structure variables: the macro-scale irreversible strain and an aggregate measure of the micro-scale frictional forces among sliding contacts. Both hypotheses are tested with simulations, which allow direct calculation of the internal variables. The paper then demonstrates the manner in which the measured variables are applied in incremental constitutive models. Among other findings are the following. (1) Dissipation from contact sliding is pervasive and occurs in all directions of incremental loading. (2) Contact motions are not reversed by a reversal of the strain direction, and contacts continue to slide when loading is reversed. (3) The free energy can not be assumed smoothly differentiable; instead, Gâteaux derivatives must be used with irreversible effects. (4) Basic assumptions of elastoplasticity are contravened: no region of purely reversible strain exists, no uniform yield direction exists, no uniform flow direction exists, and irreversible strain is not proportional to the projected total strain. A three-mechanism elastoplasticity model, however, closely fit the DEM results, and methods are demonstrated for quantifying the model. The results emphasize that advanced constitutive models are needed for capturing the general incremental behavior of granular materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This review presents a comprehensive, reduction-based methodology for analyzing nonlinear wave equations by mapping them onto lower-dimensional dynamical systems. It argues that understanding the invariant structures and stability of the reduced phase space is more crucial than finding isolated analytical solutions. This framework provides a robust tool for predicting wave behavior, assessing stability, and designing control mechanisms in various physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on general nonlinear PDEs, the rigorous connection between phase space geometry and physical wave structures is a powerful tool for analyzing complex dynamics in quantum optics (e.g., NLSE) and plasma physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the complex analysis of nonlinear wave equations, which exhibit rich wave structures due to the interplay of dispersion, nonlinearity, and forcing terms.

**Main result.** It establishes a reduction-based framework showing that the underlying phase-space structures (like invariant orbits) of the reduced dynamical system geometrically represent the physically admissible waveforms of the original PDE.

**Method.** The core method involves reducing high-dimensional nonlinear PDEs to finite-dimensional autonomous dynamical systems via transformations (e.g., travelling wave ansatz), followed by rigorous analysis of the resulting phase space.

**Model / system.** The framework is applicable to a wide range of nonlinear wave equations, including Schrödinger-type, coupled, dissipative, shallow-water, and fractional wave equations.

**Key observables.** Equilibria (constant states), periodic trajectories (periodic waves), homoclinic orbits (solitary waves), and heteroclinic connections (fronts/kinks).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends on the interplay of nonlinearity, dispersion, coupling strengths, and external forcing parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework prioritizes physical admissibility, stability, and robustness over merely generating formal analytical solutions, acknowledging limitations in parameter space characterization and correspondence with full PDE dynamics.

**Figures summary.** The figures illustrate the end-to-end workflow: from PDE formulation to reduction, phase space analysis (showing how orbits map to waveforms), and the hierarchy of diagnostics used to confirm chaotic or stable behavior.

**Paper structure.** The review is highly methodological, detailing the workflow: PDE formulation -> Reduction to ODEs -> Dynamical Systems Analysis (Phase Space) -> Stability/Bifurcation Analysis -> Full PDE Validation -> Comparison with physical/experimental observables.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonlinear wave equations exhibit rich interplay among dispersion, nonlinearity, coupling, dissipation, and external forcing, producing diverse coherent and complex wave structures. Although exact travelling-wave solutions provide analytical benchmarks, their construction alone does not reveal underlying phase-space, bifurcations, stability, or responses to perturbations. This review presents a reduction-based methodological framework connecting nonlinear partial differential equations(PDEs) to reduced dynamical systems, invariant phase-space structures, exact waveform reconstruction, stability analysis, and verification in PDEs. Its applicability and limitations are examined across Schr{ö}dinger-type, coupled, nonparaxial, dissipative, magnetic, shallow-water, and fractional wave equations. Particular emphasis is placed on correspondence between analytical waveforms and invariant orbits. Equilibria, periodic trajectories, homoclinic orbits, and heteroclinic connections geometrically represent constant, periodic, localized, and front-like structures, respectively. This review distinguishes existence and stability of invariant structures from onset of chaos, emphasizing complementary diagnostics rather than reliance on phase-portraits or finite-time indicators alone. Major methodological gaps include incomplete parameter-space characterization, weak correspondence between reduced models and full-PDE dynamics, ambiguities in generalized and fractional formulations, limited robustness analysis, inadequate numerical reproducibility, and insufficient links to experimental observables. The framework prioritizes physical admissibility, stability, robustness, and predictive relevance over generation of additional formal solutions. It supports reliable nonlinear wave prediction, stability assessment, control, and system design in fluid, optical, plasma, and other nonlinear physical systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes a mathematical model for how two types of cells interact and move together, forming segregated waves. The authors derive explicit bounds on the wave speed using variational calculus and analyze the stability in both 1D and 2D. The findings clarify the conditions under which these cell fronts remain stable or break down into finger-like protrusions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the subject is biophysics/continuum mechanics, the mathematical techniques—variational bounds, free-boundary problems, and stability analysis of traveling waves—are highly relevant to the mathematical physics of pattern formation in complex systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the speed, parameter dependence, and stability of segregated traveling wave solutions in a minimal pressure-based model describing heterogeneous cell populations.

**Main result.** It derives an almost explicit variational bound for the wave speed and shows that 1D stability requires non-proliferative cells to be more mobile than proliferative cells, while 2D stability is more complex.

**Method.** The study employs variational methods by reformulating the problem as a free-boundary problem for a generalized porous-Fisher equation, alongside asymptotic shape-perturbation analysis for circular waves.

**Model / system.** The model is a system of reaction-cross-diffusion equations describing two cell types (proliferative and non-proliferative) whose movement is governed by a pressure law derived from their total density.

**Key observables.** Wave speed, stability of segregated waves, pressure, interface velocity, and growth rates of angular modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mobility coefficients ($\mu, 
u$), growth rate function $G(p)$, and the relative values of $\mu$ and $
u$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes pressure-based movement and analyzes the incompressible limit ($\gamma 	o \infty$) where the problem reduces to a sharp-interface free boundary problem.

**Figures summary.** Figure 6 illustrates the destabilization of a circular wave, showing the growth of interfacial perturbations, particularly when the proliferative cells are more mobile than the non-proliferative cells.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the governing equations and then derives the variational bound for the wave speed. It proceeds to analyze the 1D stability, followed by a detailed asymptotic shape-perturbation analysis for 2D circular waves to characterize fingering instabilities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider a minimal pressure-based model of heterogeneous cell populations consisting of proliferative and non-proliferative cells with different mobilities. The model is formulated as a system of reaction--cross--diffusion equations describing the spatio-temporal dynamics of the cell densities. The model is known to admit one-dimensional travelling wave solutions with strictly segregated components: non-proliferative cells occupy a finite region at the leading edge, while proliferative cells remain at the rear. However, the speed, parameter dependence, and stability of these waves remain poorly understood. In this work, we derive an almost explicit variational bound on the wave speed by reformulating the problem as a free-boundary problem for a generalised porous--Fisher equation. The estimates we obtain apply to general pressure laws and growth kinetics, agree closely with the results of numerical simulations, and become sharp in the incompressible limit, where we formally recover a fully explicit characterisation of the wave speed. We then analyse the stability of the waves to show that segregated waves are stable only when non-proliferative cells are more mobile than proliferative cells. Finally, motivated by numerical observations of finger-like protrusions, we investigate the stability of incompressible segregated circular waves through asymptotic shape-perturbation analysis. This yields explicit expressions for the pressure, interface velocity, and growth rates of angular modes, thereby making evident the destabilisation mechanisms that may lead to the emergence of fingering instability. Interestingly, we find that, in contrast with the one-dimensional case, the stability of such circular waves is not determined solely by the relative value of the mobility coefficients, and thus instabilities may arise irrespective of which cell type has the larger mobility.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that the local density of states (LDOS) around impurities in gapped two-band materials can serve as a spectroscopic probe for non-local topological properties like the Berry curvature and wavefunction winding number. By analyzing the impurity-induced bound states, the authors establish a general relationship allowing the direct measurement of the Berry curvature from spectroscopic measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct link established between a measurable spectroscopic quantity (LDOS) and a fundamental topological invariant (Berry curvature) provides a powerful, experimentally testable theoretical tool for condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates using quasiparticle interference (QPI) near impurities to extract non-local topological properties, specifically the Berry curvature and wavefunction winding number, in gapped two-band electronic models.

**Main result.** It establishes a direct, local connection showing that the Berry curvature can be unambiguously extracted from the spectroscopic Local Density of States (LDOS) induced by impurities, particularly in gapped systems.

**Method.** The analysis employs the T-matrix formalism applied to the Green's function of the system, linearizing the Bloch Hamiltonian at high-symmetry points (valleys) to derive a continuum model.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on minimal two-band models derived from lattice structures like the honeycomb lattice (e.g., hBN, TMDs), encompassing various dispersion types (Dirac, semi-Dirac, quadratic) parameterized by gap, winding number, and dispersion exponent.

**Key observables.** Local Density of States (LDOS) induced by impurities, wavefunction winding number (W), and Berry curvature ($\Omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gap ($\Delta_Q$), winding number ($W_Q$), and the strength of the impurity potential ($V$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on low-energy continuum expansions around high-symmetry points and assumes the impurity potential is strong enough to reveal the topological winding number.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between analytical continuum predictions and tight-binding results, and show the dependence of the winding region on filtering parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by deriving the low-energy continuum Hamiltonian from lattice structure factors, calculating the real-space Green's function, and finally using the T-matrix formalism to determine the impurity-induced LDOS and extract the topological invariants.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We explore the possibility of using quasiparticle interference near impurities to extract non-local properties of bands. Focusing on the minimal two-band description of a system with multiple valleys, we show that the local density of states (LDOS) induced by non-magnetic impurities can effectively probe both the Berry curvature and the wavefunction winding, locally in each valley. We analyze gapped models based on Dirac, semi-Dirac, quadratic touching, and higher-order touching energy dispersions, in presence of a point-like potential scatterer. We find that for strong enough impurity potentials the LDOS along a contour around the impurity unambiguously shows the wavefunction winding number, in contrast to the case of gapless Dirac-like dispersions where the winding can be precluded by LDOS oscillations in the radial direction. We also establish a general connection between, on the one hand, the Berry curvature as a function of momentum locally in a valley, and on the other hand, the wavefunction winding number and the energy dispersion parameters, making hence the local Berry curvature directly observable from spectroscopic LDOS information.

</details>
